### [1] [Regularized Emphatic Temporal-Difference Learning: Stability under Constant Stepsizes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19170)
*Xingguo Chen,Zhaohui Wu,Jinguo Ye,Chao Li,Shangdong Yang,Guang Yang,Skylar Liang,Wenhao Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RETD (Regularized Emphatic TD) introduces normalization and delayed corrections to stabilize ETD dynamics while preserving its fixed point, with proven convergence guarantees and verified stability improvements on problematic cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While Emphatic TD (ETD) theoretically stabilizes off-policy TD updates, its sampled dynamics with constant stepsizes can be unstable even when the mean map contracts, as shown by a constructed two-state counterexample where ETD has positive Lyapunov exponents despite mean contraction.

Method: Proposes Regularized Emphatic TD (RETD) which: 1) normalizes first-order post-shock repair, 2) leaves traces and importance ratios unchanged, 3) stores emphatic TD signal in a leaky scalar state, 4) releases delayed corrections, and 5) uses harmonic diminishing stepsizes with proven convergence properties.

Result: RETD achieves: 1) raw equilibrium as affine shift of ETD equilibrium, 2) exact recovery of ETD fixed point via single/two-regularization readouts, 3) certified negative Lyapunov exponents on problematic cases (two-state construction and Baird point), 4) convergence proofs for diminishing stepsizes, and 5) validated improvements through 10,000-run experiments showing separations, fixed-point recovery, and stability regions.

Conclusion: RETD successfully addresses ETD's instability in sampled dynamics by modifying post-shock behavior while preserving the original fixed point. However, it does not reduce the inherent variance problem of follow-on traces, suggesting this remains a fundamental challenge in emphatic TD methods.

Abstract: Emphatic temporal-difference learning (ETD) stabilizes the expected off-policy TD update and changes its projection geometry, but neither property determines constant-stepsize sampled dynamics. We construct an ergodic two-state counterexample in which the ETD mean map contracts while the sampled product has a positive top Lyapunov exponent. Regenerative-cycle analysis separates this sign from the infinite variance of the follow-on trace. We introduce regularized emphatic TD (RETD), a normalized first-order post-shock repair that leaves the trace and importance ratios unchanged, stores the emphatic TD signal in a leaky scalar state, and releases a delayed correction. RETD's raw equilibrium is an affine shift of the ETD equilibrium; single- and two-regularization readouts recover the ETD fixed point exactly. We prove almost-sure convergence for harmonic diminishing stepsizes and a conditional constant-stepsize moment-contraction result from a Markovian random-product bound. RETD has certified negative exponents on the two-state construction and one Baird point, whereas the positive Baird ETD sign remains numerical. Paired 10,000-run experiments validate both separations, fixed-point recovery, a nonmonotone stability region, and task dependence. RETD changes post-shock dynamics; it does not reduce the shared follow-on-trace variance.

</details>


### [2] [BioPhys-Bridge: A Benchmark for Interdisciplinary Scientific Reasoning in Physics-Grounded Biological Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19180)
*Qingyang Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BioPhys-Bridge is a new benchmark dataset for evaluating evidence-grounded reasoning in biophysics literature, requiring models to ground answers in quantitative physics models and biological mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language models struggle with analyzing interdisciplinary scientific research, particularly in biophysics where faithful answers require grounding observations in source evidence, interpreting through quantitative physics models, and linking to biological mechanisms.

Method: Created BioPhys-Bridge benchmark with 500 cases containing evidence blocks, stable evidence IDs, quantitative values, units, equations, assumptions, mechanisms, and next decisions as grounding targets for QA and RAG tasks. Enforced strict quality controls including domain expert review.

Result: Initial evaluation shows DeepSeek-V4-Flash achieves highest evidence-ID F1 score (0.360), followed by Qwen3.7-Max (0.316) and GPT-4o-mini (0.294). Dataset covers 6 biological domains and 9 physical model families.

Conclusion: BioPhys-Bridge serves as an interdisciplinary benchmark for evaluating attribution, faithfulness, hallucination reduction, and biological experiment design. Future work will expand dataset size/complexity and perform comprehensive evaluations.

Abstract: Language models face unique challenges in analyzing interdisciplinary scientific research literature. In biophysics research, faithful answers require grounding observed data in source evidence, interpreting it through a quantitative physics model, and linking it to a biological mechanism. To address this challenge, we introduce BioPhys-Bridge, a novel benchmark dataset for evidence-grounded scientific reasoning over biophysical literature. Each case contains evidence blocks, stable evidence IDs, quantitative values, units, equations, assumptions, mechanisms, and next decisions as grounding targets for question answering (QA) and retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). The initial release contains 500 cases, 1,517 agent-facing tasks, and covers six biological domains and nine physical model families, including three sparse families reserved for future expansion. We enforce strict quality gates for all cases in schema, evidence-integrity, quantitative-grounding, source-license, duplicate, unit-normalization, with domain expert review and annotation for 81 cases. Preliminary evaluations show that DeepSeek-V4-Flash obtain the highest evidence-ID $F_1$ score (0.360), followed by Qwen3.7-Max (0.316) and GPT-4o-mini (0.294). BioPhys-Bridge is an interdisciplinary benchmark for evaluating attribution, faithfulness, hallucination reduction, and biological experiment design with complex, multi-step scientific reasoning. Future works will increase the size and complexity of the dataset and perform comprehensive evaluations. Code and data are available in the GitHub repository and on Hugging Face.

</details>


### [3] [What Do We Expect from LLMs? Mapping the Design of LLM Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19182)
*Chao Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Researchers systematically analyzed 14,767 papers on LLM evaluation benchmarks from 2022-2026 to understand how evaluation requirements and expectations for LLMs are evolving, revealing trends toward action/interaction tasks, professional applications, and LLM-based scoring while questioning if expanded evaluation truly provides independent evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While benchmarks are central to assessing LLM progress, model rankings alone don't reveal how evaluation requirements themselves are changing. The researchers wanted to understand what researchers expect from LLMs and what counts as successful performance by examining the expanding variety of benchmarks.

Method: Systematic mapping of 14,767 papers introducing or updating evaluation resources from arXiv submissions (January 2022 - August 2026) using staged screening and automated full-text coding. They examined changes in target systems/domains, evaluation materials/conditions, and scoring mechanisms.

Result: Found growing emphasis on action, interaction, and professional applications. Established and newer design elements frequently coexist. LLM-based scoring grows within both agent and non-agent groups, but model-generated materials show no comparable sustained increase. Trends show how capability expectations translate into concrete tests.

Conclusion: The findings illuminate how public research translates capability expectations into tests and success criteria. The paper raises a critical question: as AI participates in constructing tests, performing tasks, and judging responses, does expanding evaluation provide more independent evidence, or risk reproducing the preferences and blind spots of the participating models?

Abstract: Benchmarks are central to how progress in large language models (LLMs) is assessed and communicated. Yet model rankings alone reveal little about how evaluation requirements themselves are changing. The expanding variety of benchmarks offers another perspective: what researchers expect LLMs to do, and what they count as successful performance. We systematically map 14,767 papers introducing or updating evaluation resources from arXiv submissions between January 2022 and August 2026. Using staged screening and automated full-text coding, we examine changes in target systems and domains, evaluation materials and conditions, and scoring mechanisms. The collection shows growing emphasis on action, interaction, and professional applications, while established and newer design elements frequently coexist. Model participation also develops unevenly: LLM-based scoring grows within both agent and non-agent groups, whereas model-generated materials show no comparable sustained increase in recent cohorts. These findings illuminate how public research translates capability expectations into concrete tests and criteria for success. As AI participates in constructing tests, performing tasks, and judging responses, they also raise a question: does expanding evaluation provide more independent evidence, or risk reproducing the preferences and blind spots of its participating models?

</details>


### [4] [Position: It is Time to Virtualize Foundation Models with a Self-evolving Operating System Layer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19203)
*Suparna Bhattacharya,Tarun Kumar,Cong Xu,Satish Kumar Mopur,Jiahao Li,Ashish Mishra,Aalap Tripathy,Annmary Justine Koomthanam,Martin Foltin,Ian Foster*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a Foundation Model Operating System (FMOS) to unify fragmented AI agent systems by virtualizing FM interactions, similar to how operating systems abstract hardware, enabling portable applications with consistent runtime services.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI agent systems are fragmented with each framework implementing its own runtime for state, memory, budgets, and guardrails, making behavior non-portable and governance brittle. This parallels computing before operating systems where every program re-implemented basic services.

Method: Proposes a Foundation Model Operating System (FMOS) that virtualizes FM interactions analogously to how virtual machines abstract physical hardware. It gives applications the illusion of dedicated, trustworthy FM instances with effectively unbounded capabilities.

Result: The FMOS orchestrates knowledge across memory tiers, manages model selection and resource allocation, and handles verification and policy enforcement. Like the human brain, it learns when to intervene vs. let inference proceed directly, continuously adapting policies based on operational experience.

Conclusion: The field needs an operating system layer for foundation models to address the current fragmentation, making AI agent systems more portable, governable, and capable through unified runtime services.

Abstract: AI applications have shifted from single, monolithic foundation models (FM) to compound agentic systems. Yet today's stacks remain fragmented: even as protocols (e.g., MCP, A2A) ease tool/agent connectivity, each framework embeds an implicit runtime for state, memory, budgets, and guardrails, making behavior non-portable and governance brittle. It mirrors computing before operating systems, when every program re-implemented basic services. This position paper argues that the field now needs a Foundation Model Operating System (FMOS) -- a system layer that virtualizes FM interactions analogous to how virtual machines abstract physical hardware, giving applications the illusion of dedicated, trustworthy FM instances with effectively unbounded capabilities. Internally, the FMOS orchestrates knowledge across memory tiers, model selection and resource allocation, and verification and policy enforcement. Like the human brain switching between fast intuition and slow deliberation, the FMOS learns when to intervene and when to let inference proceed directly and continuously adapting its policies based on operational experience.

</details>


### [5] [What Do Current Systematic Generalization Tasks Miss? A Reasoning-Centered Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19212)
*Chengwen Qi,Deheng Ye,Yatao Bian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TranSGrid is a new testbed that combines deductive, inductive, and abductive reasoning to better measure systematic generalization beyond existing simplifications, revealing Transformers perform much worse on comprehensive reasoning tasks than standard test sets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing studies on systematic generalization rely on simplifications like linear action composition and action-explicit goals, which omit essential aspects of reasoning capabilities. There's a need for a testbed that comprehensively assesses systematic generalization by integrating all three forms of reasoning.

Method: Introduced TranSGrid testbed combining deductive, inductive, and abductive reasoning in unified tasks. Tested 7 Transformers on 4,800 instances with controlled variants: one making actions compose almost linearly (reducing inductive demand) and another making goals action-explicit (reducing abductive demand).

Result: All models performed much worse on TranSGrid than on held-out test sets: largest model solved 79.6% of test set vs 55.3% of TranSGrid (15.8% on hardest subset). The performance gap persists within training length range. When simplifications were reintroduced (linear composition or action-explicit goals), solve rates returned to test set levels.

Conclusion: Existing systematic generalization tasks reduce either inductive or abductive reasoning demands, and comprehensive measurement requires tasks involving all three reasoning forms. Productivity alone is insufficient to evaluate systematic generalization capabilities.

Abstract: Systematic generalization, the ability to solve novel problems by recombining known atomic elements, is central to human intelligence but difficult to study rigorously under controlled settings. Existing studies therefore rely on simplifications such as approximately linear action composition, productivity-based tests, and action-explicit goals, which make systematic generalization easier to study but omit some essential aspects of this capability. To characterize what these simplifications miss, we adopt a reasoning-centered lens and introduce TranSGrid, a testbed that brings deductive, inductive, and abductive reasoning together within a unified task. Experiments with seven Transformers on 4,800 TranSGrid instances show that all models perform much worse on TranSGrid than on a held-out test set: the largest model solves 79.6% of the test set, but only 55.3% of TranSGrid and 15.8% of the hardest subset. The gap remains within the training length range, showing that productivity alone is not sufficient to evaluate systematic generalization. Additionally, we reintroduce the other two simplifications into TranSGrid: one variant makes actions compose almost linearly (reducing the inductive demand), the other makes goals action-explicit (reducing the abductive one). In both, solve rates return to roughly the test set level, showing that either simplification alone is enough to reduce TranSGrid to an ordinary held-out test set. Together, our results show that existing tasks reduce either or both of the inductive and abductive demands, and that comprehensively measuring systematic generalization requires a task that involves all three forms of reasoning.

</details>


### [6] [Characterizing Web Search by Conversational LLM Agents: From Search Decisions and Strategies to Results and Responses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19244)
*Mahsa Amani,Seungeon Lee,Abhisek Dash,Asmaa El Fraihi,Yunah Jang,Elisabeth Kirsten,Qinyuan Wu,Krishna P. Gummadi,Manish Gupta,Abhilasha Ravichander,Muhammad Bilal Zafar,Soumi Das*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: First comprehensive study of conversational LLM agents' Web search lifecycle across four major platforms, revealing variability in search decisions, query strategies, domain preferences, and grounding issues.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conversational LLM agents increasingly rely on Web search, but there's poor understanding of the end-to-end lifecycle of agentic search across different platforms, including quality of search decisions, query formulation, domain preferences, and response grounding.

Method: Combined real-world user interactions (in-vivo) with controlled experiments using platform APIs (in-vitro) across four major conversational platforms: ChatGPT, Claude, Grok, and DeepSeek. Investigated search invocation decisions, query strategies, domain preferences, and response transformation.

Result: Web-search decisions vary substantially across platforms and models; more frequent search invocation doesn't necessarily improve response quality. Agents use different complex querying strategies; platform search engines return results from their preferred domains. Responses are largely grounded but some claims rely on uncited search results, raising attribution and reliability concerns.

Conclusion: Findings have important implications for designing future AI agents and Web search tools optimized for conversational retrieval, highlighting the need for better transparency, attribution, and understanding of platform-specific search behaviors.

Abstract: Conversational LLM agents increasingly rely on Web search, yet the end-to-end lifecycle of agentic search remains poorly understood. We present the first study of Web search across four major conversational platforms (ChatGPT, Claude, Grok, and DeepSeek), combining real-world user interactions (invivo) with controlled experiments using the same platform's models by their APIs (invitro). We investigate the quality of agentic decisions to invoke Web search, their strategies to formulate queries, the potential domain preferences in the search results they receive, and the choices they make when transforming search results into grounded responses. We find that Web-search decisions vary substantially across platforms and models, while more frequent Web-search invocation does not necessarily yield better response quality. We further show that conversational agents employ different complex querying strategies and that platform specific search engines return search results from their preferred domains. Finally, although responses are largely grounded in search results, some claims rely on uncited search results, raising concerns about attribution and reliability. Our findings have important implications for the design of future AI agents and Web search tools optimized for conversational retrieval.

</details>


### [7] [Do AI Agents Understand Computer Architecture?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19387)
*Ambika Sharan,Grigory Chirkov,Soheil Abbasloo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces AutoTuring, a method to distinguish between agents that genuinely understand hardware architecture versus those that just search competently over parameters. It shows that architectural knowledge improves accelerator design by 12.3% over blind search with 70.1% fewer simulator calls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations of hardware design agents cannot determine whether they are truly reasoning about machine architecture or just competently searching over parameters. The distinction matters because architectural reasoning transfers to new architectures while parameter search doesn't.

Method: AutoTuring presents the same agent with identical 15-dimensional accelerator design space twice: once with named architectural knobs and simulator counters (meaningful context), and once as anonymous variables on [0,1] (blind search). Everything else - evaluator, legal space, reachable optima - is identical, isolating the effect of meaning.

Result: Architectural knowledge pays: agents with meaningful context outperformed a modeled H200 by 5.4% and their blind counterparts by 12.3% on average, requiring 70.1% fewer simulator calls. However, adding a critic loop recovered most of the gap for blind agents, suggesting architectural knowledge and structured critique are substitutes.

Conclusion: The comparison methodology itself is the main contribution, not the specific accelerator results. The framework allows distinguishing between genuine architectural reasoning and competent parameter search, revealing that architectural knowledge and structured critique behave as substitutes in hardware design.

Abstract: Agents are increasingly asked to design hardware, and increasingly reported to succeed. Such reports establish that a design improved; they cannot establish why. An agent that improves an accelerator may be reasoning about the machine, or may be searching competently over knobs whose meaning it never recovers -- and only the first transfers to the next architecture. Existing evaluations cannot tell the two apart, because they vary the agent while holding the framing of the problem fixed. We do the opposite. AutoTuring hands the same agent the same 15-dimensional accelerator space twice: once as named architectural knobs with simulator counters, once as anonymous variables on [0,1], with the evaluator, the legal space and the reachable optima held identical, so that the only thing that varies is whether the problem means anything. The gap between the two is the measurement. On a nine-kernel FP16 GEMM basket, meaning pays: the architect beats a modeled H200 by 5.4% and its blind counterpart by 12.3% on average, with 70.1% fewer simulator calls. It does not pay uniquely: a critic loop recovers most of that gap for the blind agent and buys the architect nothing, so architectural knowledge and structured critique behave as substitutes rather than as complements. We report these as preliminary findings -- five to six runs per condition on a single modeled accelerator -- and take the comparison itself, not the accelerator, to be the contribution.

</details>


### [8] [MAGS: Multi-agent Auto-formalization Guarantees Safety for Agentic Outputs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19391)
*Albert Wu,Nicholas Roberts,Tzu-Heng Huang,Haoran Lin,Gil Friedman,Sungjun Cho,Gabriel Orlanski,Frederic Sala*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MAGS is a multi-agent framework that automatically generates formally verified programs with safety guarantees using Dafny as verification-aware intermediate representation, achieving 100% success rate across 220 examples.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-generated code is increasingly complex and difficult to review thoroughly, creating safety/security risks. Existing approaches (fuzz testing, static analysis, LLM verification) struggle to cover all edge cases, while formal verification traditionally requires substantial manual specification work.

Method: MAGS formalizes and freezes human-audited APIs and safety requirements, uses Dafny as verification-aware intermediate representation to generate code, repairs violations using verifier feedback, and compiles verified programs back to executable code.

Result: Achieved 100% success rate in producing programs with non-trivial safety guarantees across 220 examples (100 CUDA kernels, 100 terminal scripts, 20 robotic-arm tasks). Safety and functional evaluations show strong performance across all domains.

Conclusion: MAGS provides a unified approach for generating executable programs with formal safety guarantees, though limitations exist when auto-formalized semantics don't fully capture target behavior.

Abstract: LLM coding agents now generate complex programs at a scale that makes thorough human review increasingly difficult, raising the risk of safety and security failures. Common approaches, including fuzz testing, static analysis, and LLM-as-a-Verifier, can detect many failures but struggle to cover all possible edge cases. Formal verification addresses this by providing machine-checkable guarantees over specified properties, but traditionally demands substantial manual specification and proof engineering. We introduce a unified multi-agent framework, MAGS, that generates executable programs with formal safety guarantees, using Dafny as a verification-aware intermediate representation where safety properties can be mechanically checked. MAGS formalizes and freezes human-audited APIs and safety requirements, translates generated code into Dafny, repairs violations using verifier feedback, and compiles verified programs back into executable code. We evaluate MAGS on 100 CUDA kernels, 100 terminal scripts, and 20 robotic-arm tasks. Across all 220 examples, it achieves a 100% success rate in producing programs with non-trivial safety guarantees against frozen specifications. Independent safety and functional evaluations further show strong performance across all three domains, while revealing failures when the auto-formalized semantics do not fully capture the target behavior.

</details>


### [9] [Agentic AI Networking for Heterogeneous Unmanned Aerial Systems in Low-Altitude Wireless Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19538)
*Nguyen Duc Minh Quang,Chang Liu,Shuangyang Li,Derrick Wing Kwan Ng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a hierarchical LLM-MARL architecture with dual-loop structure to enable adaptive coordination in low-altitude wireless networks for heterogeneous drone services, where conventional methods fail to adapt to changing requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Low-altitude wireless networks (LAWNs) for heterogeneous unmanned aerial systems face challenges in managing the strong coupling between mobility, connectivity, and shared resources. Heterogeneous services have distinct, time-varying requirements, forming a dynamic non-cooperative game. Conventional optimization and learning-based controllers can't autonomously adapt to changing service requirements and resource priorities.

Method: A hierarchical hybrid large language model (LLM)-multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) architecture with dual-loop structure. Outer loop: LLM-assisted game orchestration interprets service requirements and operator intent, reconfigures objectives and resource priorities. Inner loop: Executes decentralized, parameter-conditioned MARL policies under the configured game structure.

Result: A logistics-monitoring case study demonstrates how the framework facilitates coordinated coexistence among heterogeneous services, adapting to evolving operating conditions without retraining the underlying MARL policies.

Conclusion: The proposed hierarchical LLM-MARL architecture addresses adaptation challenges in LAWNs. The paper also discusses key challenges and research directions toward scalable, trustworthy, and adaptive agentic LAWNs.

Abstract: Low-altitude wireless networks (LAWNs) are emerging as a key infrastructure for heterogeneous unmanned aerial systems that support concurrent services within a shared three-dimensional airspace. Their coexistence creates strong coupling among mobility, connectivity, and shared network resources, while heterogeneous services impose distinct and time-varying requirements. These interactions naturally form a dynamic non-cooperative game in which both operating conditions and coordination objectives evolve over time. Conventional optimization and learning-based controllers typically rely on predefined objectives, limiting their ability to adapt autonomously to changing service requirements and resource priorities. To address this challenge, we propose a hierarchical hybrid large language model (LLM)- multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) architecture organized as a dual-loop structure. Specifically, an outer adaptation loop employs LLM-assisted game orchestration to interpret service requirements and operator intent, and reconfigure objectives and resource priorities, while an inner loop executes decentralized, parameter-conditioned MARL policies under the configured game. A logistics-monitoring case study illustrates how the proposed framework facilitates coordinated coexistence among heterogeneous services, adapting to evolving operating conditions without retraining the underlying MARL policies. Finally, we discuss key challenges and research directions toward scalable, trustworthy, and adaptive agentic LAWNs.

</details>


### [10] [Closed-World Resolution Against Tool Hallucination in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19425)
*Laxmipriya Ganesh Iyer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM agents hallucinate tool calls that reference non-existent tools with undeclared arguments, a blind spot that existing tool-selection/gating defenses can't address because they only work with real tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current tool-augmented LLM agent defenses focus on selecting the right real tool or gating access to real tools, but fail to address the fundamental problem where agents hallucinate calls to tools that don't exist at all, passing arguments not defined in any schema.

Method: The paper presents a measurement and benchmark study with: 1) Five-class taxonomy of tool hallucination (H1-H5), 2) Resolution Rung - a training-free, closed-world resolver for reference, 3) Testing across ten hosted models under two invocation surfaces (including raw-JSON), 4) Extension to Model Context Protocol (MCP) with second taxonomy (M1-M5) for multi-server namespace collisions, 5) Release of versioned Hallucinated-Tools Benchmark (HTB) for consistent evaluation.

Result: Measurement reveals 322 genuine hallucinations across models; fabricated-tool calls concentrate on unconstrained raw-JSON surfaces (34 vs 3); model scale doesn't help (675B matches 7-8B); MCP testing shows 154 hallucinations, including from frontier models that were clean on single-registry surfaces due to structural namespace collisions and shadowing in merged environments.

Conclusion: Tool hallucination is a structural blind spot requiring defenses that must precede any causal gate; the irreducible residue is borrowed arguments schema-indistinguishable from valid calls; multi-server environments create additional hallucination surfaces that single registries can't express; benchmark release enables standardized evaluation.

Abstract: Tool-augmented large language model (LLM) agents fail in a way no tool-selection or tool-security method addresses: they call tools that do not exist and pass arguments no schema declares. Existing defenses either pick the right tool (selection) or constrain what an agent may do with real tools (gating), both of which presuppose the emitted call refers to a real tool at all. We show this is a structural blind spot: a hallucinated call is by construction not a decision any gate made, so no gate can reject it. This paper is primarily a measurement and benchmark study. We give a five-class taxonomy of tool hallucination (H1-H5) and, as a reference point, the Resolution Rung: a training-free, closed-world resolver (registry membership plus a signature check) whose interest is where it must sit, not what it computes. We prove hallucination defense must precede any causal gate, and characterize the one irreducible residue (borrowed arguments schema-indistinguishable from a valid call). Across ten hosted models under two invocation surfaces we measure 322 genuine hallucinations; fabricated-tool calls concentrate on the unconstrained raw-JSON surface (34 vs. 3), and model scale does not help (a 675B model matches a 7-8B one). We then extend to the Model Context Protocol, where merging several servers into one namespace creates hallucination surfaces a single registry cannot express (a second taxonomy, M1-M5); on the live MCP surface we measure 154 hallucinations, including from frontier models that were clean on the single-registry surface, because collisions and shadowing are structural to the merge. We release the versioned Hallucinated-Tools Benchmark (HTB) so any resolver is comparable across submissions.

</details>


### [11] [Replan, Repair, or Edit? A Unified Empirical Evaluation of Travel Agents for Itinerary Revision under Resource Disruptions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19654)
*Xiaofei Yuan,Yan Zhang,Shaobo Qiao,Huangleshuai He,Leyan Ni,Mingchen Ju,Lujia Yang,Sijia Xu,Yifu Tang,Zhengyi Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper compares three methods for revising travel itineraries after disruptions: LLM-Z3 full replanning, IPyHOPPER hierarchical repair, and iTIMO local revision. The study uses TREK-derived benchmarks with single and compound disruptions, evaluating effectiveness, plan stability, and computational cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Travel itineraries often become infeasible after acceptance due to disruptions like flight cancellations, hotel unavailability, or attraction closures. Current revision methods have differing task formulations and evaluation protocols that hinder proper comparison, making it difficult to choose appropriate revision approaches.

Method: Conducted systematic empirical study using TREK-derived benchmarks: 500 single-disruption cases and 200 feasible simultaneous compound-disruption cases. Compared three approaches: 1) LLM-Z3 full replanning, 2) IPyHOPPER hierarchical repair, and 3) iTIMO local-revision adapter. Evaluated across effectiveness, plan stability, and computational cost metrics.

Result: LLM-Z3 with Gemini achieved highest compound-disruption success. IPyHOPPER nearly matched LLM-Z3's single-disruption overall success while preserving more of the original itinerary. Successful hierarchical and local repairs made fewer edits and retained more commitments than full replanning. Computational profiles differed significantly: IPyHOPPER used no LLM inference, LLM-Z3 used compact one-call inference, while iTIMO consumed substantially more tokens.

Conclusion: The study provides practical guidelines for balancing feasibility recovery, commitment preservation, and computational cost. Different methods excel in different aspects: full replanning (LLM-Z3) works best for compound disruptions, while hierarchical repair (IPyHOPPER) preserves more of the original plan with good success rates and no LLM inference cost.

Abstract: Travel-planning agents generate itineraries that may become infeasible after acceptance because of flight cancellations, hotel unavailability, or attraction closures. Revising these itineraries involves full replanning, classical plan repair, and LLM-based travel-agent revision, whose differing task formulations and evaluation protocols hinder comparison. We conduct a systematic empirical study using two TREK-derived benchmark sets: 500 single-disruption cases, including feasible and infeasible instances, and 200 feasible simultaneous compound-disruption cases. We compare LLM-Z3 full replanning, IPyHOPPER hierarchical repair, and an iTIMO local-revision adapter across effectiveness, plan stability, and computational cost. LLM-Z3 with Gemini achieved the highest observed compound-disruption success. IPyHOPPER nearly matched that configuration's single-disruption overall success, while preserving substantially more of the accepted itinerary on successful repairs. Successful hierarchical and local repairs made fewer edits and retained more accepted commitments than full replanning. Computational profiles differed: IPyHOPPER used no LLM inference, the evaluated LLM-Z3 adapter used compact one-call inference, and the iTIMO adapter consumed substantially more tokens. The study provides practical guidelines for balancing feasibility recovery, commitment preservation, and computational cost within evaluated settings.

</details>


### [12] [The syntax and semantics of goals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19448)
*David M. Abel,Mark K. Ho*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper examines goals as compositional cognitive representations, drawing parallels with linguistics/logic to explore foundational questions about goal expressivity, design, and efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Goals are typically studied as fixed constraints on behavior, but there's a need to examine the nature of goal representations themselves - their expressivity, design properties, and how different representations can capture the same behaviors.

Method: The authors synthesize existing work that characterizes properties of different goal representations and frame these as points in a broader design space, drawing parallels with syntax-semantics interfaces in linguistics and logic.

Result: The analysis reveals a design space for goal representations with various properties, highlighting how distinguishing form (syntax) from meaning (semantics) of goals can reveal implicit assumptions and provide analytical frameworks.

Conclusion: Understanding goals as representations with both form and meaning helps clarify assumptions about goals, informs study of cognition-motivation interactions, and identifies dimensions of variation across different goal conceptions.

Abstract: In both cognitive science and computer science, goals are conceptualized as cognitive states that flexibly combine with world knowledge to organize and specify purposeful behavior. In this way, goals are compositional representations whose content relates to rational behavior. We here draw attention to goals as representations and their content because it highlights a parallel with other areas in cognitive science - in particular, the syntax-semantics interface in linguistics and logic - while also foregrounding foundational questions about the expressivity, design, and efficiency of different goal representations. For example, goals are typically taken as fixed and imposing constraints on desirable behaviors, but we can also identify constraints on goal representations themselves, such as whether a particular goal language is sufficiently expressive to capture behaviors of interest, or whether different goal representations capture the same behavior. Here, we synthesize work that aims to characterize the properties of different goal representations and suggest these are points of a broader design space. We close by discussing how distinguishing the form and meaning of goals can elucidate the implicit assumptions we make about goals, inform the study of interactions between higher-level cognition and motivation, and isolate axes of variation for different conceptions of goals.

</details>


### [13] [FINSKILLOPS: A Self-Evolving Multi-Agent System for SEC Filing QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19680)
*Yanzhang Ma,Zhenghan Tai,Hanwei Wu,Sizhe Guan,Jianliang Lei,Hailin He,Chaolong Jiang,Jijun Chi,Tung Sum Thomas Kwok,Bohuai Xiao,Jingrui Tian,Xinlu Wu,Xingao Zhan,Peng Lu,Muzhi Li,Yihong Wu,Liheng Ma,Sicheng Lyu,Tianshuo Yan,Junhao Zhu,Yaqian Xu,Lei Ding,Yufei Cui,Ziquan Liu,Boyu Han,Hengli Liu,Ling Zhou,Xinyu Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FINSKILLOPS: A multi-agent system for controlled behavioral maintenance in financial QA that turns recurring failures into scoped skill patches while preventing regressions through targeted validation and version management.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing financial QA systems have fixed reliability behavior after deployment, but new SEC-filing questions repeatedly expose heterogeneous errors in period, entity, evidence use, and calculation. Current self-improvement methods lack control over where corrections should apply and may break previously correct answers.

Method: FINSKILLOPS is a multi-agent system that derives reusable skills from evidence-grounded, typed failure diagnoses and governs them through: 1) targeted validation, 2) protected-case regression checks, 3) negative controls, and 4) versioned replacement or retirement. The system frames post-deployment improvement as controlled behavioral maintenance.

Result: Across six financial QA benchmarks, a single frozen skill registry achieves highest verdict-weighted correctness and reference consistency. Evolved skills raise correctness from 3.70 to 4.55 on enhanced benchmark. In a 12-round operational study, only 6 of 33 proposed skills were promoted, while monitoring non-correct rate fell from 20.0% to 12.5%.

Conclusion: Controlled skill scope, admission, and lifecycle management form the foundation for reliable self-improvement in financial QA systems, enabling the transformation of recurring failures into scoped skill patches without introducing regressions.

Abstract: Financial QA systems are typically improved before deployment through better retrieval, prompting, or agent coordination, leaving their reliability behavior fixed thereafter. In practice, new SEC-filing questions repeatedly expose heterogeneous errors in period, entity, evidence use, and calculation. Existing self-improvement methods can turn failures into new behaviors, but offer limited control over where a correction should apply or which previously correct answers it may break. We therefore frame post-deployment improvement as controlled behavioral maintenance: recurring failures should become scoped skill patches, and each patch should earn deployment with- out introducing regressions. We instantiate this view in FINSKILLOPS, a multi-agent system for SEC filing QA. FINSKILLOPS derives reusable skills from evidence-grounded, typed failure diagnoses and governs them through targeted validation, protected-case regression checks, negative controls, and versioned replacement or retirement. Across six financial QA benchmarks, a single frozen skill registry achieves the highest verdict-weighted correctness and reference consistency among the evaluated systems. Evolved skills raise correctness from 3.70 to 4.55 on our enhanced benchmark. In a separate 12-round operational study, only six of 33 proposed skills are promoted, while the monitoring non-correct rate falls from 20.0% to 12.5%. These results establish controlled skill scope, admission, and lifecycle management as the foundation for reliable self-improvement.

</details>


### [14] [Compositional Reasoning in Language Models under Reinforcement Learning Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19465)
*Yu He,Yingxi Li,Yifei Wang,Ellen Vitercik*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Post-training methods like RL don't reliably enable compositional generalization from decomposed skills to composed tasks, but composed training transfers better to decomposed skills.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world problem solving requires compositional reasoning where models must combine learned skills in novel ways, but the effects of post-training methods like RL on compositional reasoning are not well understood.

Method: Proposed a dependency-graph framework with three complexity levels for formalizing compositional reasoning, instantiated with data-structure tasks for deterministic evaluation, and conducted empirical studies including theoretical analysis and real-world pilot studies.

Result: Found a consistent decomposed-to-composed asymmetry: decomposed skill training doesn't reliably transfer to composed tasks, while composed-task training transfers better to decomposed tasks. Validated findings across length extrapolation, structural distribution shifts, and transfer to unseen skills.

Conclusion: Compositional reasoning exhibits asymmetric generalization patterns, with composed-task training being more effective for transfer learning, suggesting practical implications for how to structure training for better generalization.

Abstract: Compositional reasoning is critical for real-world problem solving: since training data is necessarily limited, models must generalize by composing learned skills in new ways. While post-training methods such as reinforcement learning (RL) have substantially improved the reasoning abilities of language models (LMs), their effects on compositional reasoning remain less well understood. We propose a dependency-graph framework to formalize compositional reasoning, yielding three levels of compositionality with increasing complexity. Empirically, we instantiate this framework with data-structure tasks, which provide deterministic reward computation and clear compositional structure. We find a consistent decomposed-to-composed asymmetry: decomposed-skill training does not reliably transfer to composed tasks, whereas composed-task training transfers more readily back to decomposed tasks. We provide theoretical explanation for this asymmetry, and further evaluate compositional generalization under length extrapolation, structural distribution shift, and transfer to tasks requiring unseen skills. Finally, we present a pilot study on real-world tool-calling benchmarks, showing preliminary evidence that the decomposed-to-composed asymmetry can extend to practical settings.

</details>


### [15] [LearnActCoder: Role-Aware Error Memory for Adaptive Clinical Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19721)
*Meysam Ghaffari,Bhaskar Sen,Nasim Sabetpour,Nina Fatehi,Animesh Agarwal,Carlos Morato*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LearnActCoder is a clinical coding system that learns from past mistakes to improve coding accuracy without model retraining, using a Coder-Judge pipeline with structured error memory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical coding agents face recurring failure modes like unsupported codes, missed conditions, specificity errors, and procedure-coding mismatches. There's a need for systems that can learn from past errors to improve performance without requiring full model retraining or workflow changes.

Method: Learn-Then-Act framework creates a structured Mistake Knowledge Database (MistakeKDB) from errors in a small labeled LEARN batch. False-negative lessons go to a recall-oriented Coder, false-positives to a precision-oriented Judge. LearnActCoder implements this as a Coder-Judge pipeline with lookup-table grounding.

Result: On 150 MIMIC-III notes, structured MistakeKDB improved CPT F1 by 5.9 percentage points (ICD-9 not significant). On MIMIC-IV, memory shifted ICD-10 coding toward higher precision at recall cost, leaving F1 unchanged. Applied to 1,000 MIMIC-III notes, it maintained stable ICD performance, showing scaling/stability.

Conclusion: Structured, feedback-derived error memory is useful for adapting clinical coding behavior across cases without weight updates or workflow changes. However, absolute CPT/HCPCS performance remains low, and the system was evaluated retrospectively rather than in clinical deployment.

Abstract: Clinical coding agents repeatedly encounter the same failure modes, including unsupported codes, missed documented conditions, specificity errors, and procedure-coding convention mismatches. We introduce Learn-Then-Act, an inference-time adaptation framework that converts errors from a small labeled LEARN batch into a structured Mistake Knowledge Database (MistakeKDB). False-negative lessons are routed to a recall-oriented Coder, while false-positive lessons are routed to a precision-oriented Judge. We instantiate the framework in LearnActCoder, a Coder-Judge clinical coding pipeline with lookup-table grounding where available. On 150 matched MIMIC-III notes, structured MistakeKDB improves CPT F1 by 5.9 percentage points, while raw-example and reflection-style memories remain near the no-memory baseline; the ICD-9 improvement is not significant. On a matched MIMIC-IV cohort, memory shifts ICD-10 coding toward higher precision at a recall cost, leaving F1 statistically unchanged. Applying the same memory to 1,000 held-out MIMIC-III notes maintains a stable ICD operating point, providing scale/stability evidence. Overall, the results are consistent with structured, feedback-derived error memory being useful for adapting clinical coding behavior across cases without weight updates or changes to the underlying workflow. Absolute CPT/HCPCS performance remains low, and the system is evaluated retrospectively rather than in clinical deployment.

</details>


### [16] [Safety Beyond the Interface: Detecting Harm via Latent States in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19472)
*Alizishaan Khatri,Chiquita Prabhu,Omkar Neogi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Lightweight MLP probes trained on LLM activations achieve competitive harm detection performance with 1000x smaller models, reducing latency and compute costs for safety-critical deployments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: External safety guardrails for LLMs in autonomous systems create latency and compute overhead, limiting utility in resource-constrained, time-critical deployments. Existing guardrail models are blind to the LLM's internal workings, creating an assurance gap. The key question is whether the model already knows when content is harmful.

Method: Extract activations from LLaMA-3.1-8B and train lightweight MLP classifier probes (12.6M parameters) to detect harmful prompts from the model's internal representations. Probes are evaluated on three datasets: WildJailbreak, Beavertails, and AEGIS 2.0.

Result: The lightweight probes achieve impressive F1 scores: 99% on WildJailbreak, 83% on Beavertails, and 84% on AEGIS 2.0. These results are competitive with guard models that are 1000x larger while significantly reducing latency and compute costs.

Conclusion: LLMs contain internal knowledge about harmfulness that can be efficiently extracted using lightweight probes, enabling effective safety detection without the overhead of external guardrail models, making autonomous systems more practical for resource-constrained deployments.

Abstract: Autonomous systems increasingly rely on Large Language Models (LLMs) yet the safety infrastructure surrounding these models introduces latency and compute overhead. This limits utility in resource-constrained, time-critical deployments. Existing external guardrail models remain blind to the model's internal workings, creating a fundamental assurance gap. We ask: does the model already know when the content is harmful? We extract activations from LLaMA-3.1-8B and train lightweight MLP classifier probes (12.6M parameters) to detect harmful prompts. Evaluated on WildJailbreak, Beavertails, and AEGIS 2.0, our probes achieve F1 scores of 99%, 83%, and 84%, respectively competitive with 1000x larger guard models while cutting latency and compute costs.

</details>


### [17] [A Proposal for an Agentic AI Architecture to Support Multi-Domain Decision-Making in the Brazilian Armed Forces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20080)
*Gioliano de Oliveira Braga,Sidnei Barbieri,Ágney Lopes Roth Ferraz,Wagner Comin Sonaglio,Henrique Curi de Miranda e Lourenço Alves Pereira*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a conceptual Agentic AI architecture for autonomous, auditable decision support across Brazilian Armed Forces to address data overload in complex multi-domain environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Growing complexity of multi-domain operational environments (land, aerospace, naval, cyber, EMS) is creating data overload that strains the OODA decision cycle, while current AI systems in defense are reactive, isolated tools requiring heavy human operator involvement for integration and decision-making.

Method: Proposes a conceptual Agentic AI architecture featuring systems that can plan, access data sources, execute tools, and act autonomously and audibly. Includes discussion of data/sensor access requirements and security/permission safeguards for responsible employment across administrative, strategic, operational, and tactical contexts.

Result: Identifies four key application fronts: decision support, situational analysis, feasibility studies, and countermeasure suggestion. Framework addresses the need for autonomous yet auditable AI systems capable of supporting decision-making across all three Brazilian Armed Forces (Navy, Army, Air Force).

Conclusion: An Agentic AI architecture is necessary to transform current reactive defense AI systems into proactive, integrated tools that can handle multi-domain complexity while maintaining accountability through auditability and appropriate security safeguards across all military contexts.

Abstract: The growing complexity of multi-domain operational environments (land, aerospace, naval, cyber, and electromagnetic spectrum) has increased the volume and velocity of data reaching command-and-control (C2) centers, straining the observe-orient-decide-act (OODA) decision cycle. Artificial Intelligence (AI) systems currently employed in defense are, in general, reactive and isolated tools that still rely heavily on human operators to integrate information, assess scenarios, and formulate courses of action. This paper proposes a conceptual Agentic AI architecture for AI systems that can plan, access data sources, execute tools, and act autonomously and audibly, aimed at supporting decision-making across the three Brazilian Armed Forces (Navy, Army, and Air Force). Four application fronts are discussed (decision support, situational analysis, feasibility studies, and countermeasure suggestion), as well as the data and sensor access requirements and the security and permission safeguards necessary for responsible employment across administrative, strategic, operational, and tactical contexts.

</details>


### [18] [QVAC Genesis III: A Large-Scale, High-Quality Open Synthetic STEM Corpus for Efficient Language Model Pre-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19513)
*Davide Vitabile,N. Ranjan,Akshay Nambiar,Kamal K. Gupta,Amril Nazir*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: QVAC Genesis III is a 191.43B-token STEM-focused synthetic dataset created to provide high per-token learning value for small language models targeting edge AI deployment, using a dual generation strategy of corrective explanations from student failures and contrastive reasoning from successes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a critical bottleneck in high-quality pre-training data for educational and STEM-specific language models targeting edge AI and on-device deployment where token budgets are constrained. The open ecosystem lacks STEM-focused synthetic datasets that deliver high per-token learning value efficiently for small models.

Method: Created QVAC Genesis III corpus using a dual generation strategy: targeted teacher distillation with a weak edge-scale student model, converting student failures into corrective explanations and expanding successes into contrastive option-level reasoning over all answer choices. Also introduced LLM-as-a-parser evaluation protocol to extract final answers from free-form outputs.

Result: Models trained with QVAC Genesis III outperform those trained with Cosmopedia-v2 and Cosmo-1B across ARC, GPQA Diamond, and MMLU STEM benchmarks, achieving up to +28.57% on ARC-E and +21.35% on ARC-C, with Valid Answer Rate up to 99.45%.

Conclusion: QVAC Genesis III effectively addresses the STEM-focused synthetic data gap for small language models, delivering superior performance for edge AI deployment through its innovative dual generation strategy and achieving significant improvements across STEM benchmarks.

Abstract: High-quality pre-training data is a critical bottleneck for educational and STEM-specific language models targeting edge AI and on-device deployment where token budgets are tightly constrained. While major organizations train ever-larger models on private corpora, the open ecosystem lacks STEM-focused synthetic datasets that deliver high per-token learning value efficiently for small models. To address this gap, we introduce QVAC Genesis III, a 191.43B-token, STEM-focused multi-domain synthetic corpus covering 19 domains across several difficulty levels and different educational styles. QVAC Genesis III is built via a dual generation strategy that performs targeted teacher distillation using a weak edge-scale student model as signal: the student's failures are converted into corrective explanations, while its successes are expanded into contrastive option-level reasoning over all answer choices. We further introduce an LLM-as-a-parser evaluation protocol that extracts final answers from free-form outputs and tracks both accuracy and answer validity. To validate the effectiveness of our QVAC Genesis III data, we conduct controlled from-scratch ablations with 1.7B-parameter models, showing that models trained with QVAC Genesis III consistently outperform both models trained with the open-source synthetic corpus Cosmopedia-v2 and the publicly released Cosmo-1B model across ARC, GPQA Diamond, and MMLU STEM benchmarks, achieving up to +28.57% on ARC-E and +21.35% on ARC-C, while reaching a Valid Answer Rate of up to 99.45%.

</details>


### [19] [Language-model groups overstate consensus when replaying human deliberation on a reasoning task](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20543)
*Tengfei Shao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM agent groups show substantially higher consensus rates than human groups in Wason task discussions, regardless of measurement approach, but this artificial consensus doesn't correlate with collective accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate how operationalization of participation and final states affects consensus rate estimates in collective cognition studies, and to compare human and LLM agent groups on these measures.

Method: Replayed 100 held-out human Wason groups with matched LLM agent groups, seeded with belief-anchored agents based on pre-discussion answers. Conducted sensitivity analyses including submit-based comparisons (n=98) and participation-matched comparisons (n=45) with both chat and reasoning modes.

Result: Human consensus estimates ranged 24.0-57.0% depending on scoring definitions, while agent groups showed 34.0-44.4 percentage point higher consensus rates. Agents maintained near-unanimous consensus even when wrong, and consensus did not track collective accuracy. Agent groups were biased estimators of human group outcomes.

Conclusion: LLM agent groups produce artificially high consensus that doesn't reflect human deliberative outcomes or accuracy. Studies using simulated groups need scoring-explicit approaches to assess their validity as estimators of human collective cognition.

Abstract: Full-consensus rates are often treated as indicators of collective cognition, yet depend on how participation and final states are operationalized. We replayed 100 held-out human Wason groups with matched large language model (LLM) agent groups, seeding one belief-anchored agent per participant's pre-discussion answer and scoring agents and people with the same code. Across human scoring definitions, estimates ranged from 24.0% to 57.0%; about one fifth of participants never posted, whereas agents almost always did. Agent groups remained more consensual in two post-unblinding sensitivity analyses: the submit-based comparison (n = 98) yielded gaps of 34.0 and 43.9 percentage points for chat and reasoning modes, and the participation-matched comparison (n = 45) yielded gaps of 34.1 and 44.4 points. These complementary routes reduced different measurement asymmetries yet converged within 0.5 percentage points. The gap persisted without early stopping and under a reparameterization removing the memorizable answer; reasoning-mode groups then agreed nearly unanimously, mostly on incorrect answers. Simulated consensus did not track collective accuracy, and belief-anchored agent groups were biased estimators of the human group-outcome distribution in this setting. These analyses provide a scoring-explicit basis for assessing simulated-group estimates of human deliberative outcomes.

</details>


### [20] [LLM-as-an-Improver: Turning Verification into Better Candidates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19515)
*Akiyoshi Tomihari,Yuma Ichikawa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VRR (Verify-Repair-Reselect) enhances LLM selection methods by using verification feedback not just to rank candidates but to repair and generate improved candidates, recovering correct solutions even when the initial candidate pool is entirely wrong.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current verifier-based selection methods for LLMs only use verification as a ranking step and discard verification feedback after evaluating a fixed candidate pool. The paper asks whether verification can improve the candidate set itself, not just select from it.

Method: Proposes VRR (Verify-Repair-Reselect) which uses verification feedback to generate and reselect improved candidates. It retains the initial winner while conditionally generating three complementary alternatives: repaired versions of the winner and runner-up, and a solution based on a new approach. Invalid and duplicate candidates are filtered using only inference-time information, then the final answer is reselected under original evaluation criteria.

Result: VRR improves over fixed-pool verifier-based selection across diverse models and benchmarks for code generation and reasoning tasks. It can recover correct solutions even when all candidates in the initial pool are incorrect.

Conclusion: Verification feedback can serve a broader role: not just selecting among existing solutions but also constructing stronger candidates beyond the initial pool. LLMs can function as improvers, not just generators, through this feedback loop.

Abstract: Verifier-based selection improves LLM performance by generating multiple candidate solutions and using a verifier to select the most promising one. However, existing methods typically treat verification only as a ranking step and discard its feedback once a fixed candidate pool has been evaluated. In this paper, we ask whether verification can also improve the candidate set itself. To this end, we introduce LLM-as-an-Improver and propose Verify--Repair--Reselect (VRR), which uses verification feedback to generate and reselect improved candidates. VRR retains the initial winner while conditionally generating three complementary alternatives: repaired versions of the winner and runner-up, and a solution based on a new approach. It filters invalid and duplicate candidates using only inference-time information and then reselects the final answer under the original evaluation criteria. Across diverse models and code-generation and reasoning benchmarks, VRR improves over fixed-pool verifier-based selection in many settings and can recover correct solutions even when all candidates in the initial pool are incorrect. These results highlight a broader role for LLMs as improvers: verification feedback can not only select among existing solutions but also construct stronger candidates beyond the initial pool.

</details>


### [21] [An Architecture for Long-Horizon Agents: Levels, Ticks and Cascaded Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19519)
*Erik Nijkamp,Anurag Koul,Egor Pakhomov,Bo Pang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a hierarchical architecture for language-model agents to handle long-horizon tasks spanning days/weeks without forgetting, enabling continual learning through persistent memory and escalation mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current language-model agents struggle with long-horizon tasks that exceed context windows, process lifetimes, and human attention spans, requiring continual operation without forgetting as a prerequisite for continual learning.

Method: A hierarchical architecture with: (i) time-scale indexed levels with bounded file summaries, (ii) clocked ticks as autonomous action units, and (iii) cascaded intelligence where work escalates to more capable models only after review failure.

Result: In a 10-day campaign, the agent reproduced a published reinforcement-learning result with only daily human attendance, demonstrating: (1) maintained task continuity across context resets, (2) early operating knowledge changed later behavior without weight changes, and (3) identified where learned components could integrate.

Conclusion: Continual learning for long-horizon agents requires a substrate that outlasts contexts and processes, with learning mechanisms belonging in the harness checks rather than model weights.

Abstract: Language-model agents are increasingly asked to carry out work spanning days or weeks, such as an operations remediation or a research programme. Such a task outlives any context window, any process and any interval at which a person can attend. In this paper, we argue that a long-horizon agent must run continually without forgetting before it can learn continually. This ability lies in the harness around the model rather than in the model itself. We derive seven bottlenecks from the long-horizon setting and answer them with a hierarchical architecture of three parts: (i) levels indexed by time scale, each keeping a bounded file summarising the level below; (ii) a clocked tick as the unit of autonomous action; and (iii) cascaded intelligence, where work is escalated to a more capable model only after failing review. We report on a ten-day campaign in which an agent built on this architecture reproduced a published reinforcement-learning result with a human attending once a day, and show (1) the agent kept the thread across every context reset and session boundary of the campaign, (2) operating knowledge written early changed later behaviour with no change to model weights, and (3) where learned components would enter such a system. Overall, our experience suggests continual learning for these agents needs a substrate outliving every context and process, and the checks the harness already runs are where a learner belongs.

</details>


### [22] [EconSkills: Studying Skill Transfer and Retrieval for Web Agents on Live Economic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19523)
*Yinzhu Quan,Zefang Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EconSkills introduces a skill library and evaluation framework that distills verified web trajectories into reusable procedures for retrieving live economic data, demonstrating that abstracted procedural knowledge transfers better than replaying raw trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Web agents frequently revisit the same websites but current evaluations discard procedures learned in previous successful interactions. There's a need to capture and reuse verified procedures to improve efficiency in economic data retrieval tasks.

Method: Created EconSkills: a skill library that distills verified EconWebArena trajectories into parameterized standard operating procedures. Each skill includes scope, navigation procedures, site-specific guidance, verification checks, and recovery steps, with source-instance values replaced by placeholders. Evaluates both controlled transfer (whether known procedures transfer to new tasks) and library-scale retrieval (whether agents can select appropriate skills from a library).

Result: Matched skills improved success over no-skill prompting and required fewer steps on successful tasks. Abstraction proved substantially more effective than replaying raw trajectories. At library scale, retrieval was competitive with baseline and performed best on directly covered tasks. Approximate matches on uncovered tasks offset some gains. Browser trajectories showed procedural guidance shortens navigation while semantic verification remains necessary.

Conclusion: Reusable economic web procedures can effectively transfer across task instances. The framework provides concrete design targets for coverage-aware skill selection and context delivery, establishing a foundation for more efficient web agents that can leverage learned procedures.

Abstract: Web agents often revisit the same sites, yet most evaluations discard the procedures learned in earlier successful interactions. We introduce EconSkills, a skill library and evaluation framework that distills verified EconWebArena trajectories into parameterized standard operating procedures for retrieving live economic data. Each skill records its scope, navigation procedure, site-specific guidance, verification checks, and recovery steps while replacing source-instance values with placeholders. EconSkills separates two questions: whether a known relevant procedure transfers to a held-out task, and whether an agent can retain that benefit when selecting from a library. In controlled transfer, matched skills improve success over no-skill prompting and require fewer steps on paired successes, while abstraction is substantially more effective than replaying raw trajectories. At library scale, retrieval is competitive with the no-skill baseline overall and performs best on directly covered tasks; coverage-stratified outcomes show that approximate matches on uncovered tasks offset these gains. Browser trajectories further identify when procedural guidance shortens portal-specific navigation and when semantic verification remains necessary. These results establish that reusable economic web procedures can transfer across task instances and provide a concrete design target for coverage-aware selection and context delivery.

</details>


### [23] [A Unified Evaluation Framework for Trustworthy Large Language Models, Agentic AI, and Multimodal Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19524)
*Shaina Raza,Ahmed Y. Radwan,Imran Liaquat,Kathryn Hume*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A unified framework for AI trustworthiness assessment that goes beyond benchmark scores, connecting output-level, trajectory-level, and cross-modal evaluation across eight dimensions with meta-evaluation and governance integration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Benchmark scores alone are insufficient for assessing the trustworthiness of modern AI systems (LLMs, agentic systems, multimodal models). Current evaluation methods lack interpretability for development and oversight, and there's a need for unified assessment that preserves system-specific metrics while enabling meaningful comparisons.

Method: Proposes a unified framework with eight trustworthiness dimensions (capability, robustness, safety, fairness, transparency, governance, oversight, efficiency) that connects three assessment levels: output-level, trajectory-level, and cross-modal. Includes mapping native measurements to common performance bands with uncertainty estimates and traceable evidence, plus a meta-evaluation layer for evaluating the evaluation's validity, reliability, and reproducibility.

Result: The framework provides multidimensional profiles to expose system strengths/weaknesses, includes safety-critical overrides to prevent aggregate scores from masking failures, and maps to governance frameworks, international standards, and EU regulatory requirements. It structures both system performance assessment and the credibility of supporting evidence.

Conclusion: The proposed framework offers a structured approach to AI trustworthiness assessment that connects technical evaluation with oversight needs, but requires empirical validation across deployment contexts as an essential next step.

Abstract: Benchmark scores alone provide an incomplete basis for assessing the trustworthiness of modern artificial intelligence systems. Large language models (LLMs), agentic systems, and multimodal models (MLLMs) require different forms of assessment, yet their evaluation evidence must remain interpretable for development and oversight. We propose a unified framework that connects output-level, trajectory-level, and cross-modal assessment through eight trustworthiness dimensions: capability, robustness, safety, fairness, transparency, governance, oversight, and efficiency. The framework preserves system-specific metrics while mapping native measurements to common performance bands, accompanied by uncertainty estimates and traceable evidence. A meta-evaluation layer examines the validity, reliability, and reproducibility of the evaluation itself. Multidimensional profiles expose strengths and weaknesses, while safety-critical overrides prevent aggregate scores from masking critical failures. Mappings to governance frameworks, international standards, and European Union regulatory requirements connect technical assessment with oversight needs. The framework provides a structured basis for assessing both system performance and the credibility of the evidence supporting it, with empirical validation across deployment contexts remaining an essential next step.

</details>


### [24] [Self Improvement via Fast Tree-search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19526)
*Xinghong Fu,Aravinth Kulanthaivelu,Yutaro Yamada*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SIFT introduces a sample-efficient self-improvement framework that uses LLM-as-a-judge pairwise comparisons and disaggregated tree search to significantly reduce computational costs while improving coding performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing self-improvement approaches for coding agents are costly and compute-intensive, with the main bottleneck being the evaluation of candidate self-modifications through re-running benchmark tasks. There's a need for more efficient methods under strict budget constraints.

Method: Recursive Self Improvement via Fast Tree-search (SIFT) uses LLM-as-a-judge pairwise comparisons between candidate patches, aggregates results with a regularized Bradley-Terry model, and employs rank-based parent sampling in a lightweight disaggregated tree search. Expensive downstream evaluations are reserved only for most promising nodes.

Result: SIFT outperforms existing tree-search based self-evolution frameworks on the full Polyglot benchmark while requiring significantly lower resources in terms of CPU hours, wall clock time, and API cost.

Conclusion: The SIFT framework demonstrates that efficient self-improvement is achievable through judicious use of LLM-based pairwise comparisons and disaggregated tree search, enabling significant performance gains under strict computational constraints.

Abstract: Coding agents can recursively modify their own implementations, forming a loop of self-improvement. While prior work shows this can boost performance on coding benchmarks, existing approaches are costly and compute-intensive. We introduce a simple, sample-efficient self-improvement framework that significantly improves coding performance under strict budget constraints. We identify evaluation of candidate self-modifications as the main runtime bottleneck since prior approaches estimate their effectiveness by re-running a subset of benchmark tasks with the modified agent, which is time-consuming. We introduce Recursive Self Improvement via Fast Tree-search (SIFT), which augments these downstream task evaluations with an LLM-as-a-judge signal that performs pairwise comparisons between candidate patches, where the win-loss record is aggregated with a regularized Bradley-Terry model, and the resulting strength scores drive rank-based parent sampling inside a lightweight disaggregated tree search. Expensive downstream task evaluations are reserved only for the most promising nodes. Using a fully disaggregated tree search pipeline, the judge scores provide intermediate signal to guide exploration on promising candidate patches without being bottlenecked by slow evaluation runs. SIFT outperforms existing tree-search based self-evolution frameworks on the full Polyglot benchmark with significantly lower resource requirements in terms of CPU hours, wall clock time, and API cost.

</details>


### [25] [When Hiring Becomes Agent-Mediated: Evaluating Access and Recurrence in Two-Agent Résumé Screening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19530)
*Jian Gao,Hang Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Two-agent resume screening (employer + candidate agents exchanging evidence) outperforms static one-call screening, advancing more applications and changing decisions in both directions, showing that screening procedure matters as hiring becomes agent-mediated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current automated resume screening treats hiring as a static, one-sided judgment, ignoring that real hiring is bilateral with employers assessing fit and candidates presenting/defending qualifications.

Method: Researchers studied a two-agent alternative using GPT-5.5 and Claude Opus 4.7 on 600 constructed resume-job pairs. The two-agent approach involves employer-side and candidate-side agents exchanging evidence and updating judgments before decisions.

Result: Two-agent screening advances more applications (33.3%→39.3% for GPT-5.5; 34.0%→35.5% for Opus). On borderline cases, pass rates rise from 4.5%→26.2% and 6.5%→16.1%. Procedures change decisions in both directions, and no one-call threshold recovers two-agent selections consistently. Two-agent-only selections recur less often than shared selections.

Conclusion: As hiring becomes agent-mediated on both sides, the screening procedure itself (not just the underlying model) shapes who reaches human review and how reliably that access recurs, highlighting the importance of bilateral interaction in automated screening.

Abstract: Hiring is bilateral: employers assess fit, while candidates present and defend evidence of their qualifications. Yet résumé screening, the first gate, is commonly automated as a static, one-call judgment over a résumé-job pair. We study a two-agent alternative in which employer-side and candidate-side agents represent these roles, exchange evidence, and update their judgments before deciding who advances. We compare procedures on 600 constructed résumé-job pairs using GPT-5.5 and Claude Opus 4.7. Two-agent screening advances more applications (33.3% to 39.3% for GPT-5.5; 34.0% to 35.5% for Opus 4.7). Across three runs on the common 191-pair borderline pool, pass-instance rates rise from 4.5% to 26.2% and from 6.5% to 16.1%, respectively. This is not a uniform relaxation: two-agent screening rejects applications one-call advances, changing decisions in both directions. At similar pass volumes, the procedures advance different applications, and no one-call threshold recovers applications consistently selected by two-agent screening. Among discovery-selected cases re-executed in fresh runs, two-agent-only selections recur less often than shared selections, clearly under GPT-5.5 and less certainly under Opus 4.7, while a separate one-call follow-up shows no comparable decline. As hiring becomes agent-mediated on both sides, the screening procedure, not only the model behind it, shapes who reaches human review and how reliably that access recurs.

</details>


### [26] [Continual Enterprise World Model Discovery in Dynamic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19551)
*Shambhavi Mishra,David Vazquez,Perouz Taslakian,Marco Pedersoli,Jose Dolz,Issam H. Laradji*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An agent learns and continually updates business rules in enterprise systems by interacting with data and observing outcomes, without prior knowledge of the rules, outperforming previous lookup approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
