As these models reason in symbolic representations, they can seamlessly translate game states into interpretable feedback for human-like player guidance. This paper introduces a hybrid qualitative model designed for Camelot Jr., a block-puzzle game that requires constructing multi-level bridges to connect two avatars stationed on separate towers. The game poses a challenge for the player, who must make platforms stable, plan their path, and ensure they use all the provided blocks. To handle the precise physics required by the domain, we integrate a mathematical center-of-mass stability logic to guide our qualitative solver. Our work facilitates spatial skill training in Camelot Jr. and contributes to the development of human-centric, explainable game-playing agents.

</details>


### [76] [Generating Heterogeneous 3D Geological Microstructures from 2D Images via a Stable Diffusion-Adversarial Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20358)
*Ali Aouf,Eric Laloy,Bart Rogiers,Christophe De Vleeschouwer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Hybrid diffusion-adversarial model for 3D microstructure generation from 2D images, overcoming GAN limitations for complex heterogeneous materials


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 3D imaging for clay/cementitious materials is expensive and technically limited; existing GAN-based methods like SliceGAN struggle with complex heterogeneous microstructures

Method: Hybrid approach combining denoising diffusion models with adversarial loss (replacing standard denoising loss) for stable training when 3D ground truth is unavailable

Result: Model generates microstructures of varying complexity with minimal slice artefacts and close agreement with ground-truth phase fractions and structural descriptors

Conclusion: The hybrid diffusion-adversarial framework effectively generates complex 3D microstructures from 2D images, addressing limitations of previous GAN-based methods

Abstract: Characterizing the physical properties of clay and cementitious materials matters across many fields, from materials science to geological waste disposal. Property simulation typically calls for 3D imaging, which is expensive, not always accessible, and technically limited for certain materials. Recent progress in deep generative models offers a way around this, reconstructing 3D volumes from the more easily acquired 2D images.
  Among GAN-based methods for 3D microstructure generation, SliceGAN has shown strong results for homogeneous isotropic and anisotropic systems. It struggles, however, to capture the finer detail of more complex heterogeneous microstructures, which motivates alternative generative frameworks.
  We introduce a hybrid approach that draws on the stability and generation quality of denoising diffusion models. Since no 3D ground truth is available, we replace the standard denoising loss with an adversarial loss, which yields a stable training process in our experiments. We show that the resulting model generates microstructures of varying complexity with minimal slice artefacts and close agreement with ground-truth phase fractions and structural descriptors.

</details>


### [77] [The Organization of Inference: Information, Resource Constraints, and AI Production](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20449)
*Yukun Zhang,Kemu Xu,Yishen Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows how workflow organization and information access determine AI productivity in software engineering tasks, finding that planning workflows outperform direct execution when given sufficient computational resources and task information.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The economic value of AI inference depends on how computational capacity and task information are distributed across production stages, motivating study of organizational margins in AI workflow design.

Method: Controlled workflow experiments on externally verified software-engineering tasks using matched resource panels with varying token ceilings (12,000 and 24,000), comparing direct execution vs. planning workflows with different information constraints.

Result: Direct execution maintains 59.6% success rate regardless of token ceilings, while planning success increases from 36.2% to 51.2% with higher capacity. Planning disadvantage narrows by 15 percentage points with more resources. Task-informed planning shows 29.6-point advantage over direct execution at higher capacity.

Conclusion: Scale determines available capacity, but workflow structure and information access shape productive value; properly organized planning workflows with sufficient resources and information can outperform direct execution significantly.

Abstract: The economic value of inference depends on how capacity and task information are distributed across stages of AI production. We study these organizational margins using controlled workflow experiments on externally verified software-engineering tasks. In two matched resource panels, direct execution records the same success rate of 59.6 percent at logical-token ceilings of 12,000 and 24,000, while success under information-constrained planning rises from 36.2 to 51.2 percent. The planning disadvantage narrows by 15.0 percentage points (95 percent task-cluster bootstrap interval: 4.2 to 25.8). A strict read-only planning campaign varies whether the planner sees the task issue. At 12,000 tokens, issue access raises success by about 16 percentage points over issue-hidden planning. Compared with direct execution, task-informed planning is about 10 points lower at 12,000 tokens; at 24,000 tokens, it shows a 29.6-point advantage. In the resource panels, direct execution uses substantially less than either ceiling, while the planning workflow's binding rate falls from 46.2 to 0.8 percent and downstream execution accounts for 89.9 percent of the increase in total use. Scale determines the capacity available to a system; workflow and information structure shape the productive value

</details>


### [78] [SkillAA: Attribution-Guided Skill-Graph Updating with Targeted Validation and Rollback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20455)
*Ziqiao Shang,Ling-Yue Ge,Lan-Zhe Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillAA introduces a structured skill-optimization framework that uses a unified graph to represent skills, enabling attribution-guided repair and validation for frozen language models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for using external skills with language models often edit skills directly from failed rollouts without structured routing from failures to editable locations. Current skill graphs also underutilize semantic boundaries, object addresses, and topological dependencies for skill retrieval, targeted updating, and scoped validation.

Method: SkillAA represents skill applicability, execution, and composition in a unified graph structure. It contrasts successful and failed executions to route candidate repairs to specific graph objects, updates only selected local structures, and uses Local and Big Gates to screen candidate changes before commitment.

Result: With gpt-5.6-sol, SkillAA achieves 81.5% on SearchQA, 66.7% on LiveMath, and 91.2% on DocVQA, attaining the highest observed mean in every main setting.

Conclusion: The results support the utility of attribution-guided graph editing and graph-scoped validation for structured skill optimization in frozen language models.

Abstract: External skills provide domain procedures without parameter updates, but existing methods often edit skills directly from failed rollouts without structured routing from an observed failure to an editable location; existing skill graphs also underuse semantic boundaries, object addresses, and topological dependencies for skill retrieval, targeted updating, and scoped validation. We introduce SkillAA (Skill Abductive Attribution), a structured skill-optimization framework for frozen language models. It represents skill applicability, execution, and composition in a unified graph, allowing the same structure to support skill selection, attribution-guided repair, and update validation. SkillAA contrasts successful and failed executions to route candidate repairs to specific graph objects, updates only the selected local structure, and uses Local and Big Gates to screen candidate changes before commitment. With gpt-5.6-sol, SkillAA reaches 81.5%, 66.7%, and 91.2% on SearchQA, LiveMath, and DocVQA, respectively, and attains the highest observed mean in every main setting. These results support the utility of attribution-guided graph editing and graph-scoped validation.

</details>


### [79] [How Do Agent Harnesses Create Value? Planning Information and Release Control in Stateful LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20474)
*Yukun Zhang,Kemu Xu,Yishen Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Agent systems using fixed planning guidance plus verification outperform sham guidance, with verification catching 61% of errors at minimal cost; which component matters more depends on liability levels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to understand how different components of agent systems—supply planning guidance, execution organization, and completion checking—affect success rates, error acceptance, and operational costs in real-world applications like retail and airline operations.

Method: The study uses controlled experiments comparing prewritten task-specific plans (Fixed) against shuffled policy text matched in word count (Sham) to isolate guidance content effects. They conducted two retail experiments and an airline pilot on a benchmark platform (τ²-bench), analyzing 265 matched cells with statistical methods including 90% task-clustered bootstrap intervals.

Result: Fixed planning improves oracle-verified success by 7.17 percentage points (90% CI: 1.15-13.36), with greater benefits for higher-complexity tasks. A read-only terminal verifier rejects 61% of invalid episodes while withholding only 17% of correct ones, at less than one cent additional cost per episode. The relative importance of planning vs. verification depends on liability costs: planning dominates at low liability, while verification's error prevention dominates at high liability.

Conclusion: Effective agent systems require both planning guidance and verification, but their relative importance varies with liability costs. For high-liability scenarios, standalone verification captures most of the error prevention benefits of full planning-plus-verification systems at a fraction of the cost, making it a cost-effective solution.

Abstract: Agent harnesses supply planning guidance, organize execution, and check completion. We study how these components affect success, erroneous acceptance, and cost in two Retail experiments and an Airline pilot in $τ^2$-bench. The primary comparison pairs prewritten task-specific plans (Fixed) with shuffled policy text matched in word count (Sham), isolating the contribution of guidance content. Across 265 matched cells, Fixed improves oracle-verified success by 7.17 percentage points (90\% task-clustered bootstrap interval, 1.15--13.36 points), with gains concentrated in higher-complexity tasks. A read-only terminal verifier rejects 61\% of Retail oracle-invalid episodes while withholding 17\% of correct ones, at less than one cent of additional cost per episode. Which component matters more depends on the loss assigned to erroneous acceptance: at low liability the planning gain dominates; at high liability the verifier's avoided false passes dominate---and a standalone verifier captures nearly all the false-pass benefit of the full planning-plus-verification stack at a fraction of its cost.

</details>


### [80] [SoL-Pi: Recursively Scaling Auto-Research Loops for Efficient Agent Harness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20519)
*Haozhe Liu,Tian Ye,Sensen Gao,Qihang Cao,Yitong Li,Mingchen Zhuge,Duomin Wang,Ruihua Zhang,Ping Luo,Jiawang Bian,Lei Zhu,Ligeng Zhu,Enze Xie,Song Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SoL-Pi is an automated system that reduces token usage in coding agents by 45-49% while maintaining comparable performance through optimized harness design and RSI-inspired improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As coding agents operate autonomously in long trajectories with tool use and feedback, token efficiency becomes crucial for scaling recursive self-improvement (RSI) systems. The need arises to reduce API costs while maintaining performance in production-level environments.

Method: Uses an RSI-inspired approach at the harness layer, scaling auto-research loops across diverse environments for harness rollouts. Through evolutionary selection, four key mechanisms survive: action execution, context compaction, observation handling, and delegated reading, forming the SoL-Pi system.

Result: On the 51-task EdgeBench evaluation, SoL-Pi achieves performance comparable to Pi across GPT-5.6 Sol and Opus 5 models while reducing recorded token traffic by 44.7-49.0% and API cost by about one third. Estimated hourly savings: $8.75-$13.50 vs native Codex/Claude Code harnesses, $4.36-$5.71 vs Pi.

Conclusion: SoL-Pi demonstrates that automated harness discovery can achieve production-level outcomes with substantial token efficiency gains, making recursive self-improvement more scalable and cost-effective through systematic optimization of execution mechanisms.

Abstract: As coding agents move from supervised code completion to unattended, around-the-clock exploration, their work expands from isolated predictions into long trajectories of reasoning, tool use, and feedback. Token efficiency therefore becomes important for scaling recursive self-improvement. We take an RSI-inspired approach at the harness layer, scaling auto-research loops across increasingly numerous and diverse environments for harness rollouts. At this scale, the process yields reusable improvements that transfer beyond their development setting, moving automated harness discovery toward production-level outcomes. Four mechanisms survive selection and form SoL-Pi, spanning action execution, context compaction, observation handling, and delegated reading. On the 51-task EdgeBench evaluation, SoL-Pi achieves performance comparable to Pi across GPT-5.6 Sol and Opus 5 while reducing recorded token traffic by 44.7-49.0% and API cost by about one third. In other words, estimated hourly savings are \$8.75-\$13.50 relative to native Codex and Claude Code harnesses, and \$4.36-\$5.71 relative to Pi.

</details>


### [81] [FreqCondNorm: Towards Cross-domain Predictive Maintenance through a Frequency-Conditioned Transformer Foundation Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20535)
*Zaynab Raounak,Camille LHermine,Zhiguo Zeng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FreqCondNorm is a Transformer-based model with frequency-conditioned normalization for predictive maintenance that improves fault diagnosis across different sampling frequencies but doesn't help with remaining useful life prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning models for predictive maintenance struggle with transferability across different machines and operating conditions, especially when dealing with scarce labeled data and signals that vary in sampling frequency over five orders of magnitude (1 Hz to ~100 kHz).

Method: The authors propose FreqCondNorm, a Transformer-based architecture with a FiLM-style frequency-conditioned normalization layer to handle heterogeneous time-series data. They pretrain the model on five public predictive maintenance datasets (CWRU, MFPT, UOC18, PRONOSTIA, CMAPSS) using masked auto-encoding and contrastive learning with balanced domain sampling.

Result: For fault diagnosis, the model achieves 99.2% accuracy on CWRU (a 6.4 percentage point improvement over CNN) and 82.1% zero-shot accuracy on MFPT, showing strong transfer capability across different sampling frequencies. However, the approach does not improve remaining useful life (RUL) prediction performance.

Conclusion: FreqCondNorm successfully addresses the challenge of handling heterogeneous time-series data with varying sampling frequencies for fault diagnosis, demonstrating strong transfer learning capabilities. However, the mismatch between pretraining objectives and RUL prediction tasks suggests the need for further investigation into aligning pretraining strategies with specific predictive maintenance objectives.

Abstract: Deep learning predictive maintenance models suffer from poor transferability across machines and operating conditions, especially when labelled data are scarce and signals span five orders of magnitude in sampling frequency (1 Hz to ~100 kHz). We propose FreqCondNorm, a Transformer-based architecture that introduces a FiLM-style frequency-conditioned normalization layer to unify heterogeneous time-series within a single model. The architecture is pretrained on five public predictive maintenance datasets (CWRU, MFPT, UOC18, PRONOSTIA, CMAPSS) using masked auto-encoding and contrastive learning with balanced domain sampling. On fault diagnosis, the model achieves 99.2% accuracy on CWRU (+6.4 pp over CNN) and 82.1% zero-shot accuracy on MFPT, demonstrating strong transfer across sampling frequencies. However, the approach does not improve remaining useful life prediction, suggesting a mismatch between pretraining and RUL objectives that warrants future investigation.

</details>


### [82] [Refuse, Decompose, Refresh: A Claim-Safe Protocol for Closed-Loop AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20538)
*Peiying Zhu,Sidi Chang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a claim-safe evaluation protocol for AI systems that emphasizes abstention when evidence is insufficient, decomposition of results, and refreshing reference maps instead of treating distribution shifts as faults.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI evaluations can be perfectly reproducible yet still support wrong claims, especially in closed-loop systems where policies affect what states are visited, what components are observable, and which failures leave measurable traces.

Method: Proposes a three-action protocol: 1) Refuse to evaluate when lacking proper reference data, 2) Decompose reporting into protocol execution, operational false admission, and structural hypotheses instead of single PASS/FAIL labels, 3) Refresh reference maps when distribution shift alarms occur rather than treating them as fault evidence. Implemented in an aggregate-only simulator with 24 policy components across three demand regimes and fault-mask families.

Result: In preregistered heldout tests (1,440 cases, 21,600 partition rows), only 55/72 regime-component units were reference-admitted, with 54/55 remaining runtime-admitted. Zero false admissions for 20 represented components with 95% upper bound of 0.1391. Clean traffic outperformed nominal fault-cell predictions with significant negative-log-likelihood differences. Distribution shift alarms varied dramatically across regimes, showing why null hypotheses must be reference-relative.

Conclusion: Rather than proposing universal thresholds, the paper contributes an executable contract that links observable support, statistical calibration, and justified claims, providing a more rigorous foundation for AI evaluation in closed-loop systems.

Abstract: An AI evaluation can be perfectly reproducible and still support the wrong claim. This risk is acute in closed-loop systems: policy determines visited states, observable components, and which failures leave a measurable trace. We propose a claim-safe protocol with three actions. Refuse: abstain when a clean reference stream or matched runtime comparison lacks support. Decompose: report protocol execution, operational false admission, and structural hypotheses separately rather than as one PASS/FAIL label. Refresh: treat distribution-shift alarms as requests to invalidate and recompute a reference map, not as fault evidence. We instantiate the protocol in an aggregate-only simulator with 24 policy components, three demand regimes, two fault-mask families, and independent development and heldout seeds. The preregistered heldout contains 1,440 cases and 21,600 partition rows. Only 55/72 regime-component units were reference-admitted and 54/55 remained runtime-admitted, making abstention part of the result. Stable false admission was 0/20 represented components, with a one-sided exact 95% upper bound of 0.1391 under a frozen 0.20 rule. Within admitted units, affected clean traffic outpredicted nominal fault-cell fraction: across 540 unit-arm rows nested in 20 component clusters, the cell-minus-traffic negative-log-likelihood difference was 0.1264 nats per row, with a 95% component-cluster interval of [0.0593, 0.1918]. A drift log shows why "null" must be reference-relative: clean fault-null streams triggered 15/15, 0/15, and 14/15 alarms across three regimes, while only the middle regime matched the frozen detector reference. Rather than a universal threshold, we contribute an executable contract linking observable support, statistical calibration, and justified claims.

</details>


### [83] [Limits of Confidence in Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20581)
*Russ Webb,Amitis Shidani,Alice Bizeul,Dan Busbridge*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Discrete diffusion samplers assume token independence, but real domains have dependencies between tokens. The paper shows that per-position distributions cannot capture dependencies, and demonstrates this on synthetic tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discrete diffusion samplers (remasking and uniform-state) generate sequences by writing multiple token positions per step, assuming per-position independence. However, in real domains like pixels, phonemes, or words, tokens have inherent dependencies. This creates a mismatch between the sampling assumptions and actual data distributions.

Method: The paper analyzes theoretical limitations of discrete diffusion samplers, showing that positions can only match training distributions when conditionally independent. Uses ScanAndAdd - a synthetic task with known joint distribution - to empirically verify that groups of two or more positions are dependent, and measures distribution divergence.

Result: On ScanAndAdd task, every group of two or more undetermined positions written by confidence ranking is dependent. The generated distribution shows $29\times$ total variation above sampling-noise floor, while per-sample metrics are $1.0$, indicating significant distribution mismatch despite good per-sample quality.

Conclusion: Discrete diffusion samplers fundamentally cannot capture dependencies between tokens because they use per-position distributions. This creates distributional mismatch issues that are masked by per-sample metrics. The work reveals limitations of current sampling approaches for dependent token domains.

Abstract: Discrete diffusion, including remasking and uniform-state samplers, generate a sequence by writing multiple token positions per step, drawing each from a per-position distribution and choosing which positions to write from those same distributions. For domains of general interest (pixels, phonemes, or words) there are inherent dependencies between tokens. We show that a step matches the training distribution only when the positions it writes are conditionally independent given the tokens already fixed, that no product of per-position distributions can match a dependent group, and that per-position distributions do not determine whether a group is dependent: two joint distributions can have identical per-position marginals while differing in which combinations of values occur. On ScanAndAdd, a synthetic task whose joint distribution is available in closed form, we verify that every group of two or more undetermined positions a confidence ranking writes is dependent, and measure the generated distribution to be $29\times$ the sampling-noise floor total variation while per-sample metrics are $1.0$.

</details>


### [84] [PAA: The Probabilistic Allen Algebra: A Generative and Complete Probabilistic Extension of Allen's Interval Relations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20634)
*Julian Eggert*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Probabilistic Allen Algebra (PAA) extends crisp Allen interval relations to handle uncertainty by deriving relation probabilities from distributions over interval boundaries rather than assigning scores.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard Allen interval algebra is inadequate for real-world temporal information where times, durations, and boundaries are uncertain (from language, perception, databases, etc.), and expressions like "just before" or "roughly during" have graded meanings rather than crisp definitions.

Method: Develops a generative probabilistic extension where time points follow Gaussian distributions, intervals have Gaussian midpoints and truncated-Gaussian durations. Relations become boundary-ordering predicates in a common probability space, with point-point relations reducing to error functions and point-interval/interval-interval relations becoming multivariate Gaussian orthant probabilities induced by linear inequalities.

Result: Creates a complete probabilistic extension where all thirteen Allen relations receive positive measure through tolerance bands, preserving Allen's taxonomy as intervals collapse to points. The algebra is scale-invariant, separates graded expressions from contact relations, and decomposes into correlation-aware temporal primitives. Results are Monte-Carlo validated and implemented as an open Python package.

Conclusion: PAA provides a rigorous probabilistic foundation for handling uncertain temporal relations that matches real-world data characteristics, while preserving and deriving Allen's original crisp taxonomy as a special case when uncertainty vanishes.

Abstract: Allen's interval algebra is a qualitative calculus for temporal relations, but its thirteen base relations are crisp predicates over exact interval boundaries. This is inadequate for temporal information from language, perception, databases, or uncertain histories, where times, durations, and boundaries are uncertain and expressions such as "just before" or "roughly during" have graded meaning. We develop the probabilistic Allen algebra (PAA): a generative and complete extension in which relation probabilities are derived from distributions over interval boundaries rather than assigned as scores. Time points are Gaussian; intervals have Gaussian midpoints and truncated-Gaussian durations. Every relation is a boundary-ordering predicate in one common probability space: point-point relations reduce to error functions, and point-interval and interval-interval relations to multivariate Gaussian orthant probabilities induced by linear inequalities. Contact relations (meets, starts, finishes, equals) receive positive measure through a tolerance band, and under a single tolerance the thirteen relations form a true partition that recovers crisp Allen as the tolerance vanishes. The construction derives Allen's taxonomy rather than positing it: coarse predicates such as precedence, overlap, and containment are unions of leaves whose probabilities are leaf sums, and this hierarchy is preserved as intervals collapse to points and thirteen relations reduce to five and then three. Each relation further decomposes into correlation-aware temporal primitives in the spirit of CIDOC CRM. The algebra is scale-invariant and separates graded expressions such as "shortly before" from contact relations. All results are Monte-Carlo validated and shipped as an open, tested Python package.

</details>


### [85] [Ownership in AI-Assisted Everyday Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20658)
*Megan Wei,Melanie Subbiah,Audrey Lee,Annya Dahmani,Dave Edwards,Helen Edwards,Ellie Pavlick*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: People's sense of ownership over AI-assisted work depends on their role in the collaborative process - they maintain ownership when actively leading/editing, but lose it when just approving AI suggestions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI becomes integrated into daily tasks, we need to understand how it affects people's sense of ownership and contribution when machines help produce what we make.

Method: Exploratory qualitative survey where participants described two recent AI-assisted tasks: one that felt like their own and one that did not.

Result: Ownership depends on collaborative process: active involvement (leading, iterating, rewriting) maintains ownership; passive approval loses it. People can own vision even without execution. Loss of personal voice and output incomprehension erode ownership. Disclosure of AI use is often driven by social norms rather than actual pride.

Conclusion: Need research directions for AI development that supports people's sense of authorship over their lives, addressing how collaboration processes affect ownership feelings.

Abstract: When does work done with AI still feel like ours? As AI becomes woven into everyday tasks, we must examine what happens to our sense of ownership and contribution when a machine shares in producing what we make. We report an exploratory qualitative survey in which participants were asked to describe two recent, self-selected tasks completed with AI: one that felt like their own and one that did not. We find that felt ownership depends on the process of collaboration: people disown work when they merely approve AI's suggestions, but retain ownership when they lead, iterate, or rewrite. Ownership can also extend to settings where people own the vision for a project but not the execution; respondents reported high ownership on tasks they could not have completed without AI. Loss of personal voice and a lack of comprehension of the output both erode ownership. Finally, willingness to disclose AI use is often decoupled from actual pride or ownership, and instead shaped by community norms and fear of credit erasure. We propose several research directions as a result of these findings to promote AI development that supports people's sense of authorship over their own lives.

</details>


### [86] [Deep Noir: Autonomous Steering Discovery via Architectural Chronometry in Transformer Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20722)
*Frank E. Bobe,Gregory D. Vetaw,Darshan W. Bryner,Matthew G. Cook,Jose L. Salas-Vernis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Deep Noir is an automated framework that discovers optimal activation steering parameters for LLMs using Logit Lens convergence and causal attribution, achieving significant improvements on spam detection and sentiment analysis tasks across multiple model architectures and scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current activation steering methods for modifying LLM behavior at inference time require manual identification of where and how strongly to steer, which is inefficient and doesn't scale across different tasks and architectures. There's a need for automated, principled approaches to discover optimal steering parameters.

Method: Deep Noir combines Logit Lens convergence analysis with causal head-level attribution to autonomously discover optimal steering parameters. The framework operates across different model scales (1B, 2-3B, and 7-9B parameters) and architectures, using mechanistic grounding to find intervention points that generalize well.

Result: The method achieves: 1) 16.7 percentage-point improvement on spam detection at 1B models (std 4.7; 39 runs), 2) 21-42 percentage-point improvements at 7-9B across four architectures, 3) 13.1 percentage-point improvement on SST-2 sentiment analysis with zero code changes, 4) Outperforms RepE without head masking which fails to improve over baseline, and 5) Identifies that steering creates a predictable prompt-injection attack surface whose vulnerability increases monotonically with steering magnitude.

Conclusion: Deep Noir demonstrates that automated, mechanistically-grounded discovery of steering parameters is effective and generalizes across tasks and architectures. However, the technique introduces security vulnerabilities via predictable prompt-injection surfaces, which is particularly relevant for agent systems using steered classifiers.

Abstract: Activation steering modifies LLM behavior at inference time, but identifying where and how strongly to steer remains manual. We introduce Deep Noir, a framework that uses Logit Lens convergence and causal head-level attribution to autonomously discover optimal steering parameters. Across three scales (1B x 3, 2-3B x 2, and 7-9B x 4), our engine achieves 16.7 percentage-point improvement on spam at 1B (standard deviation 4.7; 39 runs), with gains increasing to 21 to 42 percentage points at 7-9B across four architectures. On SST-2 sentiment, it achieves a 13.1 percentage-point improvement with zero code changes. Mechanistic grounding enables automated discovery of intervention points that generalize across tasks and architectures. On sentiment, RepE without head masking fails to improve over baseline, while Deep Noir improves all models (p less than 0.01). We further show that steering creates a predictable prompt-injection attack surface whose vulnerability increases monotonically with steering magnitude. This finding is relevant to agent systems deploying steered classifiers.

</details>


### [87] [Q&A on Any Spreadsheet Requires Interpreting Its Grid Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20732)
*Zofia Smoleń*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A novel framework for semantic cell annotation improves spreadsheet chunking for LLM RAG systems, but faces fundamental limitations due to spreadsheets' 2D nature and infinite potential cell roles, suggesting a shift toward dimensionality-reduction techniques is needed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spreadsheets pose challenges for LLM-driven RAG systems because they are fundamentally two-dimensional unstructured data with continuous relationships and infinite potential cell roles, creating a bottleneck in spreadsheet-to-LLM processing.

Method: Proposes a framework that splits spreadsheets into interpretable chunks using semantic cell role annotation, which beats state-of-the-art methods but recognizes inherent limitations of discrete classification approaches.

Result: The framework demonstrates improved interpretability for chunking in LLM RAG systems (aiding answer generation through enriched context), but faces a hard ceiling due to the fundamental limitation that classification models with finite, pre-defined classes cannot perfectly capture spreadsheet structural nuance.

Conclusion: Addressing the spreadsheet-to-LLM bottleneck requires moving beyond discrete cell classification. The field should develop dimensionality-reduction techniques to directly flatten 2D unstructured spreadsheets into 1D unstructured text for easier RAG interpretation and generation.

Abstract: Semantic cell annotation improves chunking interpretability for spreadsheets in LLM-driven RAG systems, aiding answer generation through enriched context rather than improved retrieval accuracy. We propose a novel framework of splitting any spreadsheet into interpretable chunks using cell role annotation. Our framework beats the state of the art, yet it faces a hard ceiling. Spreadsheets are fundamentally two-dimensional unstructured data with continuous relationships and infinite potential cell roles. Because classification models are restricted to finite, pre-defined classes, they cannot perfectly capture this structural nuance, even with human-level annotation. We show that addressing the spreadsheet-to-LLM bottleneck requires moving beyond discrete cell classification. Instead, the field must develop dimensionality-reduction techniques to directly flatten 2D unstructured spreadsheets into 1D unstructured text. Text chunks would be easier for downstream RAG to interpret and generate from.

</details>


### [88] [RAFT: A Stateful Retrieval-Augmented Framework for Troubleshooting Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20754)
*Mingxuan Zhang,Xiaowen Wang,Anupma Sharan,Zhengyi Chen,Chenyu Diana Zhang,Shanshan Yang,Chittibabu Pacharu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RAFT is a stateful RAG framework that treats troubleshooting cases as multi-stage timelines rather than static documents, enabling retrieval of cases whose intermediate states match the active case's progress.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RAG systems for enterprise customer support treat historical cases as static documents, overlooking their multi-stage, stateful nature. This fails to capture how troubleshooting cases evolve through different states and stages over time.

Method: RAFT abstracts each historical case into a directed chain of timeline entries and retrieves at the entry level. It surfaces cases whose intermediate states match the active case and returns the parent-case trajectory anchored at the matched state. An optional case-level graph links cases through configurable similarity representations.

Result: RAFT improves Case Hit over vanilla RAG and GraphRAG baselines at every stage of case progress, with statistically significant gains over the strongest baseline. Evaluation used synthetic benchmark from Microsoft Learn Windows Server documentation and real Apache Jira issues with duplicate labels, showing advantage transfers to real case histories.

Conclusion: RAFT provides a stateful RAG framework that better captures the multi-stage nature of troubleshooting cases, demonstrating improved retrieval performance over existing methods. The approach shows promise for real-world enterprise support systems.

Abstract: Effective troubleshooting agents in enterprise customer support depend on retrieving actionable guidance from similar historical cases, yet existing retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems treat support cases as static documents and overlook their multi-stage, stateful nature. We introduce RAFT (Retrieval-Augmented Framework for Troubleshooting Agents), a stateful RAG framework that abstracts each closed historical case into a directed chain of timeline entries and retrieves at the entry level, surfacing cases whose intermediate states match the active case and returning the parent-case trajectory anchored at the matched state; an optional case-level graph links cases through a configurable similarity representation. We evaluate this retrieval layer directly, which, unlike evaluating a full agent system, requires no production deployment. Because public multi-stage troubleshooting data is extremely rare, we pair a synthetic benchmark built from Microsoft Learn Windows Server documentation with real Apache Jira issues carrying human-created duplicate labels. RAFT improves Case Hit over vanilla RAG and GraphRAG baselines at every stage of case progress, with statistically significant gains over the strongest baseline; the Jira results provide directional evidence that the advantage transfers to real case histories. We release our benchmark, implementation, and the Apache Jira evaluation set.

</details>


### [89] [An Empirical Study of Harness Design for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20804)
*Run-Ze Fan,Zihao Zhang,Simin Ma,Yebowen Hu,Shouju Wang,Kaiqiang Song,Fei Liu,Hamed Zamani,Xiaoyang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes individual components of coding harnesses for autonomous coding agents, evaluating how planning, action space, and context management affect performance across different models and tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing work evaluates coding harnesses as monolithic systems, leaving the effectiveness of individual components unclear. The authors aim to enable component-level comparisons to understand how different harness components affect autonomous coding agent performance.

Method: Used a lightweight coding harness with fixed execution loop while varying three components: planning, action space, and context management. Evaluated 176 matched settings across four models on SWE-Bench Verified and Terminal-Bench 2.1, spanning five context-management strategies, four context-window budgets, and targeted ablations.

Result: Key findings: (1) Context management is most valuable with tight context-window budgets, mainly preventing context-overflow failures. (2) Staging rule-based elision before LLM-based summarization provides strongest efficiency. (3) Planning shifts from accuracy scaffold for weaker models to cost saver for stronger models. (4) Predefined tools help weaker bash models, while bash-capable models perform better with bash-only interfaces. Trajectory analysis shows how each component affects agent behavior differently.

Conclusion: The findings provide insights for model- and budget-aware harness design and establish a modular framework for evaluating future harness components, enabling more targeted improvements to autonomous coding systems.

Abstract: Coding harnesses shape how autonomous coding agents translate model capabilities into long-horizon software-engineering performance, yet existing work typically evaluates harnesses as monolithic systems, leaving the effectiveness of individual components unclear. To enable component-level comparisons, we study this question with a lightweight coding harness whose execution loop is fixed while three components are varied: planning, action space, and context management. Across four models evaluated on SWE-Bench Verified and Terminal-Bench 2.1, we evaluate 176 matched settings spanning five context-management strategies, four context-window budgets, and targeted ablations of planning and action space. We find that: (1) Context management becomes increasingly valuable as the context-window budget tightens, with most of its benefit coming from preventing context-overflow failures. (2) Staging rule-based elision before LLM-based summarization provides the strongest overall efficiency among the context-management strategies, whereas making elided content recoverable adds machinery that models rarely use and yields no accuracy gain. (3) Planning shifts from an accuracy scaffold for weaker models to a cost saver for stronger models, with little change in accuracy. (4) Predefined tools improve performance for models with weaker bash proficiency, whereas bash-capable models can operate effectively with a bash-only interface and achieve substantially lower cost, especially on command-line-centric tasks. Trajectory-level analysis explains these effects: context management extends execution trajectories without substantially altering agent behavior, planning changes where trajectories stop, and the action space changes the granularity at which code is written. These findings inform model- and budget-aware harness design and provide a modular framework for evaluating future harness components.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [90] [Generative Query Suggestion via Intent Coverage and Query-Level Credit Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19209)
*Xinpeng Liu,Lu Ma,Jiayi Qiao,Mengyu Zhou,Linglong Li,Xiaofeng Bian,Haonan Chen,Xiaoxi Jiang,Guanjun Jiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A dual-stage optimization framework for generative query suggestion that improves both individual query quality and slate diversity by modeling user intents and using intent-aware rewards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current query suggestion systems need to generate slates where individual queries are useful while the overall slate covers diverse user intents, which is challenging to achieve simultaneously.

Method: Proposes an Intent-Driven Query Suggestion Framework with: 1) Intent-aware diversity modeling using intent-aligned supervised fine-tuning data and an Intent-Aware Diversity Reward for intent coverage; 2) Query-level credit assignment that routes individual quality signals to corresponding query tokens while sharing slate-level diversity signals across the slate.

Result: Experiments on a large-scale production dataset showed improvements in click-through rate, query quality, and intent coverage, validated through both online A/B testing and offline evaluation.

Conclusion: The proposed dual-stage optimization framework effectively addresses the challenge of generating query suggestions that balance individual query usefulness with diverse intent coverage, leading to improved user engagement metrics.

Abstract: Generative query suggestion aims to enhance user engagement by anticipating user intents and recommending relevant follow-up queries. A central challenge is to generate slates whose individual queries are useful while the slate covers distinct intents. We propose an Intent-Driven Query Suggestion Framework with dual-stage optimization. First, intent-aware diversity modeling constructs intent-aligned supervised fine-tuning (SFT) data and uses an Intent-Aware Diversity Reward to optimize intent coverage. Second, query-level credit assignment routes individual quality signals to the corresponding query tokens while sharing a slate-level diversity signal across the slate. Experiments on a large-scale production dataset, including online A/B testing and offline evaluation, show improvements in click-through rate, query quality, and intent coverage.

</details>


### [91] [Layer-wise Curriculum Learning for Efficient LLM Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19213)
*Donggeon Lee,Dooyeon Na,Seungmin Oh,Jongbin Ryu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Layer-wise curriculum learning for efficient LLM compression, using progressive knowledge transfer from teacher to student models with computational efficiency improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM compression methods need better computational efficiency and stability during knowledge transfer. Current approaches face issues with cumulative errors, feature misalignment across layers, and high GPU memory/training time requirements.

Method: 1) Partition LLM into multiple layer segments for computationally efficient knowledge transfer. 2) Use curriculum learning starting with easier optimization tasks and progressively tackling harder ones. 3) Theoretical analysis of cumulative error phenomenon to guide the approach. 4) Feature caching with multi-threading strategy to address feature misalignment across layers and maximize GPU utilization.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance on multiple datasets. Reduces GPU memory usage and training hours by >50% on BERT and GPT-2. Outperforms other pruning methods on LLaMA-family and Qwen models with same training hours but lower GPU memory footprint.

Conclusion: Layer-wise curriculum learning provides an effective framework for efficient LLM compression with superior computational efficiency, stability in knowledge transfer, and excellent performance across various model families.

Abstract: In this paper, we introduce layer-wise curriculum learning for efficient LLM compression. The proposed method facilitates the knowledge transfer from the teacher model to the student model, utilizing a curriculum learning approach that begins with easier optimization tasks and progressively tackles harder ones. In order to adopt the layer-wise learning in LLM compression, we partition the whole model into multiple segments consisting of layers, thereby enabling more computationally efficient knowledge transfer for LLMs. Based on our theoretical analysis of cumulative error phenomenon, layer-wise curriculum learning accelerates convergence while stabilizing the knowledge transfer process. In addition, we present a feature caching method with a multi-threading strategy to efficiently address feature misalignment across layers, maximizing GPU utilization. Consequently, our method exhibits advanced model compression performance, as well as high computational efficiency in terms of minimized memory usage and short training hours. Experiments on multiple datasets show that the proposed method achieves state-of-the-art performance while reducing GPU memory usage and training hours by more than 50\% on BERT and GPT-2. Moreover, it outperforms the other pruning methods on LLaMA-family and Qwen models under the same training hours, with a lower GPU memory footprint.

</details>


### [92] [Block Parallelism For Efficient Distributed Long-Context Diffusion Language Model Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19242)
*Tarun Suresh,Pranshu Chaturvedi,Hangoo Kang,Parth Shroff,Ishan S. Khare,Hermann Kumbong,Azalia Mirhoseini*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Block Parallelism (BP) is a new distributed training method for Block Diffusion Language Models that improves throughput for long-context training by sharding corrupted blocks across ranks while sharing clean sequences, achieving up to 7.59x speedup.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current context parallelism (CP) for Block Diffusion Language Models suffers from communication overhead and memory constraints when training on long contexts, as it needs to communicate both clean and corrupted key/value tensors and their gradients across devices.

Method: Proposes Block Parallelism (BP) that assigns each corrupted-block computation to one rank, and Context-Sharded Block Parallelism (CSBP) that also shards the shared clean sequence across ranks, keeping corrupted K/V and gradients local while avoiding replicated clean prefixes.

Result: On 16 H200 GPUs at 256K context, CSBP improves throughput by 1.18-1.45x for SFT and 1.27-1.33x for autoregressive model conversion. On eight H100 GPUs, accelerates DFlash2 training by 2.48x at 512K and 7.59x at 1M. Achieves higher pass rates in 12-hour DiffusionGemma 26B-A4B SFT runs.

Conclusion: CSBP is an efficient distributed training method for Block Diffusion Language Models that significantly improves training throughput for long contexts while reducing communication and memory overhead, enabling better scaling and performance on benchmark tasks.

Abstract: Block diffusion language models (BDLMs) combine autoregressive dependencies across blocks with parallel denoising within blocks, but long-context training is constrained by distributed attention communication and activation memory. Conventional context parallelism (CP) shards the combined clean-plus-corrupted sequence by position, communicating shared clean K/V together with block-specific corrupted K/V and their gradients. We observe that the BDLM objective separates over target blocks. We introduce block parallelism (BP), a new distributed parallelism dimension that assigns each corrupted-block computation to one rank. To scale BP to long contexts, we introduce context-sharded block parallelism (CSBP), which also shards the shared clean sequence across those ranks. CSBP keeps corrupted K/V and gradients local, avoids replicated clean prefixes, and preserves BDLM training semantics. On 16 H200 GPUs at 256K context, CSBP improves throughput over the best baseline by 1.18-1.45x for supervised fine-tuning and 1.27-1.33x for conversion of autoregressive models to BDLMs, while matching or reducing peak HBM. Full-model speedup reaches 1.61x at 512K. On eight H100 GPUs, CSBP accelerates DFlash2 speculative-decoder training by 2.48x at 512K and 7.59x at 1M. In matched 12-hour DiffusionGemma 26B-A4B SFT runs, CSBP achieves higher pass rates at every trained checkpoint on SWE-bench Verified and Terminal-Bench Lite. Code: https://github.com/ScalingIntelligence/Turbo-dLLM

</details>


### [93] [Randomized SVD Approximations for Spectral Co-Clustering of Word-Document Matrices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19243)
*Fateme Mazdarani,Carlos Toxtli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Randomized approximations for spectral co-clustering reduce computational costs with different trade-offs based on matrix sparsity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard spectral co-clustering using SVD is computationally expensive for high-dimensional word-document matrices, and existing methods assume equal numbers of document and word clusters, which may not reflect real-world data structure.

Method: Two randomized approximations: 1) Random projection method using randomized SVD, and 2) Combined method using partial SVD with element-wise random sampling. Both handle cases where numbers of document and word clusters differ.

Result: Both methods reduce runtime compared to full-SVD baseline. Random projection method is more reliable across settings, while sampling-based method works best on denser matrices and provides limited benefit on already sparse text data.

Conclusion: Randomized approximations for spectral co-clustering should be selected according to the underlying data structure and sparsity, with different methods optimized for different matrix characteristics.

Abstract: Spectral co-clustering is a useful tool for discovering latent structure in word-document matrices, but its reliance on singular value decomposition (SVD) can make standard formulations expensive on high-dimensional data. This paper presents two randomized approximations for normalized spectral co-clustering of bipartite text data when the numbers of document and word clusters may differ. The first method uses randomized SVD through random projection, while the second combines partial SVD with element-wise random sampling. Across real-world and synthetic datasets, both methods reduce runtime relative to the full-SVD baseline, but their behavior depends on matrix sparsity. The random projection method is the more reliable approximation across the tested settings, whereas the sampling-based method is most useful on denser matrices and provides limited benefit on already sparse text data. These results show that randomized approximations for spectral co-clustering should be selected according to the underlying structure of the data.

</details>


### [94] [Radio-Frequency Convolutional Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19279)
*Zhihui Gao,Shi-Yuan Ma,Yiran Chen,Dirk Englund,Tingjun Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RF-CNNs repurpose existing wireless radio hardware (frequency mixers) to perform neural network convolution operations, enabling efficient AI inference on edge devices without additional compute hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Edge devices have limited computing capabilities for running modern neural networks. Adding dedicated AI accelerators increases SWaP-C (size, weight, power, cost). The authors identified that these devices already contain hardware capable of mathematical operations needed for AI - specifically, the frequency mixer in wireless radios can perform convolution operations.

Method: Radio-frequency convolutional neural networks (RF-CNNs) map multi-channel convolutions onto frequency tones. The existing communication hardware (passive mixer) is repurposed to execute convolutions in a single pass in the frequency domain. Weights are transmitted over the air, and the analog hardware is shared with communication functions.

Result: Experimental demonstration shows RF-CNNs can run deep CNNs up to 26.4 million parameters and nine layers for various tasks including wireless signal classification, image classification, and controllable image generation. Achieved near full-precision performance with energy consumption down to 0.72 femtojoules per multiply-accumulate operation, which is two orders of magnitude less than digital processors.

Conclusion: Existing wireless infrastructure can enable efficient, state-of-the-art AI inference on billions of connected devices by repurposing communication hardware rather than adding dedicated compute units. This approach addresses the SWaP-C constraints of edge devices while maintaining performance.

Abstract: Running artificial intelligence (AI) models directly on edge devices such as smartphones, wearables, and drones offers low latency, pervasive scalability, and data privacy, but these devices rarely carry the computing capability that modern neural networks demand. Edge accelerators have been developed in response, yet each adds computing hardware to devices already constrained in size, weight, power, and cost (SWaP-C). An alternative lies in what these devices already carry: the frequency mixer in every wireless radio multiplies signals in time, natively performing convolution in the frequency domain. Here we introduce radio-frequency convolutional neural networks (RF-CNNs), which repurpose existing communication hardware for CNN inference. Multi-channel convolutions are mapped onto frequency tones for a passive mixer to execute in a single pass. We experimentally demonstrate that RF-CNN runs deep CNNs up to 26.4 million parameters and nine layers from classification of wireless signals and images to controllable image generation, close to full-precision performance. Because the weights arrive over the air and the analog hardware is shared with communication, the edge device spends energy only on data preparation and readout-down to 0.72 femtojoules per multiply-accumulate, two orders of magnitude less than it would cost on an added digital processor. These results suggest that deployed wireless infrastructure can bring efficient, state-of-the-art AI inference to the billions of devices it already connects.

</details>


### [95] [Learning-Induced Dynamical Transition in Recurrent Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19288)
*Varun Vaidya*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A dynamical mean-field theory (DMFT) is developed to describe how recurrent neural networks transition from chaotic to stable dynamics during learning, with slow feedback-driven learning creating an evolving effective feedback strength that drives this transition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning in recurrent neural networks fundamentally reshapes their dynamics, transforming initially chaotic activity into stable task-dependent behavior. The paper aims to theoretically describe this transition during the learning process.

Method: Develops a non-equilibrium dynamical mean-field theory (DMFT) to analyze the transition. Derives the two-time correlation function throughout learning and identifies critical feedback strength and learning rate dependent critical time separating chaotic and stable regimes.

Result: Shows that slow feedback-driven learning generates an evolving effective feedback strength that drives the network through a transition from chaotic to stable dynamics. Identifies a critical feedback strength and learning rate dependent critical time. The theory predicts time evolution of network output during training with quantitative agreement with numerical simulations.

Conclusion: The transition arises from progressive deformation of an effective dynamical landscape by the growing learned feedback structure. The DMFT framework successfully describes how learning transforms chaotic dynamics into stable task-dependent behavior in recurrent neural networks.

Abstract: Learning in recurrent neural networks can fundamentally reshape their underlying dynamics, transforming initially chaotic activity into stable task-dependent behavior. We develop a non-equilibrium dynamical mean-field theory(DMFT) to describe this transition during learning. We show that a slow feedback-driven learning process generates an evolving effective feedback strength that drives the network through a transition from chaotic to stable dynamics defined by a bifurcation of the DMFT solution. By deriving the two-time correlation function throughout learning, we identify a critical feedback strength and a corresponding learning rate dependent critical time separating these regimes. The transition arises from the progressive deformation of an effective dynamical landscape by the growing learned feedback structure. Starting from the untrained state, the theory predicts the time evolution of the network output during training and shows quantitative agreement with numerical simulations.

</details>


### [96] [Personalized Federated Hierarchical Gaussian Processes for Privacy-Preserving Modeling of Heterogeneous Distributed Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19337)
*Xianjian Xie,Hao Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: pFedHGP is a personalized federated learning framework using hierarchical Gaussian processes for probabilistic regression/classification on heterogeneous distributed data, with applications in industrial monitoring and environmental modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need for probabilistic federated learning with personalized models when data is distributed across heterogeneous clients, requiring methods that keep raw data local while enabling collaboration and supporting uncertainty-aware decisions.

Method: Hierarchical Gaussian Process decomposition: each client's function splits into (1) shared global component, (2) client-specific deviation with same kernel structure, (3) flexible local residual. Uses sparse inducing-variable approximations and federated variational inference to synchronize only low-dimensional statistics for shared component while keeping raw data local.

Result: Achieves perfect fault classification in press tonnage monitoring using only 13.77% of labeled cycles, and successfully recovers geographic zones in federated air-quality modeling without centralizing station-level time series.

Conclusion: pFedHGP enables effective probabilistic federated learning with personalized models, supports uncertainty quantification, and demonstrates practical value in industrial monitoring and environmental applications through efficient collaborative learning without data centralization.

Abstract: We present Personalized Federated Hierarchical Gaussian Processes (pFedHGP) for probabilistic regression and classification when data are distributed across heterogeneous clients. Each client's latent function decomposes into (i) a shared global component, (ii) a client-specific deviation that shares the global kernel structure, and (iii) a flexible local residual. Sparse inducing-variable approximations and federated variational inference keep raw data local while the server synchronizes only low-dimensional statistics for the shared component. Full predictive distributions support uncertainty-aware decisions. In application studies, pFedHGP attains perfect fault classification in press tonnage monitoring using 13.77% of labeled cycles and recovers geographic zones in federated air-quality modeling without centralizing station-level time series. An Instantaneous Linear Mixing Model viewpoint links the hierarchy to multi-output Gaussian processes for correlated sensors.

</details>


### [97] [How to Guide Your Language Flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19356)
*Rohit Dilip,Tianrong Chen,Yuyang Wang,David Van Valen,Joshua Susskind,Miguel Angel Bautista*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Probe guidance improves flow matching models by using frozen diffusion model states to create guidance signals, eliminating extra inference passes and setting new SOTA for unconditional generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current autoguidance methods for diffusion models require additional forward passes at inference time and the relationship between weak and strong model dynamics is unclear. The authors want to improve guidance efficiency while better understanding the mechanisms behind autoguidance.

Method: Probe guidance uses the frozen internal states of an existing diffusion model to construct guidance signals. Unlike autoguidance, it eliminates the need for extra forward passes at inference and ensures weak and strong models share similar dynamics.

Result: Probe guidance sets new state-of-the-art performance on unconditional generation for continuous diffusion language models. When applied to a 1.7B diffusion language model, it consistently improves performance on multiple choice question answering benchmarks. The method also reveals that in traditional autoguidance, the weak model must come from a low-entropy region of training.

Conclusion: Probe guidance provides both a practical method to enhance diffusion language models and insights into the poorly understood mechanisms of autoguidance, showing that effective guidance requires weak models from low-entropy training regions.

Abstract: We introduce a new method to guide flow matching models. Our approach, which we call probe guidance, uses the frozen internal states of an existing diffusion model to construct a guidance signal. This works using a similar principle as autoguidance, but eliminates the need for an additional forward pass at inference time and provides a reliable path to ensure that the weak and strong model share similar dynamics. We apply and benchmark this method on continuous diffusion language models, where probe guidance sets a new state-of-the-art performance on unconditional generation. When applied to a 1.7B diffusion language model, probe guidance consistently improves on multiple choice question answering benchmarks. Using our probes, we study the traditional autoguidance setting where the strong model is a weak checkpoint, and find that the weak model must come from a low-entropy region of training. These findings both provide a practical way to improve diffusion language models and shed light on the actual mechanism behind autoguidance, which is currently poorly understood.

</details>


### [98] [Smart Insole Human Activity Recognition for Continuous Monitoring in Elderly Care](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19359)
*Edwin Rios,Antony Garcia,Fengpei Yuan,Xinming Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A wireless smart insole system uses plantar pressure and IMU data with machine learning to accurately recognize activities like sitting, standing, walking, and unstable walking, achieving high accuracy with Histogram-Based Gradient Boosting for fall prevention in elderly care.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Falls in older adults are often preceded by changes in mobility, balance, and postural transitions, creating a need for systems that can monitor these activities to enable fall prevention.

Method: Developed wireless smart insoles with 16 pressure sensors and 6D IMUs collecting data at 80Hz. Used overlapping window segmentation and participant-independent stratified group cross-validation. Tested Histogram-Based Gradient Boosting (HGB) and 1D-CNN models.

Result: HGB achieved macro-F1 scores of 0.954 (left foot), 0.959 (right foot), and 0.980 with bilateral sensing. The 1D-CNN did not significantly outperform HGB (p=0.0625).

Conclusion: Low-profile footwear sensing can accurately infer activity states from pressure and IMU measurements for unseen participants, establishing a basis for activity monitoring and fall prevention in elderly care.

Abstract: Falls in older adults are often preceded by changes in mobility, balance, and postural transitions. This paper presents a wireless smart insole platform and machine-learning workflow for recognizing sitting, standing, walking, and unstable walking from plantar-pressure and inertial signals. Each insole integrates 16 active pressure-sensing locations and a six-dimensional IMU stream consisting of tri-axial acceleration and angular velocity. Data were collected from 15 healthy adults at 80~Hz and segmented into overlapping windows. Window length and candidate model families were first screened with stratified 10-fold cross-validation; the primary performance estimate was then obtained with participant-independent 5-fold Stratified Group cross-validation, ensuring that all windows from a participant remained in a single fold. Under this protocol, Histogram-Based Gradient Boosting (HGB) achieved macro-F1 scores of 0.954 and 0.959 for the left and right feet, respectively, and 0.980 with bilateral sensing. A compact 1D-CNN evaluated with the same participant-independent folds did not significantly outperform HGB ($p=0.0625$). The results show that low-profile footwear sensing can infer activity state from pressure and IMU measurements for participants unseen during training, establishing a basis for activity monitoring and fall prevention in elderly care.

</details>


### [99] [Stiefel Attention: When the Geometry of Transformer Projection Matrices Dominates Optimizer Choice---and When It Does Not](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19363)
*Rubén Darío Guerrero*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces Stiefel manifold optimization for attention query/key projections using a Riemannian Adam optimizer with trust regions and polar retractions, showing improved training stability and performance on modular arithmetic and CIFAR-10 tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard attention mechanisms train query and key projections with Euclidean optimizers without geometric constraints, which can lead to unstable training dynamics and poor generalization. The authors aim to constrain these projections to the Stiefel manifold to preserve their geometric structure throughout training.

Method: Constrains query and key projection matrices W_Q and W_K to the Stiefel manifold St(d,r), optimizes them using a Riemannian Adam optimizer with trust region constraints and polar retractions. The method includes four key properties: steepest descent in embedded metric, gradient scale independence, well-conditioning, and exact O(d)-equivariance.

Result: Significant improvements on two tasks: 97.0% vs 61.1% validation accuracy on modular arithmetic grokking at epoch 20,000, and +8.98 percentage points improvement on CIFAR-10 patches (12/12 paired starts with t=60.6). Key finding: step scale freedom accounts for all gains, not the projector or equivariance properties.

Conclusion: Optimizing attention query/key projections on the Stiefel manifold with Riemannian methods stabilizes training by preventing weight decay from collapsing the learned attention geometry. The primary benefit comes from making the optimization step scale-free rather than from the specific projection or equivariance properties.

Abstract: The query and key projections $\WQ,\WK$ in attention are almost always trained by Euclidean optimizers with no constraint on their geometry. We constrain them to the Stiefel manifold and optimize them there with a Riemannian Adam that carries one scalar second moment per frame, caps its step by a trust region, and retracts polarly. Four propositions prove this update is steepest descent in the embedded metric, independent of gradient scale, well conditioned, and exactly $\mathrm{O}(d)$-equivariant, each certified numerically in \texttt{float64}. A fifth supplies the mechanism: weight decay has \emph{identically zero} Riemannian gradient on $\St(d,r)$, since $W = W I_r$ lies in the normal space, so the learned attention geometry survives the collapse cycles that decay drives through the rest of the model. On modular arithmetic grokking, a single run holds $97.0\%$ validation accuracy at epoch 20\,000 against the baseline's $61.1\%$---an unstable endpoint we report as evidence for the mechanism rather than as an effect size. On CIFAR-10 patches the same rule gains $\mathbf{+8.98}$\,pp over 12 paired starts ($t{=}60.6$, $12/12$), and the gap widens with data rather than eroding. The step rule earns this: a fixed-step Riemannian update is degree one in the gradient, so it moves $24$--$40\times$ less per step than an identically shaped AdamW matrix---its frames barely leave their initialization, and freezing them outright costs only $0.28$\,pp. An ablation credits the whole gain to making the step scale free, and nothing measurable to the projector or to equivariance. A negative result sharpens the account: gauge removal cannot motivate the method, because a direction along which the loss is invariant carries no gradient at all.

</details>


### [100] [Machine-Learning Assessment of the Predictive Value of Inflammatory Biomarkers for Cognitive Impairment in an Older Hispanic Adult Cohort](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19374)
*Antony Garcia,Gabrielle Britton,Alcibiades Villarreal,Diana Oviedo,Giselle Rangel,Xinming Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Small clinical tabular dataset study shows I-309/CCL1 chemokine is the only reliable incremental predictor of cognitive impairment among 18 candidate markers, using an interpretable threshold-likelihood Naive Bayes classifier with rigorous cross-validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Small clinical datasets require interpretable ML models since deep learning is impractical and ensemble models are hard to inspect. Statistical significance doesn't always imply predictive utility, so robust methods are needed.

Method: Used a leakage-safe threshold-likelihood Bernoulli/Categorical Naive Bayes classifier on PARI-HD cohort data (n=165). Continuous predictors reduced to supervised chi-square-derived states, income modeled categorically. All data-dependent steps performed within repeated stratified 10-fold cross-validation (30 repeats). Pre-specified primary analysis and exploratory analysis of 18 markers with Benjamini-Hochberg correction.

Result: Demographic baseline achieved ROC-AUC 0.630 ± 0.017. I-309 (CCL1) increased AUC by 0.110 as dominant incremental feature. DeLong tests significant in 100% of repeats. Primary analysis: fixed-partition DeLong p=0.0018, median p=0.0011 across 200 random partitions. Among 18 markers, only I-309 achieved Benjamini-Hochberg-adjusted q=0.032 (significant in 85% of random partitions), no other markers showed reliable predictive value.

Conclusion: I-309/CCL1 is an interpretable candidate feature for tabular prediction of cognitive impairment, with model inspectability providing transparency through thresholds and class-conditional probabilities. Results support external validation.

Abstract: Small clinical tabular datasets require interpretable machine learning because deep learning is often impractical and ensemble models can be difficult to inspect. A key pitfall is that statistical significance does not necessarily imply predictive utility. Using data from the Panama Aging Research Initiative--Health Disparities (PARI-HD) cohort (n=165), we implemented a leakage-safe threshold-likelihood Bernoulli/Categorical Naive Bayes (BNB/CNB) classifier. Within every training fold, each continuous predictor was reduced to a supervised chi-square-derived state, while income entered the model through a categorical likelihood. All data-dependent steps were performed within repeated stratified 10-fold cross-validation with 30 repeats. The demographic baseline achieved a ROC-AUC of 0.630 +/- 0.017. I-309 (CCL1) was the dominant incremental feature, increasing AUC by 0.110, with paired DeLong tests yielding p<0.05 in 100% of repeats. In the pre-specified primary analysis, I-309 produced a fixed-partition DeLong p=0.0018, with robustness assessed across 200 random partitions, where the median p-value was 0.0011. Within the exploratory family of 18 candidate markers, I-309 achieved a Benjamini-Hochberg-adjusted q=0.032 on the frozen partition and satisfied q<0.05 in 85% of random partitions, whereas no other marker demonstrated reliable incremental predictive value. Because the fitted model is an inspectable table of thresholds and class-conditional probabilities, these results identify I-309/CCL1 as an interpretable candidate feature for tabular prediction of cognitive impairment, pending external validation.

</details>


### [101] [COIN-GP: Cooperative Online Learning in Networked Distributed Systems with Partial Measurements via Gaussian Process Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20598)
*Zewen Yang,Xiaobing Dai,Zhenxiao Yin,Hang Zhao,Zhijun Li,C. C. Chan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A distributed observer-based framework using Gaussian Process regression for joint state estimation and dynamic model learning with partial observations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addressing the challenge of jointly estimating system states and partially unknown dynamics in distributed sensor networks with incomplete measurements, where existing methods struggle with partial observability and model deficiencies.

Method: Proposes an observer-based dynamic cooperative learning framework combining online distributed Gaussian Process (GP) regression for handling incomplete measurements and deficient models, plus a novel data collection strategy with theoretical feasibility guarantees.

Result: Derived theoretical error upper bound for both state and model estimation using deterministic GP error bounds, and demonstrated through empirical simulations that the approach outperforms existing distributed GP-based methods.

Conclusion: The proposed framework effectively solves the joint estimation problem in distributed networks with partial observations, providing theoretical guarantees and practical superiority over existing methods.

Abstract: In this paper, we tackle the problem of jointly estimating the system states and partially unknown dynamics within distributed sensor-equipped networks, particularly in scenarios where only partial state observations are available. To address this issue, we propose an observer-based dynamic cooperative learning framework incorporating online distributed Gaussian Process (GP) regression, which enables accurate estimation despite incomplete in measurements and deficient GP models. In addition, a novel data collection strategy is introduced, with theoretical conditions ensuring feasible data acquisition. Moreover, we also derive an error upper bound encompassing state estimation and model estimation, leveraging the deterministic error bounds of GPs. Empirical simulations demonstrate the superiority of our approach compared to existing distributed GP-based methods.

</details>


### [102] [FCx: An algorithm for finding Feasible Counterfactual Explanations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19383)
*Kleopatra Markou,Vana Kalogeraki,Dimitrios Gunopulos*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FCx: A novel method that generates counterfactual explanations that are not only realistic and low-cost but also explicitly enforce feasibility constraints, combining domain knowledge and causal inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing counterfactual explanation methods often fail to ensure feasibility, suggesting modifications that are non-constructive or incompatible with future changes (like changing immutable characteristics such as race), limiting their practical utility.

Method: FCx uses a modified Variational Autoencoder (VAE) with multi-factor loss optimization that accommodates both hard constraints (user-specified domain knowledge) and soft constraints (inferred via causal inference from datasets), while measuring costs based on proximity and sparsity.

Result: Extensive experiments on four public datasets show that FCx matches state-of-the-art performance across multiple metrics while guaranteeing feasibility, making it the first method to efficiently generate CFs that are realistic, low-cost, and feasible.

Conclusion: The proposed FCx method successfully addresses the feasibility gap in counterfactual explanations by incorporating both explicit domain constraints and automatically inferred causal constraints, providing a more practical and trustworthy explanation framework for real-world applications.

Abstract: Counterfactual (CF) explanations identify changes that alter an input's classification.
