While existing methods produce realistic and low-cost CFs, they often fail to ensure feasibility, by suggesting non-constructive modifications or incompatible with future changes (e.g., changing an individual's race to secure a job offer). We introduce a refinement of CF explanations that explicitly enforces feasibility. Our approach is the first to efficiently generate CFs that are realistic, low-cost and feasible. We accommodate both hard feasible constraints, specified by domain knowledge users, and soft feasible constraints, inferred automatically via causal inference from the dataset. Our method, Feasible Counterfactual Explanations (FCx), is based on a modified Variational Autoencoder (VAE) optimized with a multi-factor loss function. We measure the cost of a change based on the absolute change in values (proximity) as well as the number of features changed (sparsity) while realism is measured based on the LOF for density estimation, guaranteeing that CFs reside in densely populated regions. Extensive experiments on four public datasets show that our approach matches state-of-the-art performance across multiple metrics while guaranteeing feasibility.

</details>


### [103] [Improving Offline Goal-Conditioned Reinforcement Learning via Selective Reward Stimulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19414)
*Jing Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RSIQL improves offline goal-conditioned RL by adding intermediate reward signals to address sparse rewards and long-horizon problems, achieving competitive performance with simpler flat policy structure than hierarchical methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Goal-conditioned RL faces challenges in offline settings with sparse rewards and long-horizon dependencies, where goal-completion information is temporally distant from early decisions, and offline value estimation introduces additional errors.

Method: Proposes Reward Stimulation Implicit Q-Learning (RSIQL), a non-hierarchical method that introduces additional reward signals at progress-making intermediate states. Uses auxiliary goal-conditioned value function to identify intermediate states making progress toward goals and applies reward stimulation for less-delayed training supervision.

Result: RSIQL improves over goal-conditioned IQL on average and achieves performance competitive with hierarchical offline goal-conditioned methods on D4RL goal-reaching benchmarks and OGBench.

Conclusion: Reward stimulation effectively addresses delayed-goal problems in offline RL, providing competitive performance with hierarchical methods while maintaining simpler flat policy structure.

Abstract: Goal-conditioned reinforcement learning aims to learn policies that reach specified goals, but remains challenging in offline settings with sparse rewards and long-horizon dependencies. In such settings, goal-completion information can be temporally distant from the early decisions that enable success, while offline value estimation introduces additional error. We study this issue from a reward-propagation perspective and show, in a stylized delayed-goal setting, how goal-directed value separation can become small relative to local estimation error. Motivated by this analysis, we propose Reward Stimulation Implicit Q-Learning (RSIQL), a simple non-hierarchical method that introduces additional reward signals at progress-making intermediate states in offline trajectories. RSIQL uses an auxiliary goal-conditioned value function to identify intermediate states estimated to make progress toward the goal and applies reward stimulation to provide less-delayed training supervision. Unlike hierarchical methods, RSIQL does not learn a separate high-level subgoal policy. Experiments on D4RL goal-reaching benchmarks and OGBench show that RSIQL improves over goal-conditioned IQL on average and achieves performance competitive with hierarchical offline goal-conditioned methods, while retaining a simple flat policy structure.

</details>


### [104] [Bayesian Optimization with Rich Auxiliary Information via LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19437)
*Tejus Gupta,Efe Mert Karagözlü,Rohit Sonker,Barnabás Póczos,Jeff Schnieder*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Large language models can effectively leverage rich auxiliary information (like training curves, expert notes, images) to improve Bayesian Optimization performance across hyperparameter tuning and nuclear fusion optimization tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many real-world optimization problems contain richer information beyond simple function evaluations, such as training curves in hyperparameter optimization, expert notes and images in scientific experimentation, and prior knowledge about optima locations. Standard Bayesian Optimization (BO) doesn't fully utilize this auxiliary information.

Method: Developed three methods for incorporating auxiliary information into Bayesian Optimization using large language models (LLMs). The methods leverage LLMs' ability to process rich, diverse information types to guide the optimization process more effectively than standard BO approaches.

Result: Across hyperparameter optimization benchmarks and a real-world nuclear fusion optimization task, the LLM-enhanced methods consistently outperformed both standard Bayesian Optimization and existing LLM-based optimization approaches.

Conclusion: Large language models are effective tools for leveraging rich auxiliary information in Bayesian Optimization, demonstrating superior performance in practical optimization tasks that involve complex, multi-modal information beyond simple function evaluations.

Abstract: Bayesian Optimization (BO) is widely used for optimizing expensive black-box functions, yet many real-world optimization problems contain substantially richer information than function evaluations alone. Examples include training curves in hyperparameter optimization, expert notes and images in scientific experimentation, and prior knowledge about where optima may lie. We show that large language models (LLMs) can effectively leverage such rich auxiliary information to guide optimization. Motivated by these findings, we develop three methods for incorporating auxiliary information into BO using LLMs. Across hyperparameter optimization benchmarks and a real-world nuclear fusion optimization task, our methods consistently outperform both standard BO and existing LLM-based optimization approaches. Our results demonstrate the effectiveness of LLMs for leveraging rich auxiliary information in BO.

</details>


### [105] [Sharpness-Aware Minimization (SAM) Improves Classification Accuracy of Bacterial Raman Spectral Data Enabling Portable Diagnostics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19453)
*Kaitlin Zareno,Jarett Dewbury,Siamak K. Sorooshyari,Hossein Mobahi,Loza F. Tadesse*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SAM optimizer improves Raman spectroscopy antibiotic resistance testing by enhancing model generalization on limited clinical data, achieving up to 10.5% accuracy improvement over traditional methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Antimicrobial resistance is a global crisis expected to cause 10 million deaths annually by 2050, disproportionately affecting resource-limited regions. Current Raman spectroscopy algorithms suffer from poor generalization on limited datasets across diverse patient populations and require complex pre-processing steps to handle low-quality spectral data.

Method: The researchers apply Sharpness-Aware Minimization (SAM) to improve model generalization for clinical bacterial isolate classification tasks. SAM optimizes across a diverse array of hyperparameters in Raman spectral classification, addressing limitations of traditional optimization methods.

Result: SAM achieves accuracy improvements of up to 10.5% on a single split and an average accuracy increase of 2.7% across all splits compared to the traditional Adam optimizer in spectral classification tasks.

Conclusion: SAM demonstrates significant capability to advance clinical application of AI-powered Raman spectroscopy tools for rapid antibiotic resistance testing, offering a more robust solution that can work with limited datasets across diverse patient populations.

Abstract: Antimicrobial resistance is expected to claim 10 million lives per year by 2050, and resource-limited regions are most affected. Raman spectroscopy is a novel pathogen diagnostic approach promising rapid and portable antibiotic resistance testing within a few hours, compared to days when using gold standard methods. However, current algorithms for Raman spectra analysis 1) are unable to generalize well on limited datasets across diverse patient populations and 2) require increased complexity due to the necessity of non-trivial pre-processing steps, such as feature extraction, which are essential to mitigate the low-quality nature of Raman spectral data. In this work, we address these limitations using Sharpness-Aware Minimization (SAM) to enhance model generalization across a diverse array of hyperparameters in clinical bacterial isolate classification tasks. We demonstrate that SAM achieves accuracy improvements of up to 10.5% on a single split, and an increase in average accuracy of 2.7% across all splits in spectral classification tasks over the traditional optimizer, Adam. These results display the capability of SAM to advance the clinical application of AI-powered Raman spectroscopy tools.

</details>


### [106] [Enhanced Agriculture-informed Neural Network by Domain Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19466)
*Ci Lin,Futong Li,Rose Chong-Wu,Tet Yeap,Iluju Kiringa*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: KAINN is a hybrid neural-mechanistic framework that incorporates domain knowledge about soil processes to improve N2O emission predictions, outperforming purely data-driven models and providing better interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: N2O emissions from agriculture are difficult to predict due to complex interactions among soil, climate, and management factors, while deep learning models lack physical interpretability and may generalize poorly across different environmental conditions.

Method: Proposed KAINN (Knowledge-enhanced Agriculture-informed Neural Network), a hybrid neural-mechanistic framework that extends the Agriculture-informed Neural Network by incorporating domain knowledge about fertilizer diffusion, soil respiration, and water-filled porosity. Evaluated using CNN, LSTM, and Transformer architectures across multiple growing seasons.

Result: KAINN generally provides lower RMSE and MAE, higher R-squared values than purely data-driven models and original AINN. Learned interfaces show smoother, more physically consistent parameter trajectories with reduced uncertainty.

Conclusion: Incorporating environmental knowledge into neural networks improves reliability, interpretability, and generalization of agricultural N2O emission predictions, demonstrating the value of hybrid approaches.

Abstract: Accurate prediction of nitrous oxide (N2O) emissions from agriculture is important for assessing environmental impacts and supporting sustainable farming. However, prediction remains difficult because N2O emissions result from complex interactions among soil properties, climate, biochemical processes, and management practices, while high-quality observations are limited. Deep learning models can capture nonlinear relationships but often lack physical interpretability and may generalize poorly across environmental conditions. We propose the Knowledge-enhanced Agriculture-informed Neural Network (KAINN), a hybrid neural-mechanistic framework that extends the Agriculture-informed Neural Network by incorporating domain knowledge about fertilizer diffusion, soil respiration, and water-filled porosity. We evaluate KAINN using CNN, LSTM, and Transformer architectures across multiple growing seasons and input-feature configurations. The results show that KAINN generally provides lower root mean square error and mean absolute error and higher R-squared values than purely data-driven models and the original AINN. Analysis of the learned interfaces also shows smoother and more physically consistent parameter trajectories with reduced uncertainty. These findings demonstrate that incorporating environmental knowledge into neural networks can improve the reliability, interpretability, and generalization of agricultural N2O-emission predictions.

</details>


### [107] [Search at the Cost of Sampling: Nearly-Instant Latent Space Bayesian Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19476)
*Donney Fan,Colin Doumont,Aleksandra Kalisz,Paul Duckworth,Jacob R. Gardner,Henry Moss,Geoff Pleiss*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents a highly efficient Bayesian optimization method that achieves 100x speedup for generative model discovery pipelines by exploiting linear models on spherical domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bayesian optimization (BO) is well-suited for de novo discovery pipelines where designs are generated and filtered through virtual screens, but its computational overhead becomes a bottleneck when virtual screens are relatively cheap compared to the sequential decision-making process.

Method: The method exploits the unique combination of a linear model constrained to a spherical domain where high-dimensional latents concentrate. It builds on recent work justifying linear surrogates, while deriving nearly closed-form solutions to surrogate modeling and acquisition problems that exploit spherical symmetry.

Result: The method achieves at least a 100x speedup over state-of-the-art baselines, with matching or improved performance across molecular and image generation benchmarks.

Conclusion: This approach makes Bayesian optimization a practical drop-in solution for de novo pipelines where it was previously too slow to consider, enabling efficient optimization in generative discovery workflows.

Abstract: Generative models are increasingly central to many de novo discovery pipelines, in which designs are generated at scale and filtered through virtual screens to determine a set of candidates to experimentally validate. While Bayesian optimization (BO) is a natural fit for this setting, as it uses past evaluations to guide future proposals, the computational overhead required for its sequential decision-making becomes a bottleneck when virtual screens are relatively cheap. We make BO practical in this regime by exploiting the unique combination of a linear model constrained to a spherical domain where high-dimensional latents concentrate. We build off recent work justifying the use of linear surrogates, while deriving nearly closed-form solutions to the surrogate modelling and acquisition problems that exploit spherical symmetry. The result is at least a 100x speedup over state-of-the art baselines, with matching or improved performance across molecular and image generation benchmarks. Altogether, our method makes BO a practical drop-in for de novo pipelines where it was previously too slow to consider.

</details>


### [108] [Sample Count Is Not Enough: Candidate-Generation Strategy Shapes the Energy and Performance of LLM Test-Time Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19499)
*Mobina Kashaniyan,Ali Jannesari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Test-time scaling improves LLM reasoning by generating multiple candidates, but candidate count N alone doesn't capture systems cost; generation scheduling (batch size vs number of calls) significantly impacts efficiency metrics like latency, energy, and throughput.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current test-time scaling methods focus on candidate count N as the inference budget, but this doesn't account for how candidates are executed (batched vs sequential calls). There's a need to understand the systems cost beyond just accuracy improvements when using multi-candidate approaches.

Method: 1. First studied effect of increasing N on reasoning accuracy using Phi-3-mini and Qwen2.5-1.5B on 500 GSM8K prompts.
2. Fixed N=8 and compared four generation schedules: 1x8 (one call with 8 candidates), 2x4, 4x2, and 8x1.
3. Measured systems metrics: latency, throughput, GPU-hours, and gross GPU-device energy.
4. Experiments conducted on A100 GPUs across three independently scheduled nodes, with additional short-output SciQ/V100 experiments.

Result: 1. Increasing N from 1 to 8 improved accuracy by 8.4pp for Phi-3-mini and 18.4pp for Qwen2.5-1.5B.
2. With N=8, eight serial calls (8x1) used 4.64-4.86x more gross GPU-device energy and had 5.77-6.12x higher P95 latency than one batched call with eight candidates (1x8).
3. The efficiency pattern was consistent across different A100 nodes and V100 experiments.
4. Larger batch sizes (fewer generation calls) were more efficient when candidates are independent and memory allows.

Conclusion: Candidate count alone is insufficient to describe the systems cost of multi-candidate test-time scaling. Evaluations should report not only candidate count and accuracy, but also generation schedule and GPU-level systems metrics. When possible, fewer generation calls with larger batch sizes should be preferred for efficiency.

Abstract: Test-time scaling can improve large language model reasoning by generating and combining multiple candidate responses. In sampling-based methods, the inference budget is often described by the number of generated candidates, N. However, N tells us how many candidates are generated, not how they are executed. The same candidate budget can be produced in one batched generation call or split across several sequential calls with smaller batch sizes. We first study the effect of increasing N on reasoning accuracy using Phi-3-mini and Qwen2.5-1.5B on 500 GSM8K prompts. As expected, increasing N from 1 to 8 improves accuracy by 8.4 percentage points for Phi-3-mini and 18.4 points for Qwen2.5-1.5B. However, accuracy alone does not show the systems cost of using a larger candidate budget. We therefore fix N = 8 and compare four generation schedules: 1x8, 2x4, 4x2, and 8x1, where axb denotes a generation calls with b candidates per call. We measure latency, throughput, GPU-hours, and gross GPU-device energy while keeping the total candidate count fixed. On A100 GPUs, eight serial calls use 4.64-4.86x as much gross GPU-device energy and have 5.77-6.12x the P95 latency of one batched call with eight candidates. The same pattern appears across three independently scheduled A100 nodes per model and in short-output SciQ/V100 experiments. These results show that candidate count alone is not enough to describe the systems cost of multi-candidate test-time scaling. When candidates are independent and memory allows it, fewer generation calls with larger batch sizes are more efficient. Evaluations should therefore report not only candidate count and accuracy, but also generation schedule and GPU-level systems metrics.

</details>


### [109] [LSTM-UT and Recurrent-Depth Transformers on Cellular Automata](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19521)
*Aras Kavuncu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Recurrent-depth Transformers with bounded gated memory (LSTM-UT) outperform variants with expanding attention cache (CoTFormer) and minimal hidden state (BUT) in depth extrapolation and delayed recall tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To compare different recurrent-depth Transformer architectures (BUT, CoTFormer, LSTM-UT) in how they retain information across computation steps and their ability to extrapolate to unseen depths and perform delayed recall tasks.

Method: Evaluated three recurrent-depth Transformer variants: 1) Block Universal Transformer (BUT) with only current hidden state, 2) CoTFormer with expanding attention cache, and 3) new LSTM Universal Transformer (LSTM-UT) with bounded gated memory. Tested on Rule 30 cellular automata (depth extrapolation) and delayed-recall task with interventions on state and cache mechanisms.

Result: BUT extrapolates to unseen depths more reliably than CoTFormer on Rule 30, though accuracy eventually degrades. CoTFormer's failure involves interaction between current state and cache - correcting state temporarily restores accuracy, but retained history undermines correction. BUT outperforms CoTFormer in delayed recall despite lacking direct access to past states. LSTM-UT improves both depth extrapolation and delayed recall over both baselines.

Conclusion: Bounded gated memory (as in LSTM-UT) provides an effective inductive bias for repeated computation and later retrieval, outperforming approaches with expanding attention cache or minimal state retention in the studied tasks.

Abstract: Recurrent-depth Transformers apply shared computation repeatedly, but differ in how they retain information across steps. We compare a Block Universal Transformer (BUT), which carries only its current hidden state; CoTFormer, which also retains an expanding attention cache; and a new LSTM Universal Transformer (LSTM-UT) with bounded gated memory. On Rule 30 cellular automata, BUT extrapolates to unseen recurrent depths more reliably than CoTFormer, although its accuracy eventually degrades. State and cache interventions show that CoTFormer's failure depends on their interaction: correcting the current state can temporarily restore accuracy, while retained history can undermine that correction. In a delayed-recall task, BUT also outperforms CoTFormer despite lacking direct access to past states; CoTFormer does not reliably select the requested cached representation. LSTM-UT improves both depth extrapolation and delayed recall over these baselines. The results support bounded gated memory as an effective inductive bias for repeated computation and later retrieval in these tasks.

</details>


### [110] [Compressed Active Subspaces for Scalable Bayesian Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19539)
*Thomas Flynn,Sanket Jantre,Byung-Jun Yoon,Kibaek Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A new method called Compressed Active Subspaces (CAS) reduces memory requirements for active subspace construction in high-dimensional models by using structured isometric embeddings to compress parameters before building active subspaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard active subspace methods become impractical for large models because they require storing many full-dimensional model gradients, which is memory-prohibitive as model size increases.

Method: Proposes Compressed Active Subspaces (CAS) that first maps model parameters to a compressed space using a structured isometric embedding, then constructs the active subspace within this reduced parameterization.

Result: CAS substantially reduces memory requirements for active subspace construction, enabling Bayesian inference for large models where standard methods are impractical. Demonstrates scalability on neural networks of increasing size while maintaining predictive performance and robust uncertainty estimates.

Conclusion: The proposed CAS approach provides a scalable solution for quantifying predictive uncertainty in high-dimensional models by overcoming the memory limitations of traditional active subspace methods.

Abstract: Active subspace methods provide a framework for quantifying predictive uncertainty in high-dimensional models by identifying and performing inference along parameter directions that have the greatest influence on the model output. However, the construction of active subspaces requires storing many full-dimensional model gradients, which becomes prohibitive as model size increases. We address this limitation by proposing Compressed Active Subspaces (CAS), a scalable approach that first maps the model parameters to a compressed space using a structured isometric embedding and then constructs the active subspace within this reduced parameterization. Our approach substantially reduces the memory required for active subspace construction and enables Bayesian inference for large models where standard active subspace methods become impractical. We demonstrate the scalability of CAS on neural networks of increasing size while maintaining predictive performance and robust uncertainty estimates.

</details>


### [111] [FedFIbOS: Fisher Importance based Optimal Submodelling for Heterogeneous Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19559)
*Yasmeen Afzal,Jeremiah D. Deng,Haibo Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedFIbOS proposes a theoretically-grounded Fisher Information-based parameter selection method for heterogeneous federated learning, achieving ~10% higher accuracy than state-of-the-art methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing heterogeneous federated learning methods use heuristic importance measures (mainly parameter magnitude) without theoretical justification for convergence. There's a fundamental gap where current parameter selection criteria lack theoretical grounding, especially with partial client participation affecting Fisher score estimation.

Method: Proposes FedFIbOS (Fisher Importance-based Optimal Submodelling) which uses Fisher Information in a principled criterion derived from minimizing submodel masking error. The method formulates submodel selection through a Fisher-weighted quadratic masking surrogate and shows that raw Fisher top-k selection solves this surrogate under Fisher-dominant ranking. Fisher scores are efficiently estimated from empirical diagonal Fisher information using squared gradients.

Result: Experiments on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and AGNews under pathological and Dirichlet non-IID settings show FedFIbOS achieves approximately 10% higher accuracy than state-of-the-art methods, with improvements becoming more pronounced under stronger heterogeneity.

Conclusion: FedFIbOS provides a theoretically-grounded approach to parameter selection in heterogeneous federated learning, overcoming limitations of heuristic methods while maintaining convergence properties and achieving significant accuracy improvements, especially in highly heterogeneous settings.

Abstract: Heterogeneous federated learning requires clients with diverse computational capacities to collaboratively train a global model, where each client trains a capacity-constrained submodel. Existing methods select submodel parameters using heuristic importance measures---most prominently parameter magnitude---without theoretical justification for why these measures support convergence. We identify a fundamental gap: existing parameter selection criteria lack theoretical grounding in the convergence framework, partial client participation introduces additional estimation effects in the Fisher scores. We propose \textbf{FedFIbOS}: Fisher Importance-based Optimal Submodelling for heterogeneous federated learning, using Fisher Information in a principled criterion derived from minimizing submodel masking error. %We formally establish when magnitude selection is equivalent to Fisher selection fail under non-IID heterogeneous federated learning. We theoretically formulate submodel selection through a Fisher-weighted quadratic masking surrogate and show that the raw Fisher top-$k$ rule implemented by FedFIbOS solves this surrogate under a Fisher-dominant ranking condition. The resulting method retains the convergence structure of the underlying masked federated optimization bound. Fisher scores are efficiently estimated from empirical diagonal Fisher information using squared gradients, enabling stable and adaptive parameter selection without additional optimization overhead. Experiments on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and AGNews under pathological and Dirichlet non-IID settings show FedFIbOS achieves ${\approx}10\%$ higher accuracy than the state of the art, with improvements becoming more pronounced under stronger heterogeneity.

</details>


### [112] [The Complexity Kink: A Prompt-Side Structural Complexity Index for Code-Generation Reliability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19616)
*Michael Hernandez,Tian Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Researchers developed a pre-generation prompt complexity index with six dimensions to predict coding task difficulty, finding nonmonotonic relationships between complexity and success rates that vary by model type and task framing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods measure complexity from generated code, which is failure-dependent—difficult prompts can yield short failing programs and be assigned low output complexity. This creates a need for prompt-side complexity measurement before generation that separates complexity assessment from correctness.

Method: Introduced a six-dimension prompt-side structural-complexity index scored before code generation. Selected 5,000 Python prompts across six complexity bands, had four LLM raters rescore them (19,997 ratings total). Evaluated 21 models per prompt (105,000 generations total). Used inter-rater reliability analysis, regression modeling, fixed effects, and structural equation modeling to analyze relationships.

Result: Found nonmonotonic breakpoint at composite score 13.75: 79.9% pass rate below, 87.6% above. Breakpoints shift with task-type fixed effects (10.75) and construction-frame controls (8.50). Model-specific fits show 16 upward and 5 downward changes. Human agreement is moderate and rater-dependent, but paraphrase and cross-language rescoring preserve ordering. Overidentification tests reject joint restrictions on six dimensions.

Conclusion: The study provides a pre-generation measurement framework for prompt complexity that reliably predicts coding task difficulty. The composite index works as an ordinal predictor, though no causal interpretation is claimed. The framework enables better understanding of reliability regimes in code generation tasks.

Abstract: Complexity measured from generated code is failure-dependent: a difficult prompt can yield a short failing program and be assigned low output complexity. We introduce a six-dimension prompt-side structural-complexity index scored before generation and kept separate from correctness. We select 5,000 Python prompts across six bands of a preliminary single-rater rubric. Four out-of-panel LLM raters rescore the locked prompts, giving 19,997 score rows; composite inter-rater reliability is ICC = 0.872 on the 4,998 prompts with all four ratings. We evaluate 21 models per prompt, yielding 105,000 generations. In the unadjusted mean-pooled analysis, pass rate has a nonmonotone breakpoint at composite 13.75, with 79.9% at or below and 87.6% above. This is not a universal failure cutoff. Task-type fixed effects shift the breakpoint to 10.75 and cut the regime gap from 7.6 to 2.1 points. A construction-frame control shifts it to 8.50 with a raw gap of -3.5 points, and neither frame alone reproduces the pooled +7.6-point change. Model-specific fits include 16 upward and five downward changes. A 365-prompt audit-clean extension matches the original five-model estimates at bins 15 and 16 but adds only 14 prompts above bin 16. Among zero-pass generations with computable Lizard complexity, 28.5% pair a prompt composite above 8 with output complexity at most 10. Human agreement is moderate and rater-dependent on a disagreement-enriched calibration set; paraphrase and cross-language rescoring preserve score ordering. Overidentification tests reject the joint restrictions on the six dimensions, so we treat the composite as an index and make no causal interpretation of the 2SLS estimates. The contribution is a pre-generation measurement framework and a bounded observational analysis of reliability regimes.

</details>


### [113] [A Policy Profile for Croissant: Refusal as a Property of the Dataset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19640)
*Alexander Chernov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A system for evaluating data use conditions in Croissant ML dataset descriptors with a standardized decision procedure, verification of implementations, and composition with external policies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Croissant descriptors for ML datasets include data use conditions but lack evaluation specifications: no decision procedure, cost bounds, handling of unevaluable conditions, audit trails, or composition with caller-side policies.

Method: Introduces an additive profile that lets datasets declare admissible operations under specific conditions using a closed set of five operators with full decision procedures, enabling gates to make decisions from descriptors alone with recorded checks.

Result: The system was evaluated on three corpora: real nf-core pipeline descriptors (decisions match native records with 11.7μs added cost vs 119μs total decision), grammar-generated corpus covering all operators and cases (552 records show three-way agreement), and demonstrating that caller/data-bound policies have non-overlapping state spaces.

Conclusion: The carrier format is not the main contribution; the evaluation semantics is. The system provides a practical framework for evaluating data use conditions in Croissant descriptors with standardized decision procedures, audit trails, and proper composition of policies.

Abstract: Croissant is the de facto machine-readable descriptor for ML datasets: JSON-LD over schema.org. Since version 1.1 it also carries data use conditions, recommending DUO and ODRL for them. What no version specifies is how any of them is evaluated: no decision procedure, no bound on evaluation cost, no outcome for a condition an implementation cannot evaluate, no record of what was checked, and nothing on composition with caller-side authority. We supply that half. An additive profile lets a dataset declare the operations it admits and the conditions under which it admits them, over a closed set of five operators whose decision procedure is given in full, so a gate decides from the descriptor alone and records what it checked. Two corpora evaluate it and their evidence is kept apart. Three descriptors that gated a real nf-core pipeline give the deployment result: decisions from a profile document match the gate's native descriptor record for record, stripping the layer leaves a valid Croissant document, and the added cost is 11.7 $μ$s against a 119 $μ$s decision. A corpus generated from the profile's grammar gives the breadth, covering every operator, refusal class and conformance clause. Across its valid cases, 552 complete decision records agree three ways -- native descriptor, profile terms, and the same policy as ODRL in usageInfo. The carrier is therefore not the contribution; the evaluation semantics is. Finally, caller-bound and data-bound policies range over non-overlapping state spaces, so neither permit set contains the other.

</details>


### [114] [CoRe: Coherence and Relational Alignment for Multivariate Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19670)
*Xiaoyu Lin,Huiran Duan,Yining Liu,Zhixiang Wu,Chu Lin,Lin Lu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CoRe introduces a model-agnostic learning objective for direct multivariate forecasting that replaces pointwise errors with frequency coherence and relational graph constraints to preserve temporal and cross-variable structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard direct forecasting uses pointwise errors like MSE, which provide stable supervision but don't explicitly preserve the structure of future trajectories. Temporal coherence within variables and relational consistency across variables can both be weakened by pointwise objectives.

Method: CoRe replaces pointwise supervision with two output-space constraints: 1) frequency coherence loss that aligns predicted and target spectra, and 2) low-rank relational graph loss that matches sampled pairwise differences in a target-derived PCA subspace. The approach is model-agnostic, introduces no trainable parameters, and can be applied to existing forecasting backbones by changing only the loss function.

Result: Experiments on standard benchmarks show that CoRe improves strong baselines, compares favorably with recent forecasting objectives, and remains effective across different backbones, datasets, and hyperparameter settings consistently.

Conclusion: CoRe provides an effective alternative to pointwise objectives for direct multivariate forecasting by explicitly preserving temporal and relational structure through frequency and graph-based constraints, offering consistent improvements across various settings without adding trainable parameters.

Abstract: Direct forecasting has become a standard paradigm for multivariate time-series forecasting because it predicts the full future horizon in a single pass. However, its training objective is often still decomposed into pointwise errors such as MSE. Such objectives provide stable supervision, but they do not explicitly preserve the structure of the future trajectory: temporal coherence within each variable and relational consistency across variables can both be weakened. We propose CoRe, a model-agnostic learning objective for direct multivariate forecasting. CoRe replaces pointwise supervision with two output-space constraints: a frequency coherence loss that aligns predicted and target spectra, and a low-rank relational graph loss that matches sampled pairwise differences in a target-derived PCA subspace. The resulting objective introduces no trainable parameters and can be applied to existing forecasting backbones by changing only the loss. Experiments on standard benchmarks show that CoRe improves strong baselines, compares favorably with recent forecasting objectives, and remains effective across different backbones, datasets, and hyperparameter settings overall consistently.

</details>


### [115] [Conservation Buys Stability and Factoring Buys Counterfactuals in Physical World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19674)
*Yufeng Wang,Parivesh Priye,Lu Wei,Haibin Ling*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A learned physics simulator needs two distinct structural components: symplectic integration for long-horizon stability, and explicit linear factorization for counterfactual generalization to unseen physical laws.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learned simulators often fail in two ways when conditions change: small errors accumulate causing trajectory drift over long rollouts, and models cannot adapt to interventions on physical parameters (sticking to training laws).

Method: 1. Use symplectic integrators with learned energy to preserve geometry and ensure long-horizon stability. 2. Encode physical coupling through explicit linear factorization to enable counterfactual transfer to unseen physical laws.

Result: Symplectic integration enables 100× longer stable rollouts than comparable methods, while linear factorization allows models to follow never-seen physical couplings. These mechanisms are separable and persist across systems including pixel-based observations.

Conclusion: Long-horizon stability and changed-law generalization require distinct structural commitments in physical world models. Each can be deliberately imposed without needing the other, providing concrete design principles for robust learned simulators.

Abstract: A learned simulator can reproduce its training conditions accurately yet fail in two distinct ways once those conditions change. Over long rollouts, small errors accumulate until the trajectory drifts away from physically plausible behavior; under an intervention on a physical parameter, the model may continue to follow the law seen during training rather than the intervened one. We show that these two failures require different structural remedies. Evolving a learned energy with a symplectic integrator preserves the geometry of the conservative dynamics and keeps rollouts bounded and physically meaningful for up to $100\times$ the training horizon, while equal-capacity predictors, an energy-regularized predictor, and a tuned neural ODE diverge. By contrast, encoding the physical coupling through an explicit linear factorization enables the model to follow a never-seen sign of that coupling, whereas an unrestricted parameterization remains locked to the training law. Crucially, the two mechanisms are separable: removing the structure responsible for long-horizon stability leaves counterfactual transfer intact, while removing the factorized coupling destroys counterfactual transfer without eliminating stability. This double dissociation, established with matched controls that remove or replace one structural component at a time, persists beyond the headline three-body system and remains visible when the physical state must be inferred from pixels rather than provided directly. The result is a concrete design principle for physical world models: long-horizon stability and changed-law generalization arise from distinct structural commitments, and each can be imposed deliberately without requiring the other.

</details>


### [116] [Opinion Dynamics-based Coalition Formation for Federated Learning in Heterogeneous IoT Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19695)
*Mohammed El Hanjri,Anas Abouaomar,Hamidou Tembine,Abdellatif Kobbane*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes using Hegselmann-Krause opinion dynamics for coalition formation in federated learning to handle statistical heterogeneity in IoT deployments, achieving better forecasting accuracy than standard FedAvg and other baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard FedAvg fails to capture client-specific patterns in heterogeneous IoT deployments like smart-city water-metering networks where each smart meter observes household-specific consumption patterns. Averaging dissimilar local models into a single global model is suboptimal under statistical heterogeneity.

Method: The authors form client coalitions in the local-weight space using Hegselmann-Krause bounded-confidence opinion dynamics with Euclidean-distance and cosine-similarity confidence criteria. They apply this to short-term water-consumption forecasting with local LSTM models, creating endogenous coalition structures within 10 inner iterations without additional client-side computation or communication overhead.

Result: The proposed HK-based coalition formation reduces average MAE by up to 54% relative to FedAvg, 39% relative to FedProx, and 24% relative to Per-FedAvg while achieving 83-85% global accuracy. The method produces stable coalition structures efficiently with no extra computation/communication burden.

Conclusion: HK opinion dynamics is an effective approach for coalition formation in federated learning, particularly for heterogeneous IoT applications. It significantly improves forecasting accuracy over standard methods while maintaining computational efficiency and generating meaningful client groupings based on model similarity.

Abstract: Federated learning (FL) enables privacy-preserving, on-device training across heterogeneous Internet-of-Things (IoT) deployments such as smart-city water-metering networks, where each smart meter observes a household-specific consumption time series. Under such statistical heterogeneity, the standard Federated Averaging (FedAvg) aggregation averages dissimilar local models into a single global model that may fail to capture client-specific patterns. We address this by forming client coalitions directly in the local-weight space and aggregating at the coalition level. Extending a prior weight-driven coalition-formation scheme, we model coalition formation as a Hegselmann-Krause (HK) bounded-confidence opinion-dynamics process acting on the local weights, and develop variants of the HK interaction based on Euclidean-distance and cosine-similarity confidence criteria. The framework is applied to short-term water-consumption forecasting with local Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) models and evaluated against FedAvg, Per-FedAvg, FedProx, and FedAvg with Euclidean-distance or cosine-similarity coalition formation. Experiments on a real smart-metering dataset of water consumption show that the proposed HK-based coalition formation produces stable, endogenous coalition structures within at most ten inner iterations, incurs no additional client-side computation or communication compared to FedAvg, and reduces the average MAE by up to 54% relative to FedAvg, 39% relative to FedProx, and 24% relative to Per-FedAvg, while achieving the highest global accuracy (83-85%).

</details>


### [117] [Odds-Ratio Thompson Sampling: A Specification and Design Guide for Contrast-Based Multi-Armed Bandits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19709)
*Sulgi Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OR-TS (Odds-Ratio Thompson Sampling) uses joint posterior over log-odds contrasts rather than absolute reward rates, fitting the common level fresh in each batch to handle moving baseline levels in batched multi-armed bandits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard batched multi-armed bandits carry absolute reward rates between updates, which becomes stale when shared baseline levels move between batches. This memory deterioration hurts performance even though arm comparisons may remain valid.

Method: OR-TS maintains joint posterior over log-odds contrasts instead of absolute rates, marginalizing out the common baseline level and refitting it fresh in every batch. Uses Bayesian bandit agent with two controls: decay for past evidence survival and aggressiveness for belief-to-allocation sharpness.

Result: In 86 public A/B series, baseline level varied 25× more than contrast. In synthetic environments, moving levels cost absolute-rate memory 5× the regret of OR-TS. In 71 real experiments, contrast memory chose the better arm twice as often despite small expected-click differences (<0.1%). OR-TS fails when contrasts themselves move significantly.

Conclusion: Odds-Ratio Thompson Sampling significantly outperforms absolute-rate memory when baseline levels move frequently between batches, maintaining better arm selection and lower regret, though it struggles when arm contrasts themselves change substantially.

Abstract: Batched multi-armed bandits update on a service's own schedule, and the usual implementation carries each arm's absolute reward rate from one update to the next. When the shared level moves between batches, that memory goes stale even though the comparisons between arms may not have. Odds-Ratio Thompson Sampling (OR-TS) instead carries the joint posterior over log-odds contrasts and fits the common level afresh in every batch, marginalizing it out. This paper specifies that update, places it inside a Bayesian bandit agent with two controls, decay for how much past evidence survives an update and aggressiveness for how sharply belief becomes allocation, and evaluates it against absolute-rate memory. Across 86 public A/B series the level varies about twenty-five times more than the contrast. In prespecified synthetic environments a moving level costs absolute-rate memory five times the regret and leaves the best arm below a majority of traffic in 7 of 20 runs, against none for OR-TS. In a policy simulation built from 71 real experiments, where the contrasts are too small to resolve, expected-click differences stay within 0.1% for 58 of them, yet contrast memory still ends on the better arm more than twice as often. Where the contrasts themselves move, the bet fails, and that case is reported too.

</details>


### [118] [Learn Your Own Thoughts: Abstract Token Curriculum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19717)
*Khashayar Gatmiry,Avrajit Ghosh,Parsa Mirtaheri,Jason D. Lee,Nika Haghtalab,Emmanuel Abbe,Peter Bartlett*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ATC (Abstract Token Curriculum) is a curriculum learning framework that trains LLMs to develop continuous intermediate representations without explicit supervision or manual scratchpad design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current chain-of-thought (CoT) techniques require explicit supervision on thinking tokens and task-specific data, which limits their applicability and scalability. There's a need for methods that can elicit effective continuous intermediate representations without such supervision.

Method: ATC uses curriculum learning to gradually increase problem complexity through a sequence of distributions, training models to develop internal abstract "thoughts" in continuous representation space without direct supervision or manual scratchpad design.

Result: Theoretical analysis shows that for learning parity functions with single-layer softmax attention using ATC, attention naturally focuses on CoT tokens providing the "easiest path" to predicting next tokens. Experimental results demonstrate effectiveness on graph reachability and arithmetic learning tasks.

Conclusion: ATC provides a novel framework for eliciting continuous intermediate representations in LLMs without supervision, offering both theoretical foundations and practical advantages over previous methods for training continuous thoughts.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable reasoning capabilities by utilizing chain-of-thought (CoT) as a scratchpad for intermediate stages of thinking. However, CoT techniques require explicit supervision on thinking tokens, which requires rich, task-specific data. In this work, we propose Abstract Token Curriculum (ATC), a novel curriculum learning framework that elicits effective continuous intermediate representations without direct supervision or manual scratchpad design. ATC gradually increases problem complexity through a sequence of distributions, training the model to develop internal abstract ``thoughts'' in the continuous representation space. This paper provides both theoretical and experimental evidence for the benefits of ATC and its advantages over previous methods for training continuous thoughts. Theoretically, we show that for learning parity functions with single-layer softmax attention using ATC, attention naturally focuses on the CoT tokens in the context that provide the ``easiest path'' to predicting the next token. Experimentally, we show ATC's effectiveness on graph reachability and arithmetic learning tasks.

</details>


### [119] [Alliance Beats Isolation: Unifying Heterogeneous Allied Datasets Improves Classifier Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19748)
*Girish Keshav Palshikar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A method to merge heterogeneous feature spaces of allied datasets to create a unified representation that enables transfer learning and improves classifier performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many real-world domains have multiple labelled public datasets that are "allied" (same object type, disjoint object sets, same labels) but with largely distinct feature spaces, making it impossible to train a single classifier across them or transfer knowledge between them.

Method: Propose a method to merge feature spaces of allied heterogeneous datasets into a single feature space, then use matrix completion to create a unified dataset based on the merged representation.

Result: Experiments on several pairs of allied heterogeneous datasets show that classifiers trained on the unified representation consistently outperform classifiers separately trained on the constituent datasets.

Conclusion: The method provides an easy way to substantially improve classifier performance by unifying multiple allied datasets, facilitating knowledge transfer between them.

Abstract: In many application domains, such as student dropout, insurance fraud, loan approval, and machine failures, several labelled public datasets are available where (i) data is about the same type of objects but the set of actual underlying objects are disjoint; and (ii) the class labels are same; and (iii) the feature spaces of the datasets are largely distinct (heterogeneous), with a few shared features. We call such datasets as allied. A single classifier cannot be trained on both datasets together, and one classifier trained on one dataset cannot be tested on the other. In this paper, we propose a method to merge the feature-spaces into a single feature-space for a pair of given allied heterogeneous datasets. We then use a matrix completion method to create a unified dataset based on the merged feature-space. The hypothesis is that the merged representation facilitates the transfer of classification knowledge from one dataset to another. We conduct experiments on several pairs of allied, heterogeneous datasets and several classifiers to demonstrate that any classifier trained on the unified representation always outperforms classifiers separately trained on the constituent allied datasets on several pairs of allied datasets. This work provides an easy way to substantially improve classifier performance by unifying and using multiple allied datasets together.

</details>


### [120] [OceanMoE: Structured Conditional Sparse Computation for Long-Horizon Multivariate Ocean Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19768)
*Yishun Zhu,Jian Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OceanMoE: A conditional sparse Mixture-of-Experts framework for multivariate ocean forecasting that balances shared ocean context with adaptive specialization through target-specific routing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multivariate ocean forecasting must handle heterogeneous statistical and dynamical characteristics across different prediction variables and locations while exploiting shared evolution in the coupled ocean system. Fully shared models lack flexibility, while fully independent models discard common ocean context.

Method: Propose OceanMoE, a structured conditional sparse Mixture-of-Experts framework that fuses cross-variable information to construct target-specific local representations, uses content-conditioned sparse routing at each spatial location (with number of active experts adapted to router confidence), and includes learned geographic bias parameterized by spherical-harmonic spatial bases in the decoder with shared residual and seasonal pathways.

Result: Experiments on long-horizon autoregressive ORAS5 forecasting show OceanMoE lowers aggregate forecasting error in both evaluated settings, maintains lower geometric-mean normalized RMSE than baselines over most later rollout months, and routing analyses show expert allocation varies with prediction targets and spatial locations.

Conclusion: Structured conditional computation is an effective modeling strategy for balancing shared ocean context with adaptive specialization in multivariate ocean forecasting.

Abstract: Multivariate ocean forecasting must exploit shared evolution in a coupled ocean system while adapting to the heterogeneous statistical and dynamical characteristics of different prediction variables and locations. Fully shared models may lack the flexibility to handle this heterogeneity, whereas fully independent models discard the common ocean context shared across variables. The key question is how to retain shared context in a unified model while allowing computation to specialize according to the prediction target and local state. We propose OceanMoE, a structured conditional sparse Mixture-of-Experts framework that combines sharing and specialization for multivariate ocean forecasting. OceanMoE fuses cross-variable information to construct target-specific local representations and uses them to perform content-conditioned sparse routing at each spatial location, with the number of active experts adapted to router confidence. In the decoder, routing is augmented with a learned geographic bias parameterized by spherical-harmonic spatial bases, while shared residual and seasonal pathways provide common cross-variable and month-dependent context. Experiments on long-horizon autoregressive ORAS5 forecasting show that OceanMoE lowers aggregate forecasting error in both evaluated settings and maintains lower geometric-mean normalized RMSE than the corresponding baselines over most later rollout months. Routing analyses further show that expert allocation varies with prediction targets and spatial locations. These results support structured conditional computation as a modeling strategy for balancing shared ocean context with adaptive specialization.

</details>


### [121] [PhyRestore: Physics-Structured Latent-Factor Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19776)
*Ahmed Shafee,Chayan Lahiri*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PhyRestore is a physics-structured framework that restores corrupted physical factors rather than directly predicting soil-loss change, using known physical relationships to reconstruct temporal change from noisy inputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Estimating temporal soil-loss change is challenging when physical input factors are noisy or corrupted, especially since substantial changes are rare compared to the many locations with little change.

Method: PhyRestore uses a physics-structured latent-factor restoration framework that restores corrupted physical factors and reconstructs temporal change through the Revised Universal Soil Loss Equation (RUSLE) physical relationship, rather than directly predicting soil-loss change.

Result: Factor restoration improves high-magnitude recovery when corrupted factors remain identifiable, but its advantage weakens under joint corruption, sparse positive extremes, and factor values outside training support.

Conclusion: Physics-structured restoration of corrupted factors is effective for soil-loss change estimation, particularly when individual factors remain identifiable, though limitations exist for joint corruption and extreme values.

Abstract: Estimating temporal soil-loss change is challenging when physically meaningful input factors are noisy or corrupted, particularly because substantial changes are rare relative to the large number of locations exhibiting little change. We study this problem through the Revised Universal Soil Loss Equation (RUSLE) and introduce PhyRestore, a physics-structured latent-factor restoration framework. Rather than directly predicting soil-loss change or correcting a degraded physical estimate, PhyRestore restores corrupted physical factors and reconstructs temporal change through the known physical relationship. We evaluate PhyRestore in a watershed-scale bitemporal raster setting under isolated and simultaneous corruption of rainfall erosivity and cover management, comparing it with the degraded RUSLE estimate and Direct RF, XGBoost, MLP, and CNN models. Factor restoration improves high-magnitude recovery when the corrupted factors remain identifiable, but its advantage weakens under joint corruption, sparse positive extremes, and factor values outside the training support.

</details>


### [122] [Learning-Based Reconstruction of Optical Properties in Bilayered Media from Single-distance Time-Resolved Reflectance Measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19786)
*Caterina Amendola,Giulia Maffeis,Lorenzo Buffoni,Lorenzo Chicchi,Francesco Coghi,Duccio Fanelli,Raffaele Marino,Fabrizio Martelli,Riccardo Paoli,Lorenzo Pattelli,Lorenzo Spinelli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Machine learning approach outperforms traditional diffusion-based methods for reconstructing optical properties in layered biological media from time-domain reflectance measurements, offering faster and more accurate reconstructions without requiring prior knowledge of layer structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional analytical models based on the diffusion equation struggle with reconstructing optical properties (absorption and scattering coefficients) in layered biological media from time-domain reflectance measurements. They have poor accuracy for superficial absorption and deep-layer scattering due to structural heterogeneity.

Method: Developed a machine learning framework to reconstruct optical properties of bilayered media. Generated robust synthetic dataset using exact Monte Carlo simulations at multiple source-detector distances to overcome approximations of diffusion theory. Trained ML pipeline on this dataset and validated against state-of-the-art model-based reconstruction methods.

Result: ML approach achieved significant reconstruction speed-up and higher accuracy than model-based inverse solvers. It can estimate parameter space dimensionality without requiring a priori information about the number of layers in the investigated geometry.

Conclusion: Machine learning provides a superior alternative to traditional diffusion-based methods for optical property reconstruction in layered biological media. Future enhancements could further improve accuracy by using multiple DTOF curves from the same medium in a joint multi-distance reconstruction approach.

Abstract: The inverse problem of reconstructing optical properties, specifically absorption and scattering coefficients, in layered biological media from time-domain reflectance measurements remains a significant challenge for traditional analytical models. Inverse solvers based on the diffusion equation often struggle with structural heterogeneity, frequently yielding poor accuracy for superficial absorption and deep-layers scattering. In this work, we propose a machine learning framework as an alternative approach to reconstruct the optical properties of a bilayered medium, benchmarking its efficiency and accuracy against model-based algorithms. To overcome the intrinsic approximations of diffusion theory and inverse reconstruction, we generated a robust synthetic dataset of forward DTOF using exact Monte Carlo simulations at multiple source-detector distances. A machine learning pipeline was then trained on this dataset and validated against state-of-the-art model-based reconstruction methods. Besides the significant reconstruction speed-up, the machine learning approach achieves higher accuracy than model-based inverse solvers, further providing an estimate of the parameter space dimensionality without requiring any a priori information about the number of layers in the investigated geometry. Further enhancements in the reconstruction accuracy can be expected in future extensions of this work, by training the pipeline over multiple DTOF curves from the same medium, in a joint multi-distance reconstruction approach.

</details>


### [123] [DeliveryGym: An RL Environment for Long-Horizon Embodied Agent Planning with Adaptive Curriculum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19801)
*Haoqiang Kang,Yiming Zhang,Yiyang Guo,Chuying Li,Jianzhi Shen,Tianruo Rose Xu,Xiaokang Ye,Lianhui Qin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DeliveryGym is a 3D executable environment for training embodied LLM agents to optimize courier deliveries across complete shifts, using reinforcement learning from trajectory consequences and adaptive training scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current executable environments for LLM agents often focus only on immediate task success, but embodied agents need to consider resource consumption (time, energy, money) that affects future tasks. There's a need for environments that preserve these dependencies across complete trajectories to enable learning of true planning.

Method: The authors created DeliveryGym, a 3D environment that couples multimodal tool interaction with persistent world dynamics, computes trajectory rewards from simulator events to make decision costs available for RL, and adapts future training shifts to policy weaknesses while keeping evaluation fixed. They tested across six models and 13 city maps.

Result: Evaluation revealed a gap between executing assigned deliveries and optimizing work sequencing over complete shifts. RL improved Qwen3-VL-4B's net income by 54.3% on fixed tests, showing learning from complete shifts improves performance. Adaptive training improved test income by 16.5% over uniform sampling, demonstrating practice scenario importance.

Conclusion: DeliveryGym provides a valuable executable setting for studying how agents learn to coordinate deliveries and preserve resources within episodes, showing that both learning from complete trajectories and strategic training scenario adaptation are crucial for effective embodied agent planning.

Abstract: Executable environments enable LLM agents to learn from the consequences of their actions. For embodied agents, those consequences extend beyond whether the current task succeeds: completing a delivery can consume the time, energy, or money needed for later work. Learning to plan therefore requires environments that preserve these dependencies and turn them into feedback across a complete trajectory. We introduce DeliveryGym, a 3D environment for evaluating and training agents on continuous courier shifts. It couples multimodal tool interaction with persistent world dynamics and computes trajectory rewards from simulator events, making the costs of an agent's decisions available for reinforcement learning (RL). The environment also adapts future training shifts to the policy's observed weaknesses while keeping evaluation fixed. Across six models and 13 city maps, evaluation exposes a gap between reliably executing assigned deliveries and choosing and sequencing work over a shift. On the fixed test suite, RL improves Qwen3-VL-4B's net income by 54.3%, showing that learning from complete shifts improves performance under these coupled constraints. Adapting the training environment improves test income by 16.5% over uniform sampling at the same rollout budget, indicating that which situations an agent practices also matters. DeliveryGym provides an executable setting for studying how agents learn to coordinate deliveries and preserve resources for later orders within an episode.

</details>


### [124] [Beyond Flattened Tokens: Structure-Preserving EEG Decoding with Reusable TriDim Blocks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19842)
*Shiyue Su,Song Wang,Zekai Zhan,Junjie Zeng,Ziling Lu,Zongsheng Li,Xinyuan Ye,Zhiyuan Ma,Xinke Shen,Quanying Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TriDimEEG is a new EEG decoding architecture that preserves three explicit EEG axes (channel, intra-patch samples, inter-patch positions) using reusable TriDim blocks with cross-axis attention, achieving state-of-the-art performance across multiple EEG tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing EEG architectures struggle to effectively preserve and coordinate the three critical structural aspects of EEG signals: channel organization, local waveform dynamics, and long-range temporal context. Current approaches either use separate specialized modules or collapse everything into a single token sequence, making it difficult to maintain their distinct roles and coordinate interactions throughout the backbone.

Method: The authors propose TriDim, a reusable block that maintains three explicit EEG axes: channel (spatial), sample position within each patch (short-term temporal), and patch position across the recording (long-term temporal). Each TriDim block applies feed-forward transformations along individual axes and cross-axis attention to coordinate information exchange. They stack these blocks with multi-level tri-axis readout to create TriDimEEG, a standalone EEG decoder.

Result: Under strict cross-subject evaluation on eight datasets spanning clinical diagnosis, sleep staging, motor imagery, and emotion recognition, TriDimEEG achieves the best overall performance among fifteen evaluated models, with 4.3% relative improvement in average accuracy over the second-best model. When replacing Transformer blocks in three EEG foundation models with TriDim blocks, they achieve an average relative improvement of 7.4% in downstream accuracy while reducing parameter counts by 17.0% to 47.3%.

Conclusion: TriDim is established as an effective and reusable building block for EEG processing, and TriDimEEG is demonstrated to be a strong standalone EEG decoder with superior performance across diverse tasks while being more parameter-efficient than existing approaches.

Abstract: Effective EEG decoding requires representations that preserve organization among channels, local waveform dynamics, and long-range temporal context. Existing EEG architectures often capture these structures using separate specialized modules or collapse them into a single token sequence, making it difficult to maintain their distinct roles and coordinate their interactions throughout the backbone. We propose TriDim, a reusable block that preserves the representation shape and keeps three EEG axes explicit: channel, sample position within each patch, and patch position across the recording. These axes correspond to spatial, short-term temporal, and long-term temporal information, respectively. Each TriDim block applies feed-forward transformations along individual axes and cross-axis attention to coordinate information exchange among them. By stacking TriDim blocks with a multi-level tri-axis readout, we construct TriDimEEG, a standalone EEG decoder. Under strict cross-subject evaluation on eight datasets spanning clinical diagnosis, sleep staging, motor imagery, and emotion recognition, TriDimEEG achieves the best overall performance among fifteen evaluated models, with a 4.3% relative improvement in average accuracy over the second-best model. Replacing Transformer blocks in three EEG foundation models with TriDim blocks yields an average relative improvement of 7.4% in downstream accuracy while reducing parameter counts by 17.0% to 47.3%. These results establish TriDim as an effective and reusable building block and TriDimEEG as a strong standalone EEG decoder. Code and parameters of TriDimEEG are available at https://github.com/ncclab-sustech/TriDim_model.

</details>


### [125] [Expected Hypervolume Maximization for Multiobjective Optimization under Uncertainties](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19858)
*Victor Trappler*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A Bayesian approach for multiobjective optimization under uncertainty using expected hypervolume maximization with gradient-based methods and Gaussian Process surrogates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional multiobjective optimization under uncertainty often uses expected values of objectives, but this may not be optimal. The authors propose a Bayesian decision framework to better handle uncertainties in multiobjective optimization problems.

Method: Formulates the problem as a Bayesian decision problem maximizing expected hypervolume of a finite set of input points. Uses gradient-based stochastic optimization methods with special handling for dominated points. When differentiable code is unavailable, employs Gaussian Processes as differentiable surrogate models. Also develops active learning strategies through specialized acquisition functions for building effective surrogate models.

Result: Successfully demonstrates gradient-based optimization framework for expected hypervolume maximization. Proposes novel active learning strategies for constructing surrogate models tailored to multiobjective optimization. Strategies are validated and compared on simple analytical problems to assess their performance.

Conclusion: The proposed Bayesian approach with expected hypervolume maximization and Gaussian Process surrogates provides an effective framework for multiobjective optimization under uncertainty, with active learning strategies improving surrogate model construction for these problems.

Abstract: The problem of multiobjective optimization under uncertainties is often approached by taking the expectation of each objective. In this work, we propose instead to formulate this as a Bayesian decision problem and to rely on the expected value of the hypervolume, which is to be maximized with respect to a finite set of input points. We show that this can be performed using methods based on gradients in a stochastic optimization framework, provided that care is taken with respect to dominated points. Moreover, in the absence of readily available differentiable code, we propose to use Gaussian Processes as differentiable surrogate models, in order to perform the optimization. An additional contribution in this work are some active learning strategies, through acquisition functions which helps construct a surrogate model well-designed for the multiobjective optimization problem at stake. These strategies are compared on simple analytical problems to assess their performances.

</details>


### [126] [Pretrained Medical Representations for the Practical Screening of Drug Repositioning Candidates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19865)
*Yuhei Fujioka,Daitaro Misawa,Shingo Fukuma*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposed a unified pre-training framework with hierarchical sub-token aggregation, partial masking, and cross-reference mechanisms for medical code representation learning, enabling practical hypothesis discovery workflows including drug repositioning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing BERT-based models fail to adequately capture hierarchical structure of medical codes and complex interactions between diagnoses and treatments, limiting their effectiveness for scientific hypothesis discovery from EHR data.

Method: Unified pre-training framework integrating: 1) hierarchical sub-token aggregation, 2) partial masking, and 3) cross-reference mechanisms. For hypothesis prioritization, introduced Task-Adaptive Representation Approach to reduce over-encoding of prescription information in diagnostic vectors.

Result: Model outperformed existing methods on pre-training objectives and downstream clinical event prediction tasks (dementia onset, hospitalization). Successfully rediscovered known promising Alzheimer's drugs in data-driven drug repositioning case study, and enabled robust hypothesis prioritization.

Conclusion: Domain-informed representation learning combined with task-adaptive representation control enables practical hypothesis discovery workflows for exploratory screening, though not providing causal evidence but identifying promising candidates for subsequent rigorous causal inference.

Abstract: Representation learning from medical code sequences in electronic health records and medical claims data has been successful in various clinical applications, such as those regarding disease prediction. However, significant challenges remain in extending this approach to the discovery of scientific hypotheses. One reason is that many existing BERT-based models fail to adequately capture the hierarchical structure of medical codes and the complex interactions between diagnoses and treatments. To address these limitations, we propose a new unified pre-training framework that explicitly integrates hierarchical sub-token aggregation, partial masking, and cross-reference mechanisms. The proposed model consistently outperformed existing methods on both pre-training objectives and downstream clinical event prediction tasks, including the onset of dementia and hospitalization. We also conducted an in silico drug repositioning case study targeting Alzheimer's disease. In the hypothesis generation step, our approach successfully rediscovered known promising drugs in a data-driven manner without relying on such external knowledge sources as the literature. Subsequently, in the hypothesis prioritization step, we introduced a Task-Adaptive Representation Approach to alleviate the over-encoding of historical prescription information within diagnostic vectors, enabling the robust prioritization of generated hypotheses. This study establishes an exploratory screening workflow for hypothesis generation and prioritization based on observational associations. Importantly, this framework is not intended to provide causal evidence, but rather to identify promising candidates for subsequent rigorous causal inference. Overall, this study demonstrates that domain-informed representation learning combined with task-adaptive representation control can enable a practical hypothesis discovery workflow.

</details>


### [127] [AURA: Adaptive Uncertainty-Routed Analysis for Email Threat Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19873)
*Omran Berjawi,Walid fahs,Rida Khatoun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AURA is a multimodal email threat detection system that uses uncertainty routing: it first analyzes URL classifications, then escalates ambiguous cases to a transformer for semantic analysis, achieving strong generalization across diverse spam/phishing attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Email spam and phishing attacks remain critical security threats, with adversaries increasingly using large language models to craft convincing malicious messages. Existing spam detection systems struggle to generalize across diverse and evolving attack scenarios, reducing their effectiveness in real-world deployment.

Method: The Adaptive Uncertainty-Routed Analysis (AURA) system uses a two-layer multimodal approach: 1) It quantifies prediction uncertainty from a URL classifier, and 2) Only ambiguous messages are escalated to a fine-tuned transformer encoder for semantic analysis of email content.

Result: AURA achieves a macro F1-score of 0.9858 in-distribution. On out-of-distribution real-world corpora (NazPhish-Eval and GuenterTrap-Eval), it maintains scores of 0.9502 and 0.9436 respectively, demonstrating robust generalization across a decade of adversarial campaigns.

Conclusion: The AURA system effectively addresses generalization challenges in email threat detection through its adaptive uncertainty-routing approach, showing strong performance both in-distribution and under genuine distribution shift on real-world datasets spanning years of adversarial campaigns.

Abstract: Email spam and phishing attacks remain a critical security threat. Adversaries increasingly exploit large language models to craft contextually convincing malicious messages, and existing spam detection systems often struggle to keep pace. Generalization across diverse and evolving attack scenarios is limited, which reduces effectiveness once these systems are deployed in practice. This paper introduces Adaptive Uncertainty-Routed Analysis (AURA), a multimodal email threat detection system that analyzes both the content of an email and its embedded URLs. AURA is built around two layers: the first quantifies prediction uncertainty from a URL classifier, and only ambiguous messages are escalated to a fine-tuned transformer encoder for semantic analysis. The system is evaluated on eight heterogeneous training corpora together with two held-out real-world corpora spanning a decade of adversarial campaigns. AURA reaches a macro F1-score of 0.9858 in-distribution, and on NazPhish-Eval and GuenterTrap-Eval it maintains 0.9502 and 0.9436, respectively, which is evidence of robust generalization under genuine distribution shift.

</details>


### [128] [Uni-LaDiR: Latent Diffusion Unifies Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19878)
*Haoqiang Kang,Yizhe Zhang,Nikki Lijing Kuang,Yian Ma,Lianhui Qin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Uni-LaDiR: A framework that uses unified latent space and diffusion models for multimodal reasoning across vision-language and vision-language-action tasks, achieving significant performance gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multimodal reasoning methods concatenate modality-specific thought tokens, forcing models to bridge representational gaps during reasoning. There's a need for better integration of different modalities throughout the reasoning process.
