Result: SoftTri improves optimization stability and approximation accuracy compared to classical triangular MFs, achieves performance comparable to or better than Gaussian MFs, and provides C∞ smoothness with respect to inputs and membership parameters.

Conclusion: SoftTri offers an effective compromise between interpretability and differentiable optimization in modern neuro-fuzzy learning systems, enabling efficient gradient-based training while preserving triangular MF properties.

Abstract: Triangular membership functions (MFs) are widely used in fuzzy systems because of their interpretability, low parameterization complexity, and strong locality properties. However, their inherent nondifferentiability at knot points limits the effectiveness of gradient-based optimization in adaptive neuro-fuzzy architectures, often necessitating subgradient approximations or heuristic smoothing techniques. In this paper, we propose \emph{SoftTri}, a differentiable triangular membership function constructed using a smooth soft-hinge mechanism inspired by Swish-type activations. The proposed formulation preserves the geometric structure and localized behavior of classical triangular MFs while providing $C^\infty$ smoothness with respect to both the input variable and the membership parameters $(a,b,c)$ for any finite sharpness parameter $β>0$. Closed-form analytical gradients are derived to enable efficient and fully differentiable backpropagation-based learning. SoftTri is integrated into a Takagi--Sugeno fuzzy neural network with grid-partitioned rules and evaluated on multiple one-dimensional and two-dimensional nonlinear approximation benchmarks as well as a real-world regression task using the Airfoil Self-Noise dataset. Experimental results demonstrate that SoftTri consistently improves optimization stability and approximation accuracy compared with classical triangular membership functions, while achieving performance comparable to or better than Gaussian MFs under identical rule structures and training settings. The proposed approach provides an effective compromise between interpretability and differentiable optimization in modern neuro-fuzzy learning systems.

</details>


### [154] [Evaluating Financial Sentiment in the Age of AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20198)
*Arslan Bisharat,Oudom Hean*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: General-purpose LLMs match finance-specific models for sentiment classification but don't improve economic prediction of stock returns; sentiment captures earnings information but not short-term market reactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite widespread use of financial sentiment measures, it's unclear whether general-purpose LLMs improve on existing finance-specific methods, necessitating systematic evaluation of different sentiment model types.

Method: Evaluated 12 sentiment models (dictionary-based, finance-specific transformers, open-source LLMs) using two criteria: linguistic validity and economic validity, analyzing classification performance and economic relationships.

Result: General-purpose LLMs achieve classification performance comparable to finance-specific transformers without fine-tuning, but higher accuracy doesn't translate to stronger economic relationships. Several models associate with earnings surprises, but none significantly associate with next-day stock returns. Performance strongest for large earnings beats/misses, weaker for moderate surprises.

Conclusion: Financial sentiment captures information about firm economic performance (earnings) but has limited ability to explain short-run market reactions, suggesting caution in using sentiment models for predicting immediate stock price movements.

Abstract: Financial sentiment measures are widely used in empirical finance, but it remains unclear whether general-purpose large language models (LLMs) improve on existing finance-specific methods. This paper evaluates twelve sentiment models, including dictionary-based methods, finance-specific transformers, and open-source LLMs, using two criteria: linguistic validity and economic validity. We find that general-purpose LLMs achieve classification performance comparable to finance-specific transformer models without task-specific fine-tuning. However, higher classification accuracy does not translate into stronger economic relationships. Several models produce sentiment measures that are significantly associated with earnings surprises, but none is significantly associated with next-day stock returns. Model performance is strongest for announcements with large earnings beats or misses and substantially weaker for announcements with more moderate earnings surprises. These findings suggest that financial sentiment captures information about firms' economic performance but has limited ability to explain short-run market reactions

</details>


### [155] [Towards a Unified Modality-Agnostic Multimodal Framework for Cognitive Workload Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20199)
*Stefanos Gkikas,Christian Arzate Cruz,Calvin Joseph,Giorgos Giannakakis,Raul Fernandez Rojas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A unified Transformer architecture processes multiple biosignal modalities for cognitive workload assessment, finding EEG most effective and that adding more modalities doesn't consistently improve performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While biosignals are widely used to measure cognitive workload, there's limited research on combining multiple heterogeneous modalities. The authors aim to develop a unified framework to process different biosignals together and understand their synergistic effects.

Method: Developed a modality-agnostic hierarchical Transformer architecture that can process heterogeneous biosignals in a single model. Tested all 31 possible combinations of five modalities (ECG, EDA, RESP, SpO2, EEG) using leave-one-subject-out validation across three cognitive tasks: abstract reasoning (IQ), arithmetic (MATH), and a game (GAME).

Result: EEG was the strongest single modality, performing best in IQ, GAME, and pooled ALL settings. Adding more modalities didn't consistently improve performance. The full five-modality combination achieved highest average scores of 73.02% on IQ and 68.08% across all four evaluation settings. The method reduced model size by ~50% compared to late-fusion alternatives with lower inference time.

Conclusion: The proposed unified Transformer architecture successfully processes heterogeneous biosignals for cognitive workload assessment, with EEG being the most informative modality. The approach offers computational efficiency benefits over traditional fusion methods while demonstrating that multimodal combinations don't necessarily outperform the best single modality.

Abstract: Cognitive workload reflects the mental effort required during task performance and is central to the design of adaptive human-machine systems. The use of biosignals to measure cognitive workload has been extensively researched and documented; however, studies examining the effects of combining heterogeneous biosignal modalities for this purpose remain limited. To provide insight into this area, we developed a unified, modality-agnostic, hierarchical Transformer-based architecture to process heterogeneous biosignal modalities within a single model. We use this framework in a pilot study evaluating all $31$ possible combinations of five modalities: Electrocardiogram (ECG), Electrodermal Activity (EDA), Respiration (RESP), Peripheral Oxygen Saturation (SpO$_2$), and Electroencephalogram (EEG), under leave-one-subject-out validation across three cognitively distinct tasks: abstract reasoning (IQ), arithmetic problem solving (MATH), and a game task (GAME). In this pilot setting, the results suggest that: (i) EEG is the strongest single modality, ranking highest in IQ, GAME, and the pooled ALL setting, where samples from all three tasks are combined; (ii) adding more modalities does not consistently improve performance; (iii) the full five-modality combination achieves the highest \textit{Average} score of $73.02%$ on IQ and $68.08%$ when the \textit{Average} scores are averaged over the four evaluation settings: IQ, MATH, GAME, and ALL; and (iv) the proposed method reduces model size by approximately $50%$ compared with late-fusion alternatives while maintaining a lower inference time.

</details>


### [156] [Scene-Conditioned Relation Routing for urban cellular activity forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20209)
*Qingzhong Li,Jingye Lin,Hui Ma,Yajun Zhang,Xinjun Pei,Ming Yan,Fei Xing*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SCRR-Net is a scene-conditioned spatial relation routing framework for urban cellular activity forecasting that uses urban contextual information to dynamically control spatial dependency selection and cross-task knowledge transfer, outperforming existing methods on SMS, network traffic, and call activity prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for urban cellular activity forecasting separate temporal modeling, spatial relation learning, and multi-signal prediction, relying on fixed graph structures or static multi-task learning schemes. This limits adaptability to changing urban scenes and heterogeneous spatiotemporal signals like SMS usage, mobile network traffic, and call activity.

Method: Proposes SCRR-Net with four main components: 1) context encoder, 2) spatial graph expert routing module, 3) temporal Transformer encoder, and 4) task knowledge routing module. The framework uses urban contextual information to jointly control spatial dependency selection and cross-task knowledge transfer through dynamic routing mechanisms.

Result: Experiments on Milano and Trento datasets show SCRR-Net consistently outperforms competing methods on forecasting SMS usage, network traffic, and call activity. The model also provides interpretable routing behaviors.

Conclusion: SCRR-Net successfully addresses limitations of existing methods by introducing scene-conditioned routing that adapts to changing urban contexts, achieving superior performance in multi-signal cellular activity forecasting while offering interpretability through its routing mechanisms.

Abstract: Urban cellular activity forecasting requires jointly modeling heterogeneous spatiotemporal signals, including SMS usage, mobile network traffic, and call activity. Existing methods often separate temporal modeling, spatial relation learning, and multi-signal prediction, relying on fixed graph structures or static multi-task learning schemes, which limits their adaptability to changing urban scenes. We propose SCRR-Net, a scene-conditioned spatial relation routing framework in which urban contextual information jointly controls spatial dependency selection and cross-task knowledge transfer. SCRR-Net includes a context encoder, a spatial graph expert routing module, a temporal Transformer encoder, and a task knowledge routing module. Experiments on the Milano and Trento datasets demonstrate that SCRR-Net consistently outperforms competing methods on SMS, network traffic, and call activity forecasting, while providing interpretable routing behaviors.

</details>


### [157] [Subdomain-aware representation compression for pretrained image embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20213)
*Poowanut Niamluang,Jittat Fakcharoenphol*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper shows that applying dimensionality reduction (PCA/LDA) to subdomains rather than whole datasets improves space efficiency and accuracy for edge ML, while enabling transfer learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional dimensionality reduction is applied uniformly across entire datasets. The paper investigates using it specifically for subdomain representation compression to benefit edge-device ML applications needing space and computational efficiency.

Method: Used standard dimensionality reduction techniques like Principal Component Analysis (PCA) and Linear Discriminant Analysis (LDA) applied to subdomains in image domains, and tested transfer learning with compressed representations.

Result: Results show expected improvements in space and computation complexity (crucial for edge devices), but unexpectedly also show accuracy improvements over direct full-embedding procedures. Dimensionality reduction effectively extracts subdomain features. Transfer learning experiments with compressed representations were also successful.

Conclusion: Subdomain-specific dimensionality reduction is effective for edge ML applications, offering both efficiency gains and improved accuracy by better feature extraction, while maintaining transfer learning capabilities.

Abstract: Dimensionality reduction is a well-known technique for improving space efficiency, typically applied uniformly across an entire dataset. This paper investigates the possibilities of using dimensionality reduction techniques for subdomain representation compression. We explore standard techniques such as Principal Component Analysis (PCA) and Linear discriminant analysis (LDA) in image domains. The results not only demonstrate the expected improvements in space and computation complexity crucial for edge-device ML applications but also show improvements in accuracy over direct full-embedding procedure. One possible explanation is that dimensionality reduction effectively extracts subdomain features. We also performed experiments to demonstrate transfer learning capabilities using the compressed representations.

</details>


### [158] [Explaining spatial information flow in short-term traffic forecasting models using a gated graph attention network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20217)
*Yue Li,Shujuan Chen,Ying Jin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A gated graph attention network (GAT) for traffic forecasting that learns when to use neighbor information vs. self-information, providing interpretable spatial attention patterns and revealing architectural redundancies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current GAT models for traffic forecasting claim to capture spatial dependencies through attention weights, but these cannot be verified against measurable quantities. This leaves model explainability and component necessity uncertain.

Method: Adds a learnable gate to GAT layers that determines, at each sensor and time step, what proportion of updated state comes from neighbors vs. itself. Uses regularization for graded ablation and applies this gated GAT to ST-MetaNet architecture with encoder/decoder GAT layers between recurrent layers.

Result: The gate shows higher neighbor information share for heavier traffic sensors, following daily/weekly travel cycles. Mild regularization slightly improves accuracy; stronger regularization degrades it. Encoder gates close before decoder gates. Removing either GAT layer alone maintains accuracy, but removing both degrades it significantly, revealing redundancy rather than indispensability.

Conclusion: The gated GAT provides modest accuracy improvement, interpretable spatial information flow patterns (when/where neighbors matter), and evidence about architectural requirements - showing that two GAT layers are largely redundant rather than essential.

Abstract: Short-term traffic forecasting supports real-time monitoring and control of road networks, and graph attention networks (GAT) are the standard means of representing spatial dependence in these models. GAT layers are widely described as capturing the influence of neighbouring locations, but this is seldom verified, because the attention weights offered in support cannot be compared against any measured quantity. That leaves two questions open, how the model should be explained and which of its components are necessary. We address this by adding a gate to the GAT layer which learns, at every sensor and every time step, what share of a sensor's updated state is drawn from its neighbours rather than from itself. Regularising the gate withdraws neighbour information progressively and thereby provides a graded form of ablation. We apply the gated GAT to ST-MetaNet, whose encoder and decoder each place one GAT layer between two recurrent layers, and train it on one calendar year of records from 498 loop detectors on the strategic road network of England. The gate assigns a larger share of neighbour information to sensors carrying heavier traffic and follows the daily and weekly cycle of travel, consistent with adjacent locations being more strongly coupled when busy. Mild regularisation improves accuracy slightly, and accuracy declines at higher strengths as the penalty withdraws information the model needs. The encoder gate closes before the decoder gate, but direct ablation qualifies that ordering. Removing either GAT layer alone leaves accuracy at least as good as keeping both, whereas removing both degrades it substantially, so the two layers are largely redundant rather than either being indispensable. The gated GAT therefore yields a modest accuracy gain, an explanation of where and when spatial information flows, and evidence on which layers the architecture requires.

</details>


### [159] [Is It Still Worth Training a Classical Model in the Era of LLMs? A Crossover Benchmark on Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20218)
*Kaihua Ding*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: For tabular data labeling, trained classical models (like gradient boosting) typically outperform frozen LLMs with surprisingly small amounts of training data, with median crossover at ~6% of the training set.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: With LLMs now being integrated into spreadsheet tools for zero-shot labeling, there's a practical question: should businesses use frozen LLMs or collect data and train classical models for expensive labeling tasks?

Method: Conducted 126 independent student evaluations of small GPT models across 8 prompting configurations and 18 tabular datasets, combined with power-law learning curves for 6 classical model families to find the labeled-data crossover point N*.

Result: Training wins fast: classical models beat frozen LLMs using no more labeled data than already available in 86% of cases, with median crossover at ~6% of training set. Few-shot examples don't follow power-law improvements like training does.

Conclusion: For typical business tables, collect a few hundred labels and train a gradient-boosted model rather than relying on frozen LLMs, as classical training outperforms with minimal data.

Abstract: Large language models can label a tabular row from a plain-English description with no training - a capability now shipping in mainstream spreadsheet tools such as Microsoft Copilot in Excel and Anthropic's Claude for Excel - raising a practical question for the many business prediction problems where labels are expensive: should you prompt a frozen LLM, or collect data and train a model - and if so, how much data? We quantify the answer with the labeled-data crossover N*, the training-set size at which a trained classical model's learning curve overtakes a frozen LLM's training-free (and therefore flat) error. Aggregating 126 independent student evaluations of small GPT models under eight prompting configurations across 18 tabular datasets, paired with authoritative power-law learning curves for six classical model families, we find that training wins fast: even given an oracle choice of its best prompt configuration, a trained classical model beats the small frozen LLM using no more labeled data than is already on hand in 86% of cases, and wins by the smallest labeled subset we evaluate in 40%, with the observed crossover at a median of ~6% of the training set. In-context few-shot examples do not behave like training - error versus shot count does not follow a power law - and the same protocol re-run by independent implementers varies with a coefficient of variation of 0.148. A controlled probe indicates the LLM depends on recognizable feature-name semantics, which plausibly makes our crossover a conservative estimate (we do not claim memorization). For a typical business table, the evidence is clear: collect a few hundred labels and train a gradient-boosted model.

</details>


### [160] [Accuracy Is Not Enough: A Cross-Architecture Audit of Demographic Bias in Deep Knowledge Tracing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20249)
*Dang Quang Minh,Nguyen Dung Son,Nguyen Huu Loi,Truong Viet Vu,Nguyen Thai Anh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper conducts the first comprehensive fairness audit of deep knowledge tracing models, finding significant demographic bias that varies by context and architecture, with the most accurate model (AKT) showing the highest bias, and standard mitigation techniques proving ineffective.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep knowledge tracing (DKT) models are widely used in adaptive learning systems to infer student mastery, but there has been no comprehensive cross-architecture audit of their demographic fairness, unlike Bayesian knowledge tracing models which have been studied. The paper aims to close this research gap by systematically evaluating fairness across different DKT architectures and mitigation strategies.

Method: The study evaluates four DKT architectures (DKT, DKVMN, SAKT, AKT) trained under three regimes (standard training, reweighting, adversarial debiasing) on two public datasets with demographic metadata (Eedi with 15.9M interactions and OULAD with 167k after preprocessing). They use multiple fairness evaluation metrics: ABROCA, student-level bootstrap confidence intervals, and permutation tests to address critiques of fairness-metric instability. They also conduct ablation studies to identify sources of bias.

Result: Three key findings: (1) Bias is real but context-dependent - all architectures show significant socioeconomic ABROCA on Eedi (0.018-0.023, p<0.005), while gender bias is significant on OULAD for 3 of 4 architectures but negligible on Eedi. (2) The most accurate architecture (AKT) is also the most biased - AKT shows about 4 AUC points improvement from item-level Rasch embeddings but also has the largest socioeconomic ABROCA, and ablation removes both the accuracy gain and excess bias. (3) Standard mitigation techniques are unreliable - reweighting and adversarial debiasing leave ABROCA essentially unchanged when preserving accuracy, despite the adversary reaching chance performance at full strength.

Conclusion: This study provides the first comprehensive fairness audit of deep knowledge tracing models, revealing significant demographic biases that vary by context and architecture. The findings challenge assumptions about fairness in educational AI, showing that state-of-the-art accuracy improvements may come at the cost of increased bias, and current mitigation strategies are insufficient. The paper establishes a methodology for DKT fairness evaluation and highlights the need for more effective debiasing approaches.

Abstract: Deep knowledge tracing (DKT) models implicitly decide which students an adaptive system believes have mastered a skill, yet almost all evidence on their demographic fairness comes from Bayesian knowledge tracing; the deep models that power modern systems have received no comparable cross-architecture audit. We close this gap: four architectures (DKT, DKVMN, SAKT, AKT) trained under three regimes (standard, reweighting, adversarial) on two public datasets with demographic metadata, Eedi (15.9M interactions) and OULAD (167k after preprocessing), evaluated with ABROCA, student-level bootstrap confidence intervals, and permutation tests addressing recent critiques of fairness-metric instability. Three findings emerge. (i) Bias is real but context-dependent: every architecture shows a significant socioeconomic ABROCA on Eedi (0.018-0.023, $p<0.005$), with per-group AUC lower for economically disadvantaged students, while gender bias is significant on OULAD for three of four architectures after multiplicity correction yet negligible on Eedi. (ii) The most accurate architecture is the most biased: AKT gains about 4 AUC points from item-level Rasch embeddings and shows the largest socioeconomic ABROCA, exceeding every other architecture under a paired bootstrap ($p\leq0.002$); ablating only the Rasch embeddings removes the accuracy gain and the excess bias together. (iii) Standard mitigation is unreliable: reweighting and adversarial debiasing leave ABROCA essentially unchanged in every configuration that preserves accuracy, even though the adversary is pinned at chance at full reversal strength and a weak-strength positive control rules out a dead probe.

</details>


### [161] [How Far Can Sub-3B Open Language Models Go in Zero-Shot Essay Scoring on an 8 GB Consumer GPU?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20250)
*Nguyen Dung Son,Dang Quang Minh,Nguyen Huu Loi,Truong Viet Vu,Nguyen Thai Anh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Sub-3B open LLMs can perform zero-shot essay scoring locally with privacy-preserving deployment, achieving modest results (best QWK 0.388) that remain far below human inter-rater reliability (0.769), suitable only for formative feedback with human supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automated essay scoring often uses proprietary API models, but educational settings with strict privacy rules (like public schools) cannot send student writing to third-party APIs. This creates a need for small, local models that can run fully on consumer hardware while preserving privacy.

Method: Controlled study of four instruction-tuned open models (Qwen2.5 at 0.5B/1.5B/3B and SmolLM2 at 1.7B) on all eight ASAP-AES prompts using an 8GB consumer GPU. Experiments include bootstrap confidence intervals, Holm-corrected paired tests, and deployment-realistic design variants. Key techniques tested: rubric-decomposed vs holistic prompting, batch min-max aggregation vs mean aggregation, and Multi-Trait Specialization normalization for grader calibration.

Result: Three main findings: (1) Rubric-decomposed prompting with batch min-max aggregation consistently beats holistic prompting across models, though Qwen2.5-3B shows significant drop on one prompt. (2) Mapping trait scores is fragile to grader calibration - Multi-Trait Specialization normalization repairs naive mean aggregation collapse (QWK 0.204 to 0.388). (3) Signed error decreases with essay length (opposite to verbosity bias in large LLMs), normalized rubric decomposition flattens this slope for well-calibrated models. Best local configuration achieves QWK 0.388 vs human ceiling 0.769.

Conclusion: Sub-3B local models can provide modest essay scoring capabilities suitable for formative feedback with human supervision, but remain far below human inter-rater reliability and even basic baselines. Privacy-preserving local deployment is viable but current performance levels position these models strictly for human-supervised educational applications rather than high-stakes automated scoring.

Abstract: Zero-shot essay scoring with large language models is usually demonstrated with proprietary API models, yet the settings where automated scoring is most needed, such as public schools grading thousands of essays under strict privacy rules, are often those where sending student writing to a third-party API is unacceptable. We ask how much capability survives when the model must be a sub-3B open model running fully locally in FP16, with a controlled study of four instruction-tuned models from two families (Qwen2.5 at 0.5B/1.5B/3B, SmolLM2 at 1.7B) on all eight ASAP-AES prompts on a single 8 GB consumer GPU, with bootstrap confidence intervals, Holm-corrected paired tests, and deployment-realistic variants of the key design choices. Three findings emerge. (i) Rubric-decomposed prompting beats holistic prompting for every model under batch min-max aggregation (though Qwen2.5-3B drops significantly on one prompt), and under mean aggregation two unrelated families land within 0.01 at the 1.5-1.7B scale. (ii) Mapping trait scores into the prompt range is fragile to grader calibration: one model compresses traits into a narrow low band (2-4 on 0-10) and naive mean aggregation collapses, while the min-max normalization of Multi-Trait Specialization repairs it (macro QWK 0.204 to 0.388) and stays within 0.03 when its statistics are frozen on 30 held-out essays. (iii) Signed error falls with essay length in eleven of twelve configurations, opposite to the verbosity bias reported for large LLM judges; normalized rubric decomposition largely flattens this slope for well-calibrated models. We anchor results honestly: the best local configuration (0.388) remains far below both the human inter-rater ceiling (0.769) and a length-only baseline (0.523), so we position sub-3B local models strictly for formative, human-supervised feedback.

</details>


### [162] [Improving Online Reinforcement Learning via Bidirectional Behavior Prior Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20268)
*Gong Gao,Xiao Lai,Jiaji Shen,Ning Jia,Xianhui Liu,Weidong Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: B2PD uses bidirectional behavior prior distillation to improve online RL sample efficiency by generating high-value behavior support sets and reducing critic estimation errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Online RL suffers from poor sample efficiency and unstable learning due to critic estimation errors amplified by greedy policy updates. Existing behavior-prior methods rely on limited-quality offline datasets that can't provide effective high-value policy guidance, especially without expert trajectories.

Method: Proposes Bidirectional Behavior Prior Distillation (B2PD) algorithm that uses action-value priors to guide a conditional variational autoencoder (CVAE) to generate high-value behavior support sets. These expert behavior priors are distilled into the agent, creating bidirectional knowledge flow to reduce inefficient exploration and enable stable policy optimization.

Result: Empirical evaluations on both state- and pixel-based tasks show B2PD substantially improves sample efficiency while maintaining stable policy optimization.

Conclusion: Enforcing high-quality behavioral support during online learning effectively mitigates critic-induced error amplification, enabling structured behavior priors to guide policy updates in a principled and sample-efficient manner.

Abstract: Online reinforcement learning (RL) algorithms frequently exhibit poor sample efficiency and unstable learning dynamics, stemming from systematic critic estimation errors that are exacerbated by greedy policy updates. Existing behavior-prior reinforcement learning methods attempt to alleviate this issue by relying on offline pre-training to learn behavior models from fixed datasets and using policy priors to constrain online policy updates. However, the limited quality of offline datasets often hinders the ability to provide high-value policies that can effectively guide policy updates. The absence of expert trajectories significantly impairs online policy learning, leading to low sample efficiency and suboptimal performance. To address these challenges, we depart from conventional behavior prior approaches and propose a Bidirectional Behavior Prior Distillation (B2PD) algorithm. B2PD leverages action-value priors to guide a conditional variational autoencoder (CVAE) in generating a high-value behavior support set. The resulting expert behavior priors are further distilled into the agent, effectively reducing inefficient exploration and enabling stable policy optimization, while establishing a bidirectional knowledge flow mechanism. Empirical evaluations on both state- and pixel-based tasks verify that B2PD substantially improves sample efficiency while maintaining stable policy optimization. More broadly, this work shows that enforcing high-quality behavioral support during online learning effectively mitigates critic-induced error amplification, enabling structured behavior priors to guide policy updates in a principled and sample-efficient manner.

</details>


### [163] [Placement Is Free, Composition Is Not: The Latin Square as a Provably-Balanced Construction for Heterogeneous Sequence-Mixer Stacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20269)
*Taebong Kim,Youngsik Hong,Minsik Kim,Sunyoung Choi,Jaewon Jang,Minseo Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces Aether-7B-5Attn, a mixture-of-experts model with multiple attention mechanisms arranged in a Latin square pattern, showing that heterogeneous composition across layers is more important than specific placement order.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most Transformers use the same attention mechanism at every layer without testing whether this is optimal. The authors investigate whether combining multiple sequence-mixing mechanisms in different arrangements could improve performance.

Method: Created a 6.59B-parameter MoE model with 49 layers containing 7 different sequence-mixing mechanisms arranged as a 7×7 Latin square (each mechanism appears exactly once per row and column). Built smaller proxy models with 4 mechanisms in 4×4 Latin squares, plus variations: homogeneous stacks, clustered mechanisms, and heterogeneous stacks with different arrangements.

Result: Exact placement of mechanisms has minimal effect (0.16% validation loss change). Clustering mechanisms into contiguous bands hurts performance (0.59% penalty). Homogeneous stacks perform much worse (1.68-2.63% penalty). Removing certain mechanisms (SSM-family) causes significant degradation (3.20%).

Conclusion: Transformer performance depends primarily on having heterogeneous composition of sequence-mixing mechanisms distributed across depth, rather than on any specific permutation or placement order. Multiple mechanisms provide complementary benefits that homogeneous attention cannot match.

Abstract: Since GPT, most Transformers have repeated the same attention mechanism at every layer. Yet this design is largely a convention rather than a tested conclusion. When multiple sequence mixers are combined in one stack, improvements may arise from mechanism choice, placement, or both, making causal attribution difficult. We introduce Aether-7B-5Attn, a 6.59B-parameter mixture-of-experts model ($\approx$2.98B active) whose 49 layers contain seven sequence-mixing mechanisms arranged as a $7\times7$ Latin square. Because each mechanism appears exactly once in every row and column, the design guarantees balanced exposure across depth while eliminating placement confounds. To evaluate this principle, we build a parameter-matched proxy with four mechanisms arranged as a $4\times4$ Latin square over sixteen layers, matched to 700.9M parameters and trained with eight seeds per arm. The results reveal a clear dissociation. Rearranging a distributed heterogeneous stack into a balanced periodic cycle changes validation loss by only 0.16\%, indicating that exact placement has little effect. In contrast, clustering the same mechanisms into contiguous depth bands incurs a 0.59\% penalty, while replacing the heterogeneous stack with a homogeneous one incurs a 1.68\% penalty. These results indicate that performance depends primarily on heterogeneous composition distributed across depth rather than on any particular permutation. We confirm this finding at 2.16$\times$ larger scale (1.514B parameters), where the homogeneous-stack penalty increases to 2.63\% and removing the SSM-family mechanism produces a 3.20\% degradation. We further report per-mechanism cost profiles, English and Korean evaluations, and a causal-safety audit of all 49 layers. We release model weights, training recipes, training code, logs, and architecture source code.

</details>


### [164] [Labeled Incidence Structures for Native Transformer Modeling of Text, Knowledge Graphs, and Hypergraphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20278)
*Mahesh Godavarti*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A unified representation called labeled incidence structures (LIS) enables transformers to natively process text, knowledge graphs, and hypergraphs by encoding endpoints as (content, role, relation) triples, with structural differences handled through operators rather than architectural changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current approaches flatten structural data like knowledge graphs and hypergraphs into token sequences, losing important information about distinct roles that elements play within relation instances. This structural information is crucial for proper representation and processing of complex data types.

Method: Introduces labeled incidence structures (LIS) that represent each endpoint as (content, slot/role, relation instance). Uses structural addressing through composition of slot and instance operators (A(s,e) = R_s R_e) and relative transport (P_{j→i} = A_i^{-1} A_j) for attention, providing role- and relation-aware inductive bias without imposing sequence order.

Result: Proves that additive encodings (position term + relation term) cannot capture joint dependencies between slots and relation instances. For knowledge repositories, shows that computing relation-instance operators from content avoids storage-order sensitivity issues and yields capacity bounds independent of repository size M.

Conclusion: LIS provides a uniform representation that enables a single transformer to natively process multiple data types while preserving structural information. The approach offers principled solutions to structural representation challenges in transformers and provides theoretical guarantees about capacity and information preservation.

Abstract: Text, knowledge graphs, and hypergraphs all have elements that play distinct roles within relation instances, structure that is lost when data is flattened into token sequences. We introduce labeled incidence structures (LIS), a uniform representation that encodes each endpoint as $(x_d, s, e)$: content $x_d$, a role or slot $s$, and the relation instance $e$ in which that role appears. Because every data type maps to the same $(x_d, s, e)$ representation without flattening, a single standard transformer can process them all natively, structural differences are carried entirely by the operators, not the architecture.
  LIS assigns a structural address to each endpoint by composing a slot operator and an instance operator, $A(s,e) = R_s R_e$. We characterize when this factorization gives every token a unique, path-independent address. When it does, the natural operator comparing endpoint $j$ to endpoint $i$ is the relative transport $P_{j\to i} = A_i^{-1} A_j$, which gives attention a role- and relation-aware inductive bias without imposing an arbitrary sequence order.
  Additive encodings of the form "position term plus relation term" can miss information that depends jointly on $s$ and $e$. We prove this in a controlled example family: when the journey operator is approximated by the sum of a position-only term and a relation-only term, the approximation cannot capture how position and relation combine, only their separate effects.
  We also analyze persistent knowledge repositories. Identifiers tied to storage locations make models sensitive to storage order, while freely learned identifiers can become harder to control as the repository size $M$ grows relative to the sample size $n$. Computing relation-instance operators from content avoids this storage-order issue and yields a capacity bound independent of $M$, under fixed architectural and Lipschitz assumptions.

</details>


### [165] [Personalising a Cross-User Surface Electromyography Encoder Under a Small Calibration Budget](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20296)
*Jethro Odeyemi,W. J. Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Researchers compared four methods for personalizing myoelectric interfaces using limited calibration data, finding that full fine-tuning is most accurate but a gradient-free prototypical method achieves most benefits without optimization or per-user weight copies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing myoelectric interface calibration treats calibration as a quantity but doesn't address what to do with calibration repetitions after collection. There's a need to understand how to best use limited calibration data for personalization, as the common intuition that only a fresh classifier is needed appears incorrect.

Method: Compared four alternative approaches using exactly the same labeled repetitions from a single cross-user encoder per held-out subject: prototypical adaptation, linear probes, scaled fine-tuning, and full fine-tuning. Tested at every budget up to maximum allowed (5 reps on DB1, 4 on DB2 and DB5) across 77 subjects total.

Result: Full fine-tuning was the most accurate at every budget, with no exceptions among subject subsets. However, a gradient-free prototypical rule recovered 52-78% of full fine-tuning's benefit without requiring optimization or per-user weight copies, making personalization feasible for worn devices at donning time.

Conclusion: Personalization of cross-user encoders involves design decisions with cost trade-offs. While full fine-tuning is most accurate, gradient-free prototypical adaptation offers substantial practical benefits for wearable applications. The common belief that good representations only need fresh classifiers is incorrect in this context.

Abstract: A myoelectric interface needs calibration from the user before it will function. Earlier work has treated calibration as a quantity, but has not asked the question of what a device should do with the calibration repetitions once they have been collected. This paper views personalizing the cross-user encoder as a design decision with a cost. Four alternative approaches to using exactly the same labeled repetitions were tested from a single cross-user encoder per held-out subject. Prototypical adaptation, linear probes, scaled fine-tuning and full fine-tuning were tested at every budget up to the maximum each database allows, five repetitions on DB1 and four on DB2 and DB5. Comparing four ways to spend a small calibration budget across 77 subjects, full fine-tuning is the most accurate at every budget, consistently enough that there is no exception among subsets of subjects. The result which impacts how one might make an engineering decision however is that a gradient free prototypical rule recovers 52 to 78 per cent of its benefit with no optimiser and no per-user copy of the weights, which makes personalisation something a worn device can do at donning time. The widespread intuition that a good representation only needs a fresh classifier is incorrect here. How well each method may perform relative to a per-user classifier that would be fitted by a clinic will depend on the specific database.

</details>


### [166] [Intact-to-Amputee Transfer in Surface-EMG Gesture Decoding: Training Source and Calibration Budget](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20297)
*Jethro Odeyemi,W. J. Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A cross-user EMG encoder initially fails with amputees but outperforms per-user classifiers after minimal labeled calibration, showing intact-limb training transfers better than amputee-only training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EMG-based prosthetics require per-user calibration which is burdensome for amputees. Systematic review shows limited evidence for amputee applications (only ~17% of studies), creating need for effective cross-user transfer methods.

Method: Developed montage-agnostic cross-user encoder tested on 11 transradial amputees using protocol matched to intact-limb training. Compared zero-shot transfer vs. per-user classifiers. Trained on 40 intact subjects vs. 10 amputees vs. combination.

Result: Zero-shot cross-population transfer fails completely. With just 3 labeled repetitions, encoder outperforms per-user classifiers by 0.190 macro F1 (0.779 vs 0.589). Training on intact subjects transfers better than on other amputees; combined training performs best. Pre-registered prediction about EMG separability only holds after few repetitions and enriched amputee source data.

Conclusion: Cross-user encoders can work for amputees but require minimal labeled data (3 repetitions). Training on both intact and amputee data yields best transfer. The baseline-strength account has boundaries - it only applies after certain conditions, highlighting limitations of current theoretical models for amputee EMG decoding.

Abstract: A recogniser trained on one person rarely transfers to the next, and useful performance usually demands a fresh round of labelled calibration from the end user. A systematic review of 1077 studies quantifies where the evidence is thin: amputees appear in about one in six. Here a montage-agnostic cross-user encoder is carried to eleven transradial amputees on a protocol matched to its intact-limb training data. Zero-shot cross-population transfer fails outright: the encoder requires labeled data from the new user before it begins decoding, and it then exceeds the per-user classifier a clinic would fit by 0.190 macro F1 at three repetitions and for every subject in the cohort. Given three labelled repetitions it reaches 0.779 macro-F1 against 0.589 for the per-user pipeline. Training on forty intact subjects produces better transfers to a new amputee than training on ten other amputees, and combining the two produces better transfers than either individually. The prediction pre-registered for this study, which extends the encoder's baseline-strength account with the premise that amputee EMG is less separable, holds true only after a few repetitions become available and after enriching the source pool with additional amputees. At a single repetition, and at every budget under a source matched to the intact-limb comparison, it fails. Thus, it locates the boundary of the proposed account.

</details>


### [167] [A Learning Algorithm for Threshold Boolean Networks with Prescribed Fixed Points](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20298)
*Gonzalo A. Ruz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A learning algorithm for threshold Boolean networks that successfully reconstructs prescribed fixed points while avoiding spurious attractors, outperforming standard methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The need for algorithms that can accurately infer Boolean networks with specific dynamical properties, particularly prescribed fixed points, without introducing unwanted spurious attractors that can occur with standard methods like Perceptron and Logistic Regression.

Method: Proposes a custom differentiable loss function with four components: fixed point preservation, spurious attractor penalty, binary output encouragement, and L1 regularization for sparsity. The approach is applied to the FOS-GRN model of Arabidopsis thaliana.

Result: Achieved perfect reconstruction (all 10 desired fixed points, no spurious ones) in 5/30 runs, with average recovery of 8.53±0.90 correct fixed points and no spurious attractors. Standard methods recovered up to 10 fixed points but introduced 8-31 spurious ones. Performance remains robust within practical regularization strength ranges (up to λ=0.01).

Conclusion: The proposed algorithm is effective and stable for capturing meaningful network dynamics under prescribed dynamical constraints, successfully balancing fixed point preservation with avoidance of spurious attractors through its custom loss function design.

Abstract: We present a learning algorithm for inferring threshold Boolean networks (TBNs) with a prescribed set of fixed points. The proposed method employs a custom differentiable loss function that jointly enforces fixed point preservation, penalizes spurious attractors, encourages binary outputs, and promotes sparsity through L1 regularization. Applied to the FOS-GRN model of Arabidopsis thaliana, the approach achieved perfect reconstruction (i.e., all 10 desired fixed points and no spurious ones) in 5 out of 30 independent runs, recovering on average 8.53 $\pm$ 0.90 correct fixed points with no spurious attractors. In contrast, standard methods such as the Perceptron and Logistic Regression recovered up to 10 fixed points but introduced between 8 and 31 spurious ones. An additional analysis varying the sparsity coefficient ($λ$) confirmed that the method's performance and the structural properties of the inferred networks remain robust within a practical range (up to 0.01) of regularization strengths. Overall, the results demonstrate the effectiveness and stability of the proposed algorithm in capturing meaningful network dynamics under prescribed dynamical constraints.

</details>


### [168] [Improving Generalization and Robustness in Offline Reinforcement Learning via Boundary-Aware Data Augmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20300)
*Gong Gao,Weidong Zhao,Xianhui Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BADA (Boundary-Aware Data Augmentation) improves offline RL generalization by using neighboring states to create interpolation boundaries that preserve original data distributions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current offline RL algorithms overfit training data and perform poorly in real environments due to limited in-distribution generalization. Existing regularization methods from computer vision are insufficient because low-level physical signals are highly sensitive to distributional shifts.

Method: Theoretical analysis shows error bounds scale with state distance. BADA leverages neighboring states to construct interpolation boundaries, generating synthetic data that preserves original data distribution more faithfully than random interpolation.

Result: Qualitative studies in toy environments show BADA preserves policy smoothness and accurately reconstructs multimodal value distributions. Extensive experiments on limited offline datasets demonstrate state-of-the-art performance across diverse benchmarks.

Conclusion: BADA effectively addresses offline RL's generalization limitations by creating boundary-aware data augmentation that maintains distributional integrity, leading to improved robustness and performance in complex environments.

Abstract: Current offline reinforcement learning (ORL) algorithms tend to overfit the training dataset and exhibit poor in-distribution generalization and robustness performance when deployed to real environments, thus compromising their effectiveness. Existing methods typically enhance in-distribution generalization and robustness by leveraging regularization techniques widely used in computer vision. However, due to the high sensitivity of low-level physical signals to distributional shifts, these methods still suffer from notable limitations in in-distribution generalization and robustness, making it difficult to achieve stable performance in complex environments. To address this issue, we theoretically analyze the error bounds of the behavior policy and action-value function trained with random episode interpolation, revealing that the error scales positively correlated with the distance between states. Based on this insight, we propose a method called $\bf{B}$oundary-$\bf{A}$ware $\bf{D}$ata $\bf{A}$ugmentation (BADA), which leverages neighboring states to construct interpolation boundaries, enabling the generation of synthetic data that more faithfully preserves the original data distribution. We first conduct qualitative studies in a toy environment, showing that BADA generates mixed samples that preserve desirable policy smoothness while accurately reconstructing multimodal value distributions. Extensive experiments on limited offline datasets further demonstrate that BADA attains state-of-the-art performance across diverse benchmarks.

</details>


### [169] [SAGG: Sample-Adaptive Gradient Gating for Robust Multimodal Learning under Heterogeneous Corruption](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20302)
*Wentao Zhang,Yifan Zhu,Yutong Zhang,Wentao Mo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SAGG (Sample-Adaptive Gradient Gating) is a new multimodal learning method that makes per-sample binary decisions to retain or discard modality features based on corruption detection, overcoming limitations of batch-level modulation methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal gradient balancing methods use shared scalar per modality, assuming uniform corruption across batches. In practice, corruption is sample-heterogeneous - different samples in the same batch can have different corrupted modalities, leading to irreducible bias when using batch-level modulation.

Method: Proposed SAGG (Sample-Adaptive Gradient Gating) makes binary retain-or-discard decisions per sample via an online feature-norm quality test with truncation mechanism for variance control. It uses sample-level gating rather than batch-level modulation.

Result: Theoretical analysis shows SAGG-based SGD converges at standard O(1/√T) rate without corruption-dependent error floor. Experiments on Kinetics-Sounds and UCF-101 show SAGG consistently outperforms ten existing methods, with largest gains in high-corruption regimes.

Conclusion: Sample-level adaptive gating is necessary for unbiased gradient estimation under heterogeneous corruption. SAGG provides a theoretically sound and empirically effective solution that outperforms batch-level methods, especially in high-corruption scenarios.

Abstract: Multimodal gradient balancing methods modulate encoder gradients with a shared scalar per modality, implicitly assuming that corruption is uniform across the training batch. In practice, corruption is sample-heterogeneous: within a single mini-batch, different samples may have different modalities corrupted. We prove that under this heterogeneous corruption model, any batch-level sample-agnostic linear estimator with a shared modulation parameter incurs an irreducible bias with respect to the clean-data gradient, and that sample-level all-or-nothing gating is the unique unbiased strategy within a natural distribution-free estimator class. Motivated by this result, we propose Sample-Adaptive Gradient Gating (SAGG), which makes a binary retain-or-discard decision per sample via an online feature-norm quality test and incorporates a truncation mechanism for variance control. We prove that SAGG-based SGD converges at the standard O(1/sqrt(T)) rate to stationary points of the clean loss without a corruption-dependent error floor, and derive a certified robustness radius for the independent-encoder architecture that connects per-modality Lipschitz constants to the classification margin. Experiments on Kinetics-Sounds and UCF-101 under Gaussian noise injection, partial modality missing, and natural contribution imbalance show that SAGG consistently outperforms ten existing methods, with the largest gains in high-corruption regimes where batch-level bias is most severe.

</details>


### [170] [Hypernetwork-Parameterized Spatially Adaptive Neural Operators for PDE Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20309)
*Jiaquan Zhang,Chaoning Zhang,Shuxu Chen,Meng Ye,Xiaofeng Zhang,Qiang He,Weifeng Huang,Guoqing Wang,Yang Yang,Caiyan Qin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SANO introduces a spatially adaptive neural operator that generates location-dependent operator parameters through a coordinate-conditioned hypernetwork, improving performance on heterogeneous PDEs by allowing update rules to vary across space.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing neural operators use spatially shared update rules that may underfit boundaries and high-gradient regions in heterogeneous PDEs, with localized errors accumulating during autoregressive rollout. Hypernetwork methods typically generate only global parameterization per instance, failing to address spatial variations.

Method: SANO uses Fourier-encoded coordinates and a coordinate-conditioned hypernetwork to generate spatial operator-conditioning codes at sampling points. It employs a Hyper-Neural Element (HNE) mechanism to interpolate codes within local subregions and uses partition-of-unity weights to assemble overlapping local predictions.

Result: SANO consistently outperforms competitive neural operator, hypernetwork-based, and physics-informed baselines on one-, two-, and three-dimensional PDEs and two perforated-domain elliptic benchmarks.

Conclusion: By replacing spatially shared parameterization with spatially continuous fields of location-dependent operator parameters, SANO effectively addresses the limitations of existing methods for modeling spatially heterogeneous PDE dynamics.

Abstract: Spatially heterogeneous partial differential equations (PDEs) exhibit location-dependent dynamics arising from variations in geometry and physical coefficients. Existing neural operators improve localized modeling through multiscale features, attention mechanisms, or domain decomposition, yet their update rules often remain spatially shared. Hypernetwork-based methods adapt parameters across PDE instances but typically generate only one global parameterization per instance. Consequently, shared operators may underfit boundaries and high-gradient regions, with these localized errors accumulating during autoregressive rollout. We propose a spatially adaptive neural operator (SANO), which replaces this spatially shared parameterization with a spatially continuous field of location-dependent operator parameters. SANO uses Fourier-encoded coordinates and a coordinate-conditioned hypernetwork to generate spatial operator-conditioning codes at sampling points. A Hyper-Neural Element (HNE) mechanism interpolates these codes within local subregions, coupling neighboring operators while allowing their update rules to vary across space, and partition-of-unity weights assemble the overlapping local predictions. Experiments on one-, two-, and three-dimensional PDEs and two perforated-domain elliptic benchmarks show that SANO consistently outperforms competitive neural-operator, hypernetwork-based, and physics-informed baselines.

</details>


### [171] [ZeroHAT: Behavior-Conditioned Zero-Shot Human Activity Trace Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20310)
*Rongchao Xu,Dahai Yu,Lin Jiang,Guang Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ZeroHAT: A zero-shot framework for generating synthetic human activity traces using cross-region behavioral transfer with contextual adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Access to large-scale human activity traces (HATs) is challenging due to high collection costs and privacy concerns. Existing methods require real data from the same region, which is unavailable for many regions, creating a significant gap in synthetic HAT generation.

Method: ZeroHAT uses a behavior-conditioned framework with three novel components: 1) multidimensional consistency-aware intent extractor, 2) cross-region behavioral cloning module, and 3) behavior-conditioned activity realization module. It transfers behavioral patterns from source regions and adapts them using publicly available contextual information from target regions.

Result: Evaluated on a ten-city benchmark, ZeroHAT achieves 4.5-6.4x normalized downstream utility compared to strongest baselines and improves average fidelity by 15.6%-40.8% across target regions.

Conclusion: ZeroHAT successfully addresses the zero-shot synthetic HAT generation problem by effectively transferring behavioral patterns across regions and adapting them with contextual information, enabling synthetic data generation for regions without real HAT data.

Abstract: Human activity traces record individuals' timestamped visits to points of interest and are essential for applications such as mobility prediction and urban simulation. However, accessing large-scale HATs is challenging due to high collection costs and privacy concerns. Synthetic HAT generation offers a promising way to make such data available and has attracted growing interest from both industry and academia. Although many efforts have been devoted to this topic, most of them rely on real data from a region to generate synthetic data for the same region, which is infeasible for the many regions where real HATs are unavailable. To fill this gap, we propose ZeroHAT, a behavior-conditioned framework that generates synthetic HATs for a target region in a zero-shot manner by transferring behavioral patterns learned from real HATs in source regions and adapting them with publicly available contextual information about the target region. ZeroHAT has three key novel components: (i) a multidimensional consistency-aware intent extractor; (ii) a cross-region behavioral cloning module; and (iii) a behavior-conditioned activity realization module. We evaluate ZeroHAT on a ten-city benchmark, where extensive experiments show that ZeroHAT achieves 4.5-6.4x the normalized downstream utility of the strongest baseline and improves average fidelity by 15.6%-40.8% across target regions.

</details>


### [172] [EviRec: Continual Evidence Learning for Dual Cold-Start POI Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20313)
*Rongchao Xu,Lin Jiang,Guang Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EviRec is a continual evidence-learning framework for POI recommendation that addresses dynamic environments with POI/user turnover by scoring candidates from three views and using reliability gating to adaptively combine evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most POI recommendation methods assume fixed user populations and POI catalogs, but real-world data shows substantial POI churn, user turnover, category drift, and decay in static POI memory, creating a need for continual dual cold-start POI recommendation.

Method: EviRec is a continual evidence-learning framework that estimates trustworthiness of historical evidence per candidate POI. It scores candidates from three views: matching (recent mobility), transition-memory (repeated routines), and lifecycle (candidate maturity). A reliability gate adaptively routes between transition-memory and lifecycle evidence based on observation state.

Result: EviRec outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, with 20.4% improvement in NDCG@10 on Dual-New cases over the strongest baseline. Gains are concentrated on cold-start queries, and analyses confirm improvements come from candidate-specific reliability gating while preserving learned mobility routines.

Conclusion: The EviRec framework effectively addresses the challenges of continual POI recommendation in dynamic environments through its evidence-learning approach with reliability gating, demonstrating strong performance particularly for cold-start scenarios.

Abstract: Point-of-Interest (POI) recommendation is a core task in location-based services, yet most existing methods assume a fixed user population and POI catalog. Through a large-scale data-driven analysis of 10 U.S. cities, we identify substantial POI churn, user turnover, category drift, and decay in static POI memory, motivating the study of continual dual cold-start POI recommendation. To address this setting, we propose EviRec, a continual evidence-learning framework that estimates how much historical evidence should be trusted separately for each candidate POI. EviRec scores each visible candidate from three complementary views: a matching view based on the user's recent mobility profile, a transition-memory view that captures repeated mobility routines, and a lifecycle view that reflects candidate maturity. Because a near-zero transition score may indicate either irrelevance or insufficient observation, EviRec qualifies the evidence using each candidate's observation state and applies a reliability gate to adaptively route between transition-memory and lifecycle evidence. We evaluate EviRec on a full-year, five-city POI check-in dataset containing more than 30,000 users and 684,200 trajectories. Experimental results show that EviRec consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, with the largest gains concentrated on cold-start queries. In particular, EviRec improves NDCG@10 by 20.4\% on Dual-New cases over the strongest baseline. In-depth analyses further confirm that these gains arise primarily from candidate-specific reliability gating while largely preserving previously learned mobility routines.

</details>


### [173] [Sharp Reconstruction Bounds for Autoencoders Using the Same Forward Map](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20333)
*Patricia Medina,Hy P. G. Lam*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Autoencoder reconstruction error analysis: For odd-dimensional autoencoders with constrained Jacobian singular values, the minimal uniform reconstruction-derivative error is derived, with affine maps achieving optimal bounds. A radial rotation technique can achieve near-perfect reconstruction despite theoretical limitations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper investigates reconstruction performance in autoencoders that use the same forward map before and after zeroing observed coordinates. The motivation is to understand theoretical limits on reconstruction accuracy given constraints on the transformation properties (orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms with bounded Jacobian singular values) and to bridge theoretical predictions with practical applications.

Method: Theoretical analysis of autoencoders with odd input and hidden dimensions ≥3, examining orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms with Jacobian singular values bounded in [m,M]. Derives sharp bound on reconstruction-derivative error and shows affine maps achieve optimality. Introduces translated radial rotation technique for near-perfect reconstruction. Validates predictions on large-scale LiDAR forest scan data (798,452 points).

Result: Proves sharp bound: minimal uniform reconstruction-derivative error is max{1-M(M-m)/2,0}. Shows affine maps attain this bound at any depth. Demonstrates translated radial rotation can reconstruct any ball exactly with singular values arbitrarily close to one. Experimental results: at input scale 0.05, theoretical bound (0.155) matches ~84% of mean training error (0.185). Adding one hidden coordinate reduces reconstruction error below 6×10^-6.

Conclusion: Theoretical bounds on autoencoder reconstruction error are established and validated empirically. While theoretical limitations exist, practical techniques (translated radial rotation) can achieve near-perfect reconstruction despite constraints. The close match between theoretical predictions and experimental results on real-world LiDAR data demonstrates the relevance of the theoretical framework for understanding autoencoder performance.

Abstract: We study reconstruction in autoencoders that apply the same forward map before and after setting the observed coordinates to zero. For equal odd input and hidden dimensions $d\geq 3$, among orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms whose Jacobian singular values lie in $[m,M]$, we show that the least uniform reconstruction-derivative error is $\max\{1-M(M-m)/2,0\}$, with affine maps attaining this sharp bound at every prescribed depth. A translated radial rotation can nevertheless reconstruct any prescribed ball exactly with singular values arbitrarily close to one, motivating additional conditions for a finite-data bound. We test this prediction on a 798,452-point terrestrial LiDAR forest scan. At input scale $0.05$, the mean theoretical bound is $0.155$, about $84\%$ of the mean normalized training error $0.185$ across four spatial regions, two depths, and three seeds. At this scale, adding one hidden coordinate reduces the mean reconstruction error below $6\times10^{-6}$.

</details>


### [174] [COMPASS: Ordered Clustered Routing at 100K Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20352)
*Ido Greenberg,Hugo Linsenmaier,Piotr Sielski,Shie Mannor,Alex Fender,Gal Chechik,Eli Meirom*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: COMPASS algorithm solves Ordered Clustered TSP by combining search with learning-accelerated routing using parallel sub-solvers, scales to 100K+ nodes with no quality ceiling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Optimizing clusters independently in Ordered Clustered TSP misses non-local dependencies, and existing methods have limitations in handling large-scale routing with asymmetric distances.

Method: COMPASS orchestrates parallel sub-solvers through search with learning-accelerated routing, exploiting clustered structure to achieve exact solutions in time exponential in cluster size rather than instance size.

Result: Consistently outperforms alternative methods, scales to 100K synthetic nodes and 28.5K real e-commerce nodes (largest reported routing solution over asymmetric distances, 9x beyond ATSP benchmarks), works with general distance matrices.

Conclusion: COMPASS enables scalable solution of large-scale Ordered Clustered TSP with no quality ceiling, making significant advances in routing over asymmetric distances for practical applications.

Abstract: Large-scale routing often requires visiting clusters of nodes in a prescribed order, giving rise to the Ordered Clustered Traveling Salesman Problem (OCTSP). Optimizing each cluster independently seems natural, but misses non-local dependencies. We introduce the COMPASS algorithm for OCTSP, which combines search with learning-accelerated routing by orchestrating parallel sub-solvers. COMPASS has no quality ceiling and its solutions keep improving with compute. It exploits the clustered structure, and can reach exact solutions in time exponential in cluster size rather than instance size. Empirically, COMPASS consistently outperforms alternative methods. Unlike common large-scale routing solvers, COMPASS consumes general distance matrices and is not limited to coordinate inputs. We demonstrate scaling to 100K synthetic nodes and to 28.5K real e-commerce nodes. To our knowledge, the latter is the largest reported routing solution over asymmetric distances, 9x beyond established ATSP benchmarks.

</details>


### [175] [Minimax-Optimal Online Contract Design with Unrestricted Bounded Contracts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20353)
*Rui Ai,David Simchi-Levi,Han Zhong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper studies repeated contract design with bounded outcome payments, showing that minimax regret scales as T^(m/(m+1)) for m≥2 outcomes, up to logarithmic factors, and establishes both upper and lower bounds for this learning problem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In repeated contract design, principals observe outcomes but not the agent's actions that generate them, creating a learning problem where the agent's best response can make expected profit discontinuous in payments, making optimization challenging.

Method: The approach involves: 1) Effective dimension reduction that allows benchmark normalization even with non-shift-invariant tie-breaking, 2) Using revealed preference to obtain monotone response maps in payment-difference coordinates, 3) Building a learning policy based on Lipschitz parametrization of this map, 4) Developing both upper bound (via constructive algorithm) and lower bound (via construction accounting for incentive loss accumulation) analyses.

Result: For m≥2 outcomes, the minimax regret over T rounds is Θ(T^(m/(m+1))) up to logarithmic factors. Each additional contractible outcome creates a precise and unavoidable increase in worst-case learning cost.

Conclusion: The work characterizes fundamental limits of learning in repeated contract design without smoothness assumptions, showing how outcome dimensionality affects learning difficulty, and provides constructive learning policies achieving optimal rates using only observed outcome categories.

Abstract: We study repeated contract design when a principal observes outcomes but not the actions that generate them. The principal may use any bounded outcome-contingent payment vector, and the agent's best response can make expected profit discontinuous in those payments. For every fixed number $m\ge2$ of outcomes, the minimax regret over $T$ rounds is of order $T^{m/(m+1)}$, up to logarithmic factors. The upper bound allows arbitrary action spaces and agent heterogeneity, without smoothness or monotone-surplus assumptions. Its key is an effective-dimension reduction that the benchmark can be normalized even when fixed tie-breaking is not shift invariant, after which revealed preference yields a monotone response map in payment-difference coordinates. A learning policy built on a Lipschitz parametrization of this map attains the rate using only observed outcome categories. The lower-bound construction accounts for how incentive losses accumulate across outcome dimensions. It shows that each additional contractible outcome creates a precise and unavoidable increase in the worst-case cost of learning.

</details>


### [176] [Learning Principal-Agent Contracts for Equitable Smallholder Carbon Farming under Moral Hazard and Adverse Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20404)
*Rishi Bharadwaj,Yadati Narahari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Reinforcement learning reveals that carbon farming contracts for smallholders, designed as POMDPs, unintentionally exclude small farms due to MRV cost gradients, with adoption rates of 87.7% on large farms vs only 8.2% on smallholdings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Smallholder farmers dominate agriculture in South Asia and sub-Saharan Africa and are crucial for scaling carbon farming for climate mitigation, but current carbon programs largely fail to reach them despite their potential as a major untapped carbon sink.

Method: Formulated the evolving contracting problem as a Partially Observable Markov Decision Process (POMDP) to model heterogeneous smallholder farmers with private adoption costs (adverse selection) and unobserved effort (moral hazard), then used reinforcement learning to learn dynamic profit-maximizing contracts and analyzed aggregator performance under various conditions.

Result: Profit-maximizing aggregators amplify exclusion of smallholders rather than merely inheriting it: 87.7% adoption on large farms vs only 8.2% on smallholdings. MRV costs fall as farm size rises, and pooling contracts compound rather than offset this gradient. Making MRV costs purely area-proportional eliminates the disparity.

Conclusion: Current contract designs unintentionally exclude smallholders from carbon farming programs due to MRV cost structures that favor larger farms. Policy and contract redesign is needed to make MRV costs area-proportional, which could enable smallholders to participate while scaling agricultural soil carbon sequestration for climate mitigation.

Abstract: Agricultural soils are a major untapped carbon sink. Carbon farming is emerging as a promising practice for tapping this potential. Smallholder farmers, who dominate agriculture across South Asia and sub-Saharan Africa, are key to scaling climate mitigation via carbon farming. It is ironic that real-world carbon programs largely fail to reach them. We study this important gap through the lens of contract design. An aggregator offers a single pooled contract to a heterogeneous population of smallholder farmers who have private adoption costs (adverse selection) and exert unobserved effort (moral hazard), with agronomic outcomes evolving over multiple seasons. We formulate this evolving contracting problem as a POMDP and use reinforcement learning to learn a dynamic profit-maximising contract. We analyse the performance of the aggregator under various conditions. We find that a profit-maximising aggregator does not merely inherit the exclusion of smallholders, it amplifies it. On large farms the aggregator realises 87.7% of achievable adoption, against only 8.2% on smallholdings. Per-hectare Measurement, Reporting and Verification (MRV) costs fall as farm size rises, and the aggregator's pooling contract compounds this gradient rather than offsetting it. A counterfactual that makes MRV costs purely area-proportional eliminates this disparity. Our results and simulation can guide contract and policy design that opens carbon income to smallholders while enabling agricultural soils to contribute to climate mitigation at scale.

</details>


### [177] [The Bias of Nonlinear Two-Time-scale Stochastic Approximation under Constant Step-Sizes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20409)
*Djamel Rassem Lamouri,Dorian Baudry,Nicolas Gast*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Nonlinear two-timescale stochastic approximation with constant step-sizes achieves tight mean-squared error bounds of O(α + β²/α²), revealing qualitative differences from linear TTSA with additional finite-time effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Finite-time guarantees for nonlinear two-timescale stochastic approximation schemes are difficult to obtain, especially under constant step-sizes, despite TTSA being fundamental for analyzing coupled iterative algorithms in reinforcement learning, optimization, and stochastic control.

Method: Study nonlinear TTSA with step-sizes α ≫ β under standard stability, regularity, and Markovian noise assumptions. Analyze mean-squared error and bias of iterates around their limiting equilibria, separating contributions of initial conditions, fast-timescale tracking error, Markovian dependence, and timescale coupling.

Result: Proved tight mean-squared error bounds scaling as O(α + β²/α²) when β ≤ α^(3/2). Revealed qualitative differences from linear TTSA setting, showing that nonlinear dynamics introduce additional finite-time effects absent in the linear case.

Conclusion: The analysis provides fundamental finite-time guarantees for nonlinear two-timescale stochastic approximation, establishes tight error bounds, and clarifies the mathematical structure of nonlinear TTSA dynamics including the origin of the β²/α² term.

Abstract: Two-timescale stochastic approximation (TTSA) is a fundamental tool for analyzing coupled iterative algorithms in reinforcement learning, optimization, and stochastic control. However, finite-time guarantees for nonlinear two-timescale schemes remain difficult to obtain, especially under constant step-sizes. In this paper, we study nonlinear TTSA with step-sizes $α\ggβ$. Under standard stability, regularity, and Markovian noise assumptions, we upper bound the mean-squared error and the bias of both iterates around their limiting equilibria. Our bounds scale as $O(α+β^2/α^2)$, which we prove to be tight when $β\leα^{3/2}$. The analysis separates the contributions of initial conditions, fast-timescale tracking error, Markovian dependence, and timescale coupling, thereby clarifying the origin of the $β^2/α^2$ term. Our results reveal qualitative differences from the linear TTSA setting previously studied, showing that nonlinear dynamics introduce additional finite-time effects that are absent in the linear case.

</details>


### [178] [SCGFM-ART: Amortized Relational Transport for Structure-Centric Graph Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20419)
*Xiaodong He,Xincheng Wang,Zhao Kang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SCGFM-ART is a structure-centric graph foundation model that uses a relational atlas and amortized transport plans to align heterogeneous graphs into a unified representation space, achieving state-of-the-art transferability with significant computational efficiency gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
